  Otherwise $S = -\frac{1}{2}\Leftrightarrow\sin\frac{(2n + 1)x}{2} - \sin\frac{x}{2} = -\sin\frac{x}{2}
  \Leftrightarrow\sin\frac{4049x}{2} = 0\Leftrightarrow x = \frac{2k\pi}{4049}$.

  With $0 < x < 2\pi$: $k = 1, 2, \ldots, 4048$. So there are $4048$ solutions.

  Their sum $= \frac{2\pi}{4049}(1 + 2 + \cdots + 4048) = \frac{2\pi}{4049}\cdot\frac{4048\cdot4049}{2} =
  4048\pi$.
  %367
\item Let $h(x) = \sin x - \frac{x}{100}$. Since $h(-x) = -h(x)$, the roots are symmetric about $0$, and $x = 0$
  is a root. Any root satisfies $\left|\frac{x}{100}\right| = |\sin x|\le 1$, i.e.\ $|x|\le 100$. We count the
  positive roots.

  For $x > 0$ we need $\sin x > 0$, i.e.\ $x\in(2k\pi, (2k + 1)\pi)$ for some integer $k\ge 0$. Since $31\pi <
  100 < 32\pi$ ($\pi\approx 3.1416$), the relevant intervals are $k = 0, 1, \ldots, 15$; the part $(31\pi, 100]$
  lies in $(31\pi, 32\pi)$ where $\sin x < 0$.

  On each interval $I_k = [2k\pi, (2k + 1)\pi]$, $h''(x) = -\sin x\le 0$, so $h$ is concave there and has at most
  two roots in $I_k$ (a concave function vanishing at three points would be zero between them).

  For $1\le k\le 15$: $h(2k\pi) < 0, h\left(2k\pi + \frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 1 - \frac{(4k + 1)\pi}{200} > 0$ (as
  $(4k + 1)\pi\le 61\pi < 200$) and $h((2k + 1)\pi) < 0$. So $h$ has exactly two roots in $I_k$.

  For $k = 0$: $h(0) = 0$, $h\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) > 0$, $h(\pi) < 0$, so there is a root in
  $\left(\frac{\pi}{2}, \pi\right)$; with $x = 0$ that makes two, the maximum. So exactly one positive root in
  $I_0$.

  Number of positive roots $= 1 + 2\times15 = 31$.

  Hence the total number of real roots $= 31 + 31 + 1 = 63$.
  %368
\item Let $u = \sin2x$. Then $\sin^4x + \cos^4x = 1 - 2\sin^2x\cos^2x = 1 - \frac{u^2}{2}$.

  The equation becomes $1 - \frac{u^2}{2} + u + a = 0\Rightarrow (u - 1)^2 = 3 + 2a$.

  We need a root $u\in[-1, 1]$. If $3 + 2a < 0$ there is none. Otherwise $u = 1\pm\sqrt{3 + 2a}$; the root $1 +
  \sqrt{3 + 2a}$ lies in $[-1, 1]$ only when it equals $1$, i.e.\ $a = -\frac{3}{2}$, when it coincides with the
  other root. So the only admissible root is $u = 1 - \sqrt{3 + 2a}$, which lies in $[-1, 1]$ iff $0\le 3 + 2a\le
  4$, i.e.\ $-\frac{3}{2}\le a\le\frac{1}{2}$.

  Now count $x\in[0, \pi]$ with $\sin2x = u$, i.e.\ $\theta = 2x\in[0, 2\pi]$ with $\sin\theta = u$: one value if
  $u = \pm1$, three values ($0, \pi, 2\pi$) if $u = 0$, and two values if $u\in(-1, 0)\cup(0, 1)$.

  $u = 1\Leftrightarrow a = -\frac{3}{2}$; $u = -1\Leftrightarrow a = \frac{1}{2}$; $u = 0\Leftrightarrow 3 + 2a = 1
  \Leftrightarrow a = -1$.

  Hence the number of solutions in $[0, \pi]$ is: $0$ if $a < -\frac{3}{2}$ or $a > \frac{1}{2}$; $1$ if $a =
  -\frac{3}{2}$ or $a = \frac{1}{2}$; $3$ if $a = -1$; and $2$ if $a\in\left(-\frac{3}{2}, -1\right)\cup\left(-1,
  \frac{1}{2}\right)$.
  %369
\item Let $s = \sin x$. The equation is $as + 1 - 2s^2 = 2a - 7$, i.e.\ $2s^2 - as + 2a - 8 = 0$.

  $2s^2 - as + 2a - 8 = (s - 2)(2s + 4 - a)$, as is checked by expanding.

  Since $s\neq 2$, the equation is equivalent to $\sin x = \frac{a - 4}{2}$.

  This has a solution iff $-1\le\frac{a - 4}{2}\le 1$, i.e.\ $2\le a\le 6$.

  In $[0, 2\pi]$, $\sin x = s$ has: one solution if $s = \pm1$; three solutions ($0, \pi, 2\pi$) if $s = 0$; two
  solutions if $s\in(-1, 0)\cup(0, 1)$.

  Hence the number of solutions is: $0$ for $a < 2$ or $a > 6$; $1$ for $a = 2$ ($x = \frac{3\pi}{2}$) and for $a =
  6$ ($x = \frac{\pi}{2}$); $3$ for $a = 4$; $2$ for $a\in(2, 4)\cup(4, 6)$.
  %370
\item Let $v = \sin^22x\in[0, 1]$. Then $\sin^6x + \cos^6x = 1 - 3\sin^2x\cos^2x = 1 - \frac{3v}{4}$ and $\sin^4x +
  \cos^4x = 1 - \frac{v}{2}$.

  The equation becomes $4 - 3v = a(4 - 2v)$. As $4 - 2v > 0$,
  \startformula
    a = \frac{4 - 3v}{4 - 2v} = \frac{3}{2} - \frac{1}{2 - v}.
  \stopformula

  As $v$ increases from $0$ to $1$, $\frac{1}{2 - v}$ increases from $\frac{1}{2}$ to $1$, so $a$ decreases
  strictly from $1$ to $\frac{1}{2}$. Hence there is a solution iff $\frac{1}{2}\le a\le 1$, and then $v = 2 -
  \frac{2}{3 - 2a}$ is uniquely determined.

  Now count $x\in[0, 2\pi]$, i.e.\ $\theta = 2x\in[0, 4\pi]$, with $\sin^2\theta = v$.

  $v = 0$ ($a = 1$): $\theta = 0, \pi, 2\pi, 3\pi, 4\pi$, i.e.\ $5$ solutions.

  $v = 1$ ($a = \frac{1}{2}$): $\theta = \frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{3\pi}{2}, \frac{5\pi}{2}, \frac{7\pi}{2}$, i.e.\ $4$
  solutions.

  $0 < v < 1$: $\sin\theta = \sqrt{v}$ has $4$ and $\sin\theta = -\sqrt{v}$ has $4$ solutions in $[0, 4\pi]$, i.e.\
  $8$ solutions.

  Hence the number of solutions is $0$ for $a < \frac{1}{2}$ or $a > 1$, $4$ for $a = \frac{1}{2}$, $8$ for
  $\frac{1}{2} < a < 1$ and $5$ for $a = 1$.
  %371
\item Second equation: $\sin2x\cos3x = \frac{1}{2}(\sin5x - \sin x)$, so it becomes $\sin x\cos2x = -\frac{1}{2}\sin
  x$, i.e.\ $\sin x(2\cos2x + 1) = 0$.

  Since $\sin x = 0\Leftrightarrow\cos2x = 1$, the solution set of the second equation is $\{x: \cos2x\in\{1,
  -\frac{1}{2}\}\}$.

  First equation: let $c = \cos2x$. Then $\cos4x = 2c^2 - 1, \cos6x = 4c^3 - 3c$ and the equation becomes $P(c) =
  0$, where
  \startformula
    P(c) = 4c^3 + 2|a|c^2 + (a - 3)c - |a| - 1.
  \stopformula

  So the solution set of the first equation is $\{x: \cos2x\in Z\}$, where $Z$ is the set of roots of $P$ in $[-1,
  1]$. As $\cos2x$ takes every value of $[-1, 1]$, the two equations have the same solutions iff $Z = \left\{1,
  -\frac{1}{2}\right\}$.

  $P(1) = a + |a|$ and $P\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) = -\frac{1}{2} + \frac{|a|}{2} - \frac{a - 3}{2} - |a| - 1 =
  -\frac{a + |a|}{2}$. Both vanish iff $a\le 0$. So $a > 0$ is rejected.

  Let $a\le 0$, so $|a| = -a$ and $P(c) = 4c^3 - 2ac^2 + (a - 3)c + a - 1$. Dividing by $(c - 1)(2c + 1) = 2c^2 - c -
  1$,
  \startformula
    P(c) = (2c^2 - c - 1)(2c + 1 - a),
  \stopformula
  as is checked by expanding. The third root is $c_3 = \frac{a - 1}{2}$.

  We need $c_3\notin[-1, 1]$ or $c_3\in\left\{1, -\frac{1}{2}\right\}$. With $a\le 0$, $c_3\le -\frac{1}{2}$;
  $c_3 < -1\Leftrightarrow a < -1$ and $c_3 = -\frac{1}{2}\Leftrightarrow a = 0$; for $-1\le a < 0$, $c_3\in\left[-1,
  -\frac{1}{2}\right)$ is an extra root, and the first equation has extra solutions.

  Hence the required values are $a < -1$ and $a = 0$. (Check $a = 0$: $\cos6x = 1\Leftrightarrow x = \frac{k\pi}{3}$,
  and the second equation gives $x = k\pi$ or $x = k\pi\pm\frac{\pi}{3}$, the same set.)
  %372
\item Let $s = \sin x$. Since the left side is $\ge 0$, any solution has $s\ge 0$.

  Let $g(t) = \sqrt{a + t}$ $(t\ge -a)$; $g$ is strictly increasing and the equation is $g(g(s)) = s$.

  If $g(s) > s$, then $g(g(s)) > g(s) > s$; if $g(s) < s$, then $g(g(s)) < g(s) < s$. Both contradict $g(g(s)) =
  s$. $\therefore g(s) = s$, i.e.\ $\sqrt{a + s} = s$.

  $\Rightarrow a = s^2 - s$ with $0\le s\le 1$.

  Conversely, if $s\in[0, 1]$ and $a = s^2 - s$, then $a + s = s^2\ge 0$, $\sqrt{a + s} = s$ and $\sqrt{a + \sqrt{a
  + s}} = \sqrt{a + s} = s$; and $s = \sin x$ for some $x$. So a solution exists iff $a$ belongs to the range of
  $s^2 - s$ on $[0, 1]$.

  $s^2 - s = \left(s - \frac{1}{2}\right)^2 - \frac{1}{4}$, whose range on $[0, 1]$ is $\left[-\frac{1}{4},
  0\right]$.

  Hence $-\frac{1}{4}\le a\le 0$.
  %373
\item Let $F(x) = x^2 - 2a\sin(\cos x) + a^2$. Since $F(-x) = F(x)$, if $x_0$ is a root so is $-x_0$. For the
  root to be unique we need $x_0 = -x_0$, i.e.\ $x_0 = 0$.

  $F(0) = 0\Rightarrow a^2 - 2a\sin1 = 0\Rightarrow a = 0$ or $a = 2\sin1$.

  $a = 0$: $F(x) = x^2 = 0\Leftrightarrow x = 0$, a unique root.

  $a = 2\sin1$: $F(x) = 0\Leftrightarrow x^2 = 4\sin1\left[\sin(\cos x) - \sin1\right]$.

  Since $-1\le\cos x\le 1$ and $\sin$ is increasing on $[-1, 1]\subset\left[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$,
  $\sin(\cos x)\le\sin1$. So the right side is $\le 0\le x^2$, and equality forces $x = 0$. Again a unique root.

  Hence $a = 0$ or $a = 2\sin1$.
  %374
\item Let $s = \sin x\in[-1, 1]$. Then $\cos2x + a\sin x = 1 - 2s^2 + as = 1 + \frac{a^2}{8} - 2\left(s -
  \frac{a}{4}\right)^2$.

  If $|a|\le 4$, then $\frac{a}{4}\in[-1, 1]$ and the greatest value is $1 + \frac{a^2}{8}$.

  If $a > 4$, the quadratic increases on $[-1, 1]$; the greatest value is at $s = 1$: $a - 1$. If $a < -4$, it is at
  $s = -1$: $-a - 1$. So for $|a| > 4$ the greatest value is $|a| - 1$.

  Case $|a|\le 4$: $1 + \frac{a^2}{8} = 2\Rightarrow a = \pm2\sqrt{2}$, and indeed $2\sqrt{2}\le 4$.

  Case $|a| > 4$: $|a| - 1 = 2\Rightarrow |a| = 3$, contradicting $|a| > 4$.

  Hence $a = \pm2\sqrt{2}$. (A careless use of the vertex value alone, or of the endpoint value alone, gives the wrong
  answers $a = \pm3$.)
  %375
\item Let $\theta = \frac{p}{q}\pi$ with integers $p, q, q > 0$, and let $x_k = 2\cos2^k\theta$ for $k = 0, 1, 2,
  \ldots$.

  $x_{k + 1} = 2\cos2^{k + 1}\theta = 2\left(2\cos^22^k\theta - 1\right) = x_k^2 - 2$.

  Each $2^k\theta$ is $\frac{j\pi}{q}$ for an integer $j$, and $\cos\frac{j\pi}{q}$ depends only on $j$ modulo $2q$.
  So the sequence $x_k$ takes at most $2q$ different values.

  Suppose $x_0 = 2\cos\theta = \frac{r}{s}$ in lowest terms with $s > 1$. Then $x_1 = \frac{r^2 - 2s^2}{s^2}$. Any
  prime dividing $s$ and $r^2 - 2s^2$ divides $r$, which is impossible; so $x_1$ is in lowest terms with denominator
  $s^2 > s$.

  Repeating, $x_k$ has denominator $s^{2^k}$, so all $x_k$ are different, contradicting the previous paragraph.
  $\therefore 2\cos\theta$ is an integer in $[-2, 2]$, i.e.\ $\cos\theta\in\left\{0, \pm\frac{1}{2},
  \pm1\right\}$.

  (a) For $n\ge 1$, $\frac{2\pi}{n}\in(0, 2\pi]$. $\cos\frac{2\pi}{n}$ rational $\Rightarrow\cos\frac{2\pi}{n}\in
  \left\{1, \frac{1}{2}, 0, -\frac{1}{2}, -1\right\}\Rightarrow\frac{2\pi}{n}\in\left\{2\pi, \frac{\pi}{3},
  \frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{2\pi}{3}, \pi\right\}$ (values $> \pi$ occur only for $n = 1$). Hence $n = 1, 2, 3, 4, 6$.

  (b) $\sin\frac{\pi}{n} = \cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \frac{\pi}{n}\right)$, again a rational multiple of $\pi$. For
  $n\ge 2$, $0 < \sin\frac{\pi}{n}\le 1$, so it must be $\frac{1}{2}$ or $1$, giving $n = 6$ or $n = 2$. For $n = 1$,
  $\sin\pi = 0$. Hence $n = 1, 2, 6$.

  (c) $\tan^2\frac{\pi}{n} = \frac{1 - \cos\frac{2\pi}{n}}{1 + \cos\frac{2\pi}{n}}$ and conversely
  $\cos\frac{2\pi}{n} = \frac{1 - \tan^2\frac{\pi}{n}}{1 + \tan^2\frac{\pi}{n}}$, so one is rational iff the other
  is. By (a), with $n\ge 3$: $n = 3, 4, 6$ ($\tan^2 = 3, 1, \frac{1}{3}$).
  %376
\item For $n = 1$ the product is $\cos\pi\cos2\pi\cos4\pi\cos8\pi\cos16\pi = -1$. So $n\ge 2$ and $\sin\frac{\pi}{n}
  \neq 0$.

  Multiplying the product $P$ by $32\sin\frac{\pi}{n}$ and using $2\sin\theta\cos\theta = \sin2\theta$ five times,
  $32P\sin\frac{\pi}{n} = \sin\frac{32\pi}{n}$.

  $\therefore P = \frac{1}{32}\Leftrightarrow\sin\frac{32\pi}{n} = \sin\frac{\pi}{n}$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{32\pi}{n} = \frac{\pi}{n} + 2k\pi$ or $\frac{32\pi}{n} = \pi - \frac{\pi}{n} + 2k\pi$.

  First case: $31 = 2kn$, impossible since $31$ is odd.

  Second case: $33 = (2k + 1)n$, so $n$ is a divisor of $33$ (and then $\frac{33}{n}$ is automatically odd). With
  $n\ge 2$: $n = 3, 11, 33$.

  All steps are reversible since $\sin\frac{\pi}{n}\neq 0$. Hence $n = 3, 11, 33$.

  (Check for $n = 3$: $\frac{1}{2}\cdot\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right)^4 = \frac{1}{32}$.)
  %377
\item $\sin\frac{\pi}{2n} + \cos\frac{\pi}{2n} = \sqrt{2}\sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2n} + \frac{\pi}{4}\right) > 0$, so we
  may square both sides without introducing extraneous roots:
  \startformula
    1 + \sin\frac{\pi}{n} = \frac{n}{4}.
  \stopformula

  $n = 1$: $1 = \frac{1}{4}$, false.

  $n\ge 2$: $0 < \sin\frac{\pi}{n}\le 1\Rightarrow 1 < \frac{n}{4}\le 2\Rightarrow n\in\{5, 6, 7, 8\}$.

  $n = 5$: need $\sin\frac{\pi}{5} = \frac{1}{4}$, but $\sin\frac{\pi}{5} > \sin\frac{\pi}{6} = \frac{1}{2}$. False.

  $n = 6$: $\sin\frac{\pi}{6} = \frac{1}{2} = \frac{6}{4} - 1$. True.

  $n = 7$: need $\sin\frac{\pi}{7} = \frac{3}{4}$, but $\sin\frac{\pi}{7} < \frac{1}{2}$. False.

  $n = 8$: need $\sin\frac{\pi}{8} = 1$. False.

  Hence $n = 6$.
  %378
\item (a) The domain is $|2x|\le 1$, i.e.\ $|x|\le\frac{1}{2}$. The left side is strictly increasing and odd, so
  there is at most one root and it is positive.

  For $0 < x\le\frac{1}{2}$: $\sin^{-1}2x = \frac{\pi}{3} - \sin^{-1}x$, and both sides lie in $\left[-\frac{\pi}{2},
  \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$ (the right side lies in $\left[\frac{\pi}{6}, \frac{\pi}{3}\right)$), where $\sin$ is one-one.
  So the equation is equivalent to

  $2x = \sin\frac{\pi}{3}\cos\left(\sin^{-1}x\right) - \cos\frac{\pi}{3}x = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\sqrt{1 - x^2} -
  \frac{x}{2}$

  $\Rightarrow 5x = \sqrt{3}\sqrt{1 - x^2}\Rightarrow 25x^2 = 3 - 3x^2$ (squaring is reversible since $x > 0$)

  $\Rightarrow x = \sqrt{\frac{3}{28}} = \frac{\sqrt{21}}{14}$, which is $\le\frac{1}{2}$.

  (b) Domain: $|x|\le\frac{1}{6\sqrt{3}}$. Write the equation as $\sin^{-1}6\sqrt{3}x = -\frac{\pi}{2} -
  \sin^{-1}6x$.

  The left side lies in $\left[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$, so we need $-\frac{\pi}{2} - \sin^{-1}6x\ge
  -\frac{\pi}{2}$, i.e.\ $x\le 0$. Then both sides are in $\left[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$ and taking
  sine is reversible:

  $6\sqrt{3}x = \sin\left(-\frac{\pi}{2} - \sin^{-1}6x\right) = -\cos\left(\sin^{-1}6x\right) = -\sqrt{1 - 36x^2}$

  $\Rightarrow 108x^2 = 1 - 36x^2\Rightarrow x^2 = \frac{1}{144}$, and with $x\le 0$, $x = -\frac{1}{12}$.

  Check: $\sin^{-1}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) + \sin^{-1}\left(-\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right) = -\frac{\pi}{6} -
  \frac{\pi}{3} = -\frac{\pi}{2}$.

  The value $x = \frac{1}{12}$ produced by squaring is extraneous: it gives $\frac{\pi}{6} + \frac{\pi}{3} =
  \frac{\pi}{2}$. Hence $x = -\frac{1}{12}$ only.
  %379
\item Write the equation as $\tan^{-1}(x - 1) + \tan^{-1}(x + 1) = \tan^{-1}3x - \tan^{-1}x$.

  The right side: $\tan^{-1}3x$ and $\tan^{-1}x$ have the same sign, so their difference lies in
  $\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ and its tangent is $\frac{3x - x}{1 + 3x^2} = \frac{2x}{1 + 3x^2}$.

  So a root must have the left side in $\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ as well, and taking tangents,
  \startformula
    \frac{(x - 1) + (x + 1)}{1 - (x^2 - 1)} = \frac{2x}{1 + 3x^2}\Rightarrow\frac{2x}{2 - x^2} = \frac{2x}{1 + 3x^2}.
  \stopformula

  $\Rightarrow x = 0$ or $2 - x^2 = 1 + 3x^2\Rightarrow x = 0, \pm\frac{1}{2}$.

  Since taking tangents can introduce extraneous roots, we check each directly.

  $x = 0$: $-\frac{\pi}{4} + 0 + \frac{\pi}{4} = 0 = \tan^{-1}0$. True.

  $x = \frac{1}{2}$: $\tan^{-1}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) + \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{2} + \tan^{-1}\frac{3}{2} =
  \tan^{-1}\frac{3}{2}$. True.

  $x = -\frac{1}{2}$: similarly (the equation is odd in $x$). True.

  Hence $x = 0, \pm\frac{1}{2}$.
  %380
\item Let $f(x) = \tan^{-1}x + \tan^{-1}2x + \tan^{-1}3x$. $f$ is continuous, odd and strictly increasing, with
  $f(x)\rightarrow\frac{3\pi}{2}$ as $x\rightarrow\infty$. So $f(x) = \pi$ has exactly one root, and it is positive.

  If we take tangents: $\tan(\tan^{-1}x + \tan^{-1}2x) = \frac{3x}{1 - 2x^2}$ and $\tan f(x) = \frac{\frac{3x}{1 -
  2x^2} + 3x}{1 - \frac{9x^2}{1 - 2x^2}} = \frac{6x - 6x^3}{1 - 11x^2}$.

  $\tan f(x) = 0\Rightarrow x = 0, \pm1$. But $f(0) = 0$ and $f(-1) = -f(1)$, so at most one of these can give
  $\pi$.

  $x = 1$: $\tan^{-1}2 + \tan^{-1}3 = \pi + \tan^{-1}\frac{5}{1 - 6} = \frac{3\pi}{4}$ (both positive with product
  $> 1$), so $f(1) = \frac{\pi}{4} + \frac{3\pi}{4} = \pi$.

  Hence $x = 1$ is the only root; $x = 0$ and $x = -1$ are extraneous (they give $0$ and $-\pi$).
  %381
\item The domain is $x\neq 0, -\frac{1}{2}, -\frac{1}{4}$. The right side lies in $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$.

  $\tan$ of the left side: $\frac{\frac{1}{2x + 1} + \frac{1}{4x + 1}}{1 - \frac{1}{(2x + 1)(4x + 1)}} = \frac{6x
  + 2}{(2x + 1)(4x + 1) - 1} = \frac{2(3x + 1)}{2x(4x + 3)} = \frac{3x + 1}{x(4x + 3)}$, which is defined unless $x
  = -\frac{3}{4}$.

  At $x = -\frac{3}{4}$ the left side is $\tan^{-1}(-2) + \tan^{-1}\left(-\frac{1}{2}\right) = -\frac{\pi}{2}$, not a
  root. Elsewhere, any root must satisfy
  \startformula
    \frac{3x + 1}{x(4x + 3)} = \frac{2}{x^2}\Rightarrow 3x^2 + x = 8x + 6\Rightarrow 3x^2 - 7x - 6 = 0\Rightarrow (x - 3)(3x + 2) = 0.
  \stopformula

  $x = -\frac{2}{3}$: $2x + 1 = -\frac{1}{3}, 4x + 1 = -\frac{5}{3}$, so the left side is negative while the right
  side is positive. Extraneous.

  $x = 3$: left side $= \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{7} + \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{13} = \tan^{-1}\frac{\frac{20}{91}}{1 -
  \frac{1}{91}} = \tan^{-1}\frac{2}{9}$ (product $< 1$) $=$ right side. True.

  Hence $x = 3$ is the only solution.
  %382
\item The domain is $0\le x\le 1$. Using $\cos^{-1}x = \frac{\pi}{2} - \sin^{-1}x$, the equation becomes
  $\sin^{-1}(1 - x) = \frac{\pi}{2} - 2\sin^{-1}x$.

  Let $\alpha = \sin^{-1}x\in\left[0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$. Then $\frac{\pi}{2} - 2\alpha\in\left[-\frac{\pi}{2},
  \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$, so both sides lie in $\left[-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$ and taking sine is
  reversible:

  $1 - x = \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - 2\alpha\right) = \cos2\alpha = 1 - 2\sin^2\alpha = 1 - 2x^2$

  $\Rightarrow 2x^2 - x = 0\Rightarrow x = 0$ or $x = \frac{1}{2}$.

  Check: $x = 0$: $0 + \frac{\pi}{2} = \frac{\pi}{2}$; $x = \frac{1}{2}$: $\frac{\pi}{6} + \frac{\pi}{6} =
  \frac{\pi}{3}$. Both true. Hence $x = 0, \frac{1}{2}$.
  %383
\item The domain is $-1\le x\le 1$. Let $x = \cos\theta, 0\le\theta\le\pi$, so $\cos^{-1}x = \theta$ and $\sqrt{1 -
  x^2} = \sin\theta$.

  $\frac{x}{2} + \frac{\sqrt{3}\sqrt{1 - x^2}}{2} = \cos\theta\cos\frac{\pi}{3} + \sin\theta\sin\frac{\pi}{3} =
  \cos\left(\theta - \frac{\pi}{3}\right)$.

  $\theta - \frac{\pi}{3}\in\left[-\frac{\pi}{3}, \frac{2\pi}{3}\right]$, so $\cos^{-1}\cos\left(\theta -
  \frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \left|\theta - \frac{\pi}{3}\right|$.

  The equation becomes $\theta + \left|\theta - \frac{\pi}{3}\right| = \frac{\pi}{3}$.

  If $\theta\le\frac{\pi}{3}$: $\theta + \frac{\pi}{3} - \theta = \frac{\pi}{3}$, always true.

  If $\theta > \frac{\pi}{3}$: $2\theta - \frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{\pi}{3}\Rightarrow\theta = \frac{\pi}{3}$, a
  contradiction.

  $\therefore 0\le\theta\le\frac{\pi}{3}\Leftrightarrow\frac{1}{2}\le x\le 1$. The solution set is the whole
  interval $\left[\frac{1}{2}, 1\right]$.
  %384
\item (a) Write $x = 2k\pi + t$ with $k$ an integer and $-\pi < t\le\pi$. Then $\cos^{-1}(\cos x) = \cos^{-1}(\cos
  t) = |t|$.

  $\sin^{-1}(\sin x) = \sin^{-1}(\sin t)$, which equals $t$ if $|t|\le\frac{\pi}{2}$, $\pi - t$ if $\frac{\pi}{2} <
  t\le\pi$ and $-\pi - t$ if $-\pi < t < -\frac{\pi}{2}$.

  $|t|\le\frac{\pi}{2}$: $|t| = t\Leftrightarrow t\ge 0$, so $0\le t\le\frac{\pi}{2}$.

  $\frac{\pi}{2} < t\le\pi$: $t = \pi - t\Rightarrow t = \frac{\pi}{2}$, not in this range.

  $-\pi < t < -\frac{\pi}{2}$: $-t = -\pi - t$, impossible.

  Hence the solution set is $\bigcup_k\left[2k\pi, 2k\pi + \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$.

  (b) $\tan^{-1}(\tan x)$ is defined for $x\neq k\pi + \frac{\pi}{2}$. Write $x = k\pi + t, -\frac{\pi}{2} < t <
  \frac{\pi}{2}$. Then $\tan^{-1}(\tan x) = t$ and $\sin x = (-1)^k\sin t$, so $\sin^{-1}(\sin x) = (-1)^kt$.

  The equation is $t = (-1)^kt$: always true for even $k$; for odd $k$ only when $t = 0$.

  Hence the solution set is $\bigcup_k\left(2k\pi - \frac{\pi}{2}, 2k\pi + \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ together with
  the points $x = (2k + 1)\pi$.
  %385
\item (a) $\frac{2}{n^2} = \frac{(n + 1) - (n - 1)}{1 + (n + 1)(n - 1)}$, and $(n + 1)(n - 1)\ge 0$, so
  $\tan^{-1}\frac{2}{n^2} = \tan^{-1}(n + 1) - \tan^{-1}(n - 1)$.

  $\displaystyle\sum_{n = 1}^N\tan^{-1}\frac{2}{n^2} = \sum_{n = 1}^N\left[\tan^{-1}(n + 1) - \tan^{-1}(n -
  1)\right] = \tan^{-1}(N + 1) + \tan^{-1}N - \tan^{-1}1 - \tan^{-1}0$

  As $N\rightarrow\infty$ this tends to $\frac{\pi}{2} + \frac{\pi}{2} - \frac{\pi}{4} = \frac{3\pi}{4}$.

  (b) Let $f(m) = \tan^{-1}\frac{m^2}{2}$. With $u = \frac{(n + 1)^2}{2}, v = \frac{(n - 1)^2}{2}$: $u - v = 2n$ and
  $1 + uv = 1 + \frac{(n^2 - 1)^2}{4} = \frac{n^4 - 2n^2 + 5}{4}$.

  Since $uv\ge 0$, $f(n + 1) - f(n - 1) = \tan^{-1}\frac{u - v}{1 + uv} = \tan^{-1}\frac{8n}{n^4 - 2n^2 + 5}$.

  $\displaystyle\sum_{n = 1}^N\tan^{-1}\frac{8n}{n^4 - 2n^2 + 5} = f(N + 1) + f(N) - f(1) - f(0) = f(N + 1) + f(N) -
  \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{2}$

  $\rightarrow\pi - \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{2} = \pi - \left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \tan^{-1}2\right) = \frac{\pi}{2} +
  \tan^{-1}2$.
  %386
\item (a) $\frac{1}{2n^2} = \frac{2}{4n^2} = \frac{(2n + 1) - (2n - 1)}{1 + (2n + 1)(2n - 1)}$, so
  $\tan^{-1}\frac{1}{2n^2} = \tan^{-1}(2n + 1) - \tan^{-1}(2n - 1)$.

  $\displaystyle\sum_{n = 1}^N\tan^{-1}\frac{1}{2n^2} = \tan^{-1}(2N + 1) - \tan^{-1}1\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2} -
  \frac{\pi}{4} = \frac{\pi}{4}$.

  (b) $\frac{2}{n^2 + n + 4} = \frac{\frac{1}{2}}{1 + \frac{n(n + 1)}{4}} = \frac{\frac{n + 1}{2} - \frac{n}{2}}{1 +
  \frac{n + 1}{2}\cdot\frac{n}{2}}$, so $\tan^{-1}\frac{2}{n^2 + n + 4} = \tan^{-1}\frac{n + 1}{2} -
  \tan^{-1}\frac{n}{2}$.

  $\displaystyle\sum_{n = 1}^N\tan^{-1}\frac{2}{n^2 + n + 4} = \tan^{-1}\frac{N + 1}{2} -
  \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{2}\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2} - \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{2} = \tan^{-1}2$.
  %387
\item First we prove Cassini's identity $F_{m - 1}F_{m + 1} - F_m^2 = (-1)^m$ for $m\ge 1$ by induction.

  $m = 1$: $F_0F_2 - F_1^2 = 0 - 1 = -1$. True.

  If it holds for $m$, then $F_mF_{m + 2} - F_{m + 1}^2 = F_m(F_{m + 1} + F_m) - F_{m + 1}(F_m + F_{m - 1}) = F_m^2 -
  F_{m + 1}F_{m - 1} = -(-1)^m = (-1)^{m + 1}$.

  Taking $m = 2n + 1$: $F_{2n}F_{2n + 2} - F_{2n + 1}^2 = -1$, i.e.\ $F_{2n}F_{2n + 2} + 1 = F_{2n + 1}^2$.

  For $n\ge 1$ all $F$'s involved are positive, so

  $\tan^{-1}\frac{1}{F_{2n}} - \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{F_{2n + 2}} = \tan^{-1}\frac{\frac{1}{F_{2n}} - \frac{1}{F_{2n +
  2}}}{1 + \frac{1}{F_{2n}F_{2n + 2}}} = \tan^{-1}\frac{F_{2n + 2} - F_{2n}}{F_{2n}F_{2n + 2} + 1}$

  $= \tan^{-1}\frac{F_{2n + 1}}{F_{2n + 1}^2} = \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{F_{2n + 1}}$, which is the required identity.

  Adding for $n = 1, 2, \ldots, N$: $\displaystyle\sum_{n = 1}^N\tan^{-1}\frac{1}{F_{2n + 1}} =
  \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{F_2} - \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{F_{2N + 2}}$.

  As $F_2 = 1$ and $F_{2N + 2}\rightarrow\infty$, the sum tends to $\tan^{-1}1 = \frac{\pi}{4}$.
  %388
\item Let $\alpha_n = \sin^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}$ for $n\ge 1$; then $\alpha_1 = \frac{\pi}{2}$, $0 < \alpha_{n +
  1} < \alpha_n\le\frac{\pi}{2}$, $\cos\alpha_n = \sqrt{\frac{n - 1}{n}}$.

  $\sin(\alpha_n - \alpha_{n + 1}) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sqrt{\frac{n}{n + 1}} - \sqrt{\frac{n -
  1}{n}}\frac{1}{\sqrt{n + 1}} = \frac{\sqrt{n} - \sqrt{n - 1}}{\sqrt{n(n + 1)}}$

  As $0 < \alpha_n - \alpha_{n + 1} < \frac{\pi}{2}$, $\sin^{-1}\frac{\sqrt{n} - \sqrt{n - 1}}{\sqrt{n(n + 1)}} =
  \alpha_n - \alpha_{n + 1}$.

  $\displaystyle\sum_{n = 1}^N\sin^{-1}\frac{\sqrt{n} - \sqrt{n - 1}}{\sqrt{n(n + 1)}} = \alpha_1 - \alpha_{N + 1}
  = \frac{\pi}{2} - \sin^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{N + 1}}$

  $\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2}$ as $N\rightarrow\infty$.
  %389
\item Putting $x = y = 0$: $2f(0) = 2f(0)^2\Rightarrow f(0) = 0$ or $1$.

  If $f(0) = 0$, putting $y = 0$ gives $2f(x) = 2f(x)f(0) = 0$, so $f\equiv 0$.

  Let $f(0) = 1$. Putting $x = 0$: $f(y) + f(-y) = 2f(y)\Rightarrow f(-y) = f(y)$, so $f$ is even.

  Putting $y = x$: $f(2x) + 1 = 2f(x)^2$. $\quad (1)$

  Putting $x = mt, y = t$: $f((m + 1)t) = 2f(t)f(mt) - f((m - 1)t)$. $\quad (2)$

  As $f$ is continuous and $f(0) = 1$, there is $c > 0$ with $f(x) > 0$ for $0\le x\le c$.

  Case 1: $f(c)\le 1$. Then $0 < f(c)\le 1$, so $f(c) = \cos\theta$ for a unique $\theta\in\left[0,
  \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$.

  Claim A: $f\left(\frac{c}{2^n}\right) = \cos\frac{\theta}{2^n}$ for $n = 0, 1, 2, \ldots$. It holds for $n = 0$. If
  it holds for $n$, then by $(1)$, $f\left(\frac{c}{2^{n + 1}}\right)^2 = \frac{1 + \cos\frac{\theta}{2^n}}{2} =
  \cos^2\frac{\theta}{2^{n + 1}}$, and both $f\left(\frac{c}{2^{n + 1}}\right)$ and $\cos\frac{\theta}{2^{n + 1}}$ are
  positive, so they are equal.

  Claim B: with $t = \frac{c}{2^n}, \varphi = \frac{\theta}{2^n}$, $f(mt) = \cos m\varphi$ for all integers $m\ge 0$.
  It holds for $m = 0, 1$; if it holds for $m - 1$ and $m$, then by $(2)$, $f((m + 1)t) = 2\cos\varphi\cos m\varphi -
  \cos(m - 1)\varphi = \cos(m + 1)\varphi$.

  So $f(x) = \cos\frac{\theta x}{c}$ for every $x = \frac{mc}{2^n}$ $(m, n\ge 0)$. Such numbers are dense in $[0,
  \infty)$, and both sides are continuous, so $f(x) = \cos kx$ $\left(k = \frac{\theta}{c}\right)$ for all $x\ge 0$,
  and, $f$ being even, for all real $x$.

  Case 2: $f(c) > 1$. Let $C(\varphi) = \frac{e^\varphi + e^{-\varphi}}{2}$. $C$ maps $(0, \infty)$ onto $(1,
  \infty)$, $C > 0$, and by expanding, $C(\varphi)^2 = \frac{1 + C(2\varphi)}{2}$ and $C((m + 1)\varphi) + C((m -
  1)\varphi) = 2C(\varphi)C(m\varphi)$. Writing $f(c) = C(\theta), \theta > 0$, exactly the same two claims give
  $f(x) = C\left(\frac{\theta x}{c}\right)$ for all $x$.

  Conversely, $\cos(x + y) + \cos(x - y) = 2\cos x\cos y$ and the analogous identity for $C$ show that these
  functions satisfy the equation.

  Hence $f(x) = 0$, $f(x) = \cos kx$ or $f(x) = \frac{e^{kx} + e^{-kx}}{2}$, where $k\ge 0$ is a constant.
  %390
\item (a) Let $a = f(0), b = f\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ and let $t$ be any real number.

  $x = 0, y = t$: $f(t) + f(-t) = 2a\cos t$. $\quad (1)$

  $x = \frac{\pi}{2} + t, y = \frac{\pi}{2}$: $f(\pi + t) + f(t) = 2f\left(\frac{\pi}{2} + t\right)\cos\frac{\pi}{2}
  = 0$. $\quad (2)$

  $x = \frac{\pi}{2}, y = \frac{\pi}{2} + t$: $f(\pi + t) + f(-t) = 2b\cos\left(\frac{\pi}{2} + t\right) = -2b\sin
  t$. $\quad (3)$

  $(1) + (2) - (3)$: $2f(t) = 2a\cos t + 2b\sin t$.

  So $f(x) = a\cos x + b\sin x$. Conversely, $a[\cos(x + y) + \cos(x - y)] + b[\sin(x + y) + \sin(x - y)] = 2(a\cos x
  + b\sin x)\cos y$, so every such function works ($a, b$ arbitrary constants).

  (b) $x = 0$: $f(y) - f(-y) = 2f(y)\Rightarrow f(-y) = -f(y)$, so $f$ is odd and $f(0) = 0$.

  Interchanging $x$ and $y$ in the given equation: $f(x + y) - f(y - x) = 2f(x)\cos y$, and as $f$ is odd, $f(x + y) +
  f(x - y) = 2f(x)\cos y$.

  By (a), $f(x) = a\cos x + b\sin x$, and $f(0) = 0$ gives $a = 0$. So $f(x) = b\sin x$.

  Conversely, $b[\sin(x + y) - \sin(x - y)] = 2b\cos x\sin y$. Hence $f(x) = b\sin x$, $b$ an arbitrary constant.
  %391
\item Define $g(\theta) = f(\tan\theta)$ for $-\frac{\pi}{2} < \theta < \frac{\pi}{2}$; $g$ is continuous.

  If $\alpha, \beta, \alpha + \beta\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$, then $1 - \tan\alpha\tan\beta =
  \frac{\cos(\alpha + \beta)}{\cos\alpha\cos\beta} > 0$, so with $x = \tan\alpha, y = \tan\beta$ we have $xy < 1$ and
  $\frac{x + y}{1 - xy} = \tan(\alpha + \beta)$.

  $\therefore g(\alpha) + g(\beta) = g(\alpha + \beta)$ whenever $\alpha, \beta, \alpha + \beta\in
  \left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$. $\quad (1)$

  $\alpha = \beta = 0$: $g(0) = 0$. $\beta = -\alpha$: $g(-\alpha) = -g(\alpha)$.

  Let $c = \frac{4}{\pi}g\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right)$ and $h(\theta) = g(\theta) - c\theta$. Then $h$ is continuous,
  satisfies $(1)$, is odd and $h\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 0$.

  From $(1)$ with $\alpha = \beta$: $h(2\alpha) = 2h(\alpha)$ for $|\alpha| < \frac{\pi}{4}$. So
  $h\left(\frac{\pi}{2^{n + 2}}\right) = \frac{1}{2^n}h\left(\frac{\pi}{4}\right) = 0$ for all $n\ge 0$.

  With $t = \frac{\pi}{2^{n + 2}}$, $(1)$ gives $h((m + 1)t) = h(mt) + h(t) = h(mt)$ as long as $(m + 1)t <
  \frac{\pi}{2}$. Hence $h(mt) = 0$ for $0\le mt < \frac{\pi}{2}$, and by oddness for $|mt| < \frac{\pi}{2}$.

  The numbers $\frac{m\pi}{2^{n + 2}}$ are dense in $\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ and $h$ is
  continuous, so $h\equiv 0$, i.e.\ $g(\theta) = c\theta$.

  $\therefore f(x) = g\left(\tan^{-1}x\right) = c\tan^{-1}x$.

  Conversely, for $xy < 1$, $\tan^{-1}x + \tan^{-1}y\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ (as shown above
  with the roles reversed) and its tangent is $\frac{x + y}{1 - xy}$, so $\tan^{-1}x + \tan^{-1}y = \tan^{-1}\frac{x
  + y}{1 - xy}$.

  Hence $f(x) = c\tan^{-1}x$, $c$ an arbitrary constant.
  %392
\item (a) Replacing $x$ by $x + 1$ and by $x - 1$:

  $f(x + 2) + f(x) = \sqrt{2}f(x + 1)$ and $f(x) + f(x - 2) = \sqrt{2}f(x - 1)$.

  Adding: $f(x + 2) + 2f(x) + f(x - 2) = \sqrt{2}[f(x + 1) + f(x - 1)] = \sqrt{2}\cdot\sqrt{2}f(x) = 2f(x)$

  $\Rightarrow f(x + 2) = -f(x - 2)$ for all $x$, i.e.\ $f(t + 4) = -f(t)$ for all $t$.

  $\Rightarrow f(t + 8) = -f(t + 4) = f(t)$. So $f$ has period $8$.

  (b) From $f(x + 2) = \sqrt{3}f(x + 1) - f(x)$ repeatedly:

  $f(x + 3) = \sqrt{3}f(x + 2) - f(x + 1) = 2f(x + 1) - \sqrt{3}f(x)$

  $f(x + 4) = \sqrt{3}f(x + 3) - f(x + 2) = \sqrt{3}f(x + 1) - 2f(x)$

  $f(x + 5) = \sqrt{3}f(x + 4) - f(x + 3) = f(x + 1) - \sqrt{3}f(x)$

  $f(x + 6) = \sqrt{3}f(x + 5) - f(x + 4) = -f(x)$

  $\Rightarrow f(x + 12) = -f(x + 6) = f(x)$. So $f$ has period $12$.

  Examples: $\cos\frac{\pi(x + 1)}{4} + \cos\frac{\pi(x - 1)}{4} = 2\cos\frac{\pi x}{4}\cos\frac{\pi}{4} =
  \sqrt{2}\cos\frac{\pi x}{4}$, and $\cos\frac{\pi x}{4}$ has least positive period $8$. Similarly $\cos\frac{\pi
  x}{6}$ satisfies the equation in (b), since $2\cos\frac{\pi}{6} = \sqrt{3}$, and its least positive period is $12$.
  So $8$ and $12$ cannot be improved.
  %393
\item $y = 0$: $f(x)(f(0) - 1) = 0$ for all $x$. If $f(0)\neq 1$, then $f\equiv 0$, but then $x = y = \frac{\pi}{2}$
  gives $0 = 1$. $\therefore f(0) = 1$.

  Let $b = f\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right)$.

  $x = y = \frac{\pi}{2}$: $b^2 - f(\pi) = 1\Rightarrow f(\pi) = b^2 - 1$.

  $y = \pi$: $f(x)f(\pi) - f(x + \pi) = 0\Rightarrow f(x + \pi) = (b^2 - 1)f(x)$. $\quad (1)$

  $y = \frac{\pi}{2}$: $bf(x) - f\left(x + \frac{\pi}{2}\right) = \sin x\Rightarrow f\left(x + \frac{\pi}{2}\right) =
  bf(x) - \sin x$. $\quad (2)$

  Applying $(2)$ twice: $f(x + \pi) = bf\left(x + \frac{\pi}{2}\right) - \sin\left(x + \frac{\pi}{2}\right) =
  b^2f(x) - b\sin x - \cos x$.

  Comparing with $(1)$: $(b^2 - 1)f(x) = b^2f(x) - b\sin x - \cos x\Rightarrow f(x) = b\sin x + \cos x$.

  Substituting back: $(b\sin x + \cos x)(b\sin y + \cos y) - b\sin(x + y) - \cos(x + y) = b^2\sin x\sin y + \sin x\sin
  y$ (the terms $b\sin x\cos y + b\cos x\sin y$ cancel with $b\sin(x + y)$, and $\cos x\cos y - \cos(x + y) = \sin
  x\sin y$).

  This must equal $\sin x\sin y$ for all $x, y$, so $b^2 = 0$.

  Hence $f(x) = \cos x$, which indeed satisfies $\cos x\cos y - \cos(x + y) = \sin x\sin y$.
  %394
\item Let $y = a\sec x - b\tan x = \frac{a - b\sin x}{\cos x}$, where $\cos x > 0$.

  Case $a > b$: $a - b\sin x > a - b > 0$, so $y > 0$. Also $y\cos x + b\sin x = a$.

  But $y\cos x + b\sin x\le\sqrt{y^2 + b^2}$. $\therefore a\le\sqrt{y^2 + b^2}\Rightarrow y^2\ge a^2 - b^2\Rightarrow
  y\ge\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}$.

  At $\sin x = \frac{b}{a}, \cos x = \frac{\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}}{a}$: $y = \frac{a - \frac{b^2}{a}}{\frac{\sqrt{a^2 -
  b^2}}{a}} = \sqrt{a^2 - b^2}$.

  So the least value is $\sqrt{a^2 - b^2}$, attained at $x = \sin^{-1}\frac{b}{a}$.

  Case $a = b$: $y = \frac{a(1 - \sin x)}{\cos x} = \frac{a\cos x}{1 + \sin x} > 0$, and $y\rightarrow 0$ as
  $x\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2}^-$. So the infimum is $0$, but it is not attained; there is no least value.

  Case $a < b$: as $x\rightarrow\frac{\pi}{2}^-$, the numerator tends to $a - b < 0$ and $\cos x\rightarrow 0^+$, so
  $y\rightarrow -\infty$. There is no least value.
  %395
\item Let $u = \frac{x + y}{2}, v = \frac{x - y}{2}$ (as $x, y$ range over all reals, so do $u, v$ independently).

  $E = k(\cos x + \cos y) - \cos(x + y) = 2k\cos u\cos v - (2\cos^2u - 1)$.

  Let $t = |\cos u|\in[0, 1]$. Since $-1\le\cos v\le 1$: $1 - 2kt - 2t^2\le E\le 1 + 2kt - 2t^2$, and for fixed $u$
  each bound is attained by a suitable $v$.

  Greatest value: $1 + 2kt - 2t^2 = 1 + \frac{k^2}{2} - 2\left(t - \frac{k}{2}\right)^2$.

  If $0 < k\le 2$, $\frac{k}{2}\in(0, 1]$ and the greatest value is $1 + \frac{k^2}{2}$, attained at $x = y =
  \cos^{-1}\frac{k}{2}$ (check: $2k\cdot\frac{k}{2} - \left(2\cdot\frac{k^2}{4} - 1\right) = 1 + \frac{k^2}{2}$).

  If $k > 2$, the expression increases on $[0, 1]$; the greatest value is at $t = 1$: $2k - 1$, attained at $x = y =
  0$.

  Least value: $1 - 2kt - 2t^2$ decreases in $t$, so it is least at $t = 1$: $-2k - 1$, attained at $x = \pi, y =
  -\pi$ ($\cos x + \cos y = -2, \cos(x + y) = 1$).

  Hence the greatest value is $1 + \frac{k^2}{2}$ for $0 < k\le 2$ and $2k - 1$ for $k\ge 2$; the least value is $-2k
  - 1$. (For $k = 1$: $\cos x + \cos y - \cos(x + y)$ lies in $\left[-3, \frac{3}{2}\right]$.)
  %396
\item Let $s = \sin x, c = \cos x$ (with $sc\neq 0$) and $t = s + c = \sqrt{2}\sin\left(x + \frac{\pi}{4}\right)$,
  so $sc = \frac{t^2 - 1}{2}$ and $t\in\left[-\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{2}\right], t\neq\pm1$.

  $\tan x + \cot x = \frac{s^2 + c^2}{sc} = \frac{1}{sc}, \sec x + {\rm cosec}\, x = \frac{s + c}{sc} = \frac{t}{sc}$.

  The expression $E = t + \frac{1 + t}{sc} = t + \frac{2(1 + t)}{t^2 - 1} = t + \frac{2}{t - 1}$.

  Let $u = t - 1\in\left[-\sqrt{2} - 1, \sqrt{2} - 1\right], u\neq 0, -2$. Then $E = u + \frac{2}{u} + 1$.

  If $u > 0$: $u\le\sqrt{2} - 1 < 1$, so $\frac{2}{u} > 2$ and $E > 3 > 2\sqrt{2} - 1$.

  If $u < 0$: let $w = -u > 0$. $E = 1 - \left(w + \frac{2}{w}\right)\le 1 - 2\sqrt{2}$ by A.M.--G.M., so $|E|\ge
  2\sqrt{2} - 1$, with equality iff $w = \sqrt{2}$.

  $w = \sqrt{2}$ means $t = 1 - \sqrt{2}\in\left[-\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{2}\right], t\neq\pm1$, i.e.\ $\sin\left(x +
  \frac{\pi}{4}\right) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} - 1$, which has solutions, and for them $sc\neq 0$.

  Hence the least value is $2\sqrt{2} - 1$.
  %397
\item For real $p, q$: $-\frac{p^2 + q^2}{2}\le pq\le\frac{p^2 + q^2}{2}$, since $(p\mp q)^2\ge 0$.

  $\therefore\sin x\cos y + \sin y\cos z + \sin z\cos x\le\frac{\sin^2x + \cos^2y}{2} + \frac{\sin^2y + \cos^2z}{2} +
  \frac{\sin^2z + \cos^2x}{2} = \frac{3}{2}$

  and similarly the expression is $\ge -\frac{3}{2}$.

  At $x = y = z = \frac{\pi}{4}$ each term is $\frac{1}{2}$, giving $\frac{3}{2}$; at $x = y = z = -\frac{\pi}{4}$
  each term is $-\frac{1}{2}$, giving $-\frac{3}{2}$.

  Hence the greatest value is $\frac{3}{2}$ and the least value is $-\frac{3}{2}$.
  %398
\item Let $u = \cos x + \sin y$.

  $(\sin x + \cos y)^2 + (\cos x + \sin y)^2 = 2 + 2(\sin x\cos y + \cos x\sin y) = 2 + 2\sin(x + y)$

  $\Rightarrow 1 + u^2 = 2 + 2\sin(x + y)\Rightarrow u^2 = 1 + 2\sin(x + y)\le 3\Rightarrow -\sqrt{3}\le
  u\le\sqrt{3}$.

  Now we show every value in $\left[-\sqrt{3}, \sqrt{3}\right]$ occurs. For $0\le x\le\pi$, $0\le 1 - \sin x\le 1$; let
  $\varphi(x) = \cos^{-1}(1 - \sin x)\in\left[0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$, a continuous function. Both $(x, \varphi(x))$
  and $(x, -\varphi(x))$ satisfy $\sin x + \cos y = 1$.

  Let $g_1(x) = \cos x + \sin\varphi(x)$ and $g_2(x) = \cos x - \sin\varphi(x)$, continuous on $[0, \pi]$.

  $\varphi(0) = 0$, $\varphi\left(\frac{\pi}{6}\right) = \varphi\left(\frac{5\pi}{6}\right) = \cos^{-1}\frac{1}{2} =
  \frac{\pi}{3}$.

  $g_1(0) = 1, g_1\left(\frac{\pi}{6}\right) = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} + \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \sqrt{3}$; $g_2(0) = 1,
  g_2\left(\frac{5\pi}{6}\right) = -\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} - \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = -\sqrt{3}$.

  By the intermediate value property, $g_1$ takes every value in $\left[1, \sqrt{3}\right]$ and $g_2$ every value in
  $\left[-\sqrt{3}, 1\right]$.

  Hence the set of values of $\cos x + \sin y$ is $\left[-\sqrt{3}, \sqrt{3}\right]$.
  %399
\item Let $p = \frac{1}{\sin^nx}, q = \frac{1}{\cos^nx}$, both positive.

  $(1 + p)(1 + q) = 1 + p + q + pq\ge 1 + 2\sqrt{pq} + pq = \left(1 + \sqrt{pq}\right)^2$, with equality iff $p = q$.

  $pq = \frac{1}{(\sin x\cos x)^n} = \left(\frac{2}{\sin2x}\right)^n\ge 2^n$, with equality iff $\sin2x = 1$.

  $\therefore\left(1 + \frac{1}{\sin^nx}\right)\left(1 + \frac{1}{\cos^nx}\right)\ge\left(1 + 2^{\frac{n}{2}}\right)^2$.

  Both equalities hold at $x = \frac{\pi}{4}$. Hence the least value is $\left(1 + 2^{\frac{n}{2}}\right)^2$ (e.g.\ $3
  + 2\sqrt{2}$ for $n = 1$, $9$ for $n = 2$).
  %400
\item Let $t = \cos x\in[-1, 1]$, $p(t) = t^2 + at + b$ and $M = \max_{|t|\le 1}|p(t)|$.

  $p(1) - 2p(0) + p(-1) = (1 + a + b) - 2b + (1 - a + b) = 2$.

  $\therefore 2\le|p(1)| + 2|p(0)| + |p(-1)|\le M + 2M + M = 4M\Rightarrow M\ge\frac{1}{2}$.

  For $a = 0, b = -\frac{1}{2}$: $\left|\cos^2x - \frac{1}{2}\right| = \frac{|\cos2x|}{2}\le\frac{1}{2}$, so $M =
  \frac{1}{2}$. Hence the least possible value is $\frac{1}{2}$.

  If $M = \frac{1}{2}$, all inequalities above are equalities: $p(1) = \frac{1}{2}, p(0) = -\frac{1}{2}, p(-1) =
  \frac{1}{2}$. So $b = -\frac{1}{2}$ and $1 + a + b = \frac{1}{2}\Rightarrow a = 0$. It is attained only for $a = 0,
  b = -\frac{1}{2}$.
  %401
\item Let $t = |\cos x|\in[0, 1]$. Then $|\cos2x| = |2t^2 - 1|$ and $g(t) = t + |2t^2 - 1|$.

  For $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\le t\le 1$: $g(t) = 2t^2 + t - 1$, increasing, so $g(t)\ge
  g\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right) = \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$.

  For $0\le t\le\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$: $g(t) = 1 + t - 2t^2$ and
  \startformula
    g(t) - \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} = \left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} - t\right)\left(2t + \sqrt{2} - 1\right)\ge 0,
  \stopformula
  as is checked by expanding.

  So $|\cos x| + |\cos2x|\ge\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$ for all $x$, with equality at $x = \frac{\pi}{4}$.

  Hence the largest $c$ is $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}$.
  %402
\item (a) Let $c = \cos x$ and $y = \sin x(1 + \cos x)$. Then $y^2 = (1 - c^2)(1 + c)^2 = (1 - c)(1 + c)^3 =
  \frac{1}{3}(3 - 3c)(1 + c)(1 + c)(1 + c)$.

  All four factors are $\ge 0$ and their sum is $6$, so by A.M.--G.M., $(3 - 3c)(1 + c)^3\le\left(\frac{6}{4}\right)^4
  = \frac{81}{16}$.

  $\Rightarrow y^2\le\frac{27}{16}\Rightarrow -\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4}\le y\le\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4}$.

  Equality needs $3 - 3c = 1 + c$, i.e.\ $c = \frac{1}{2}$: at $x = \frac{\pi}{3}$, $y = \frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\cdot
  \frac{3}{2} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4}$, and at $x = -\frac{\pi}{3}$, $y = -\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4}$.

  So the greatest value is $\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4}$ and the least is $-\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{4}$.

  (b) Let $t = \sin x + \cos x\in\left[-\sqrt{2}, \sqrt{2}\right]$, $\sin x\cos x = \frac{t^2 - 1}{2}$.

  $(1 + \sin x)(1 + \cos x) = 1 + t + \frac{t^2 - 1}{2} = \frac{(1 + t)^2}{2}$.

  The greatest value is at $t = \sqrt{2}$ ($x = \frac{\pi}{4}$): $\frac{\left(1 + \sqrt{2}\right)^2}{2} = \frac{3 +
  2\sqrt{2}}{2}$. The least value is $0$, at $t = -1$ ($x = \pi$ or $x = \frac{3\pi}{2}$).
  %403
\item Let $t = \tan y\ge 0$, so $\tan x = kt$ and $0\le x - y < \frac{\pi}{2}$.

  $\tan(x - y) = \frac{kt - t}{1 + kt^2} = \frac{(k - 1)t}{1 + kt^2}$.

  By A.M.--G.M., $1 + kt^2\ge 2\sqrt{k}t$, so $\tan(x - y)\le\frac{k - 1}{2\sqrt{k}}$ (also trivially when $t = 0$),
  with equality iff $t = \frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}$.

  Since $\tan$ is increasing on $\left[0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$, the greatest value of $x - y$ is $\varphi =
  \tan^{-1}\frac{k - 1}{2\sqrt{k}}$, attained at $y = \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{\sqrt{k}}, x = \tan^{-1}\sqrt{k}$.

  $\sec^2\varphi = 1 + \frac{(k - 1)^2}{4k} = \frac{(k + 1)^2}{4k}\Rightarrow\cos\varphi = \frac{2\sqrt{k}}{k +
  1}$

  $\Rightarrow\sin\varphi = \tan\varphi\cos\varphi = \frac{k - 1}{2\sqrt{k}}\cdot\frac{2\sqrt{k}}{k + 1} = \frac{k -
  1}{k + 1}$.

  Hence the greatest value of $x - y$ is $\sin^{-1}\frac{k - 1}{k + 1}$. For $k = 3$ it is $\sin^{-1}\frac{1}{2} =
  \frac{\pi}{6}$, attained at $x = \frac{\pi}{3}, y = \frac{\pi}{6}$.
  %404
\item We use the standard results $r = 4R\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}$ and $r_1 =
  4R\sin\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}$.

  (a) The incircle touches $AB$ at a distance $r$ from $I$ and $\angle IAB = \frac{A}{2}$, so $AI =
  \frac{r}{\sin\frac{A}{2}} = 4R\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}$.

  $\triangle OAB$ is isosceles with $\angle AOB = 2C$, so $\angle OAB = 90^\circ - C$. Since $\angle IAB =
  \frac{A}{2}$, $\angle OAI = \left|\frac{A}{2} - 90^\circ + C\right| = \frac{|C - B|}{2}$ (with signed angles
  this holds for every triangle).

  Applying the cosine rule to $\triangle OAI$ and writing $p = \sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}, q =
  \cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}$ so that $\cos\frac{B - C}{2} = q + p$,

  $OI^2 = R^2 + 16R^2p^2 - 8R^2p(q + p) = R^2 - 8R^2p(q - p)$

  But $q - p = \cos\frac{B + C}{2} = \sin\frac{A}{2}$

  $\therefore OI^2 = R^2 - 8R^2\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2} = R^2 - 2Rr$.

  (b) $I_1$ lies on the line $AI$ at distance $r_1$ from $AB$, so $AI_1 = \frac{r_1}{\sin\frac{A}{2}} =
  4Rq$, and $\angle OAI_1 = \angle OAI = \frac{|B - C|}{2}$.

  $\therefore OI_1^2 = R^2 + 16R^2q^2 - 8R^2q(q + p) = R^2 + 8R^2q(q - p) = R^2 +
  8R^2\sin\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2} = R^2 + 2Rr_1$.

  (c) Similarly $OI_2^2 = R^2 + 2Rr_2, OI_3^2 = R^2 + 2Rr_3$. Hence the sum is $4R^2 + 2R(r_1 + r_2 + r_3 -
  r)$.

  Now $r_1 - r = \frac{\Delta}{s - a} - \frac{\Delta}{s} = \frac{\Delta a}{s(s - a)}$ and $r_2 + r_3 =
  \Delta\frac{(s - c) + (s - b)}{(s - b)(s - c)} = \frac{\Delta a}{(s - b)(s - c)}$.

  $\Rightarrow r_1 + r_2 + r_3 - r = \frac{\Delta a[(s - b)(s - c) + s(s - a)]}{s(s - a)(s - b)(s - c)}$

  Since $(s - b)(s - c) + s(s - a) = 2s^2 - s(a + b + c) + bc = bc$ and $s(s - a)(s - b)(s - c) = \Delta^2$,

  $r_1 + r_2 + r_3 - r = \frac{abc}{\Delta} = 4R$.

  $\therefore OI^2 + OI_1^2 + OI_2^2 + OI_3^2 = 4R^2 + 8R^2 = 12R^2$.

  Finally $R^2 - 2Rr = OI^2 \geq 0 \Rightarrow R\geq 2r$. Equality means $O = I$; then $\angle OAI = 0$ gives
  $B = C$ and similarly $C = A$, i.e.\ the triangle is equilateral.
  %405
\item Take $O$ as origin and let $\vec{h} = \vec{OA} + \vec{OB} + \vec{OC}$.

  $(\vec{h} - \vec{OA})\cdot(\vec{OB} - \vec{OC}) = (\vec{OB} + \vec{OC})\cdot(\vec{OB} - \vec{OC}) = OB^2 -
  OC^2 = 0$

  So the point with position vector $\vec{h}$ lies on the altitude from $A$; similarly it lies on the other
  altitudes. Hence $\vec{OH} = \vec{OA} + \vec{OB} + \vec{OC}$.

  $OH^2 = 3R^2 + 2R^2(\cos\angle BOC + \cos\angle COA + \cos\angle AOB) = 3R^2 + 2R^2(\cos2A + \cos2B +
  \cos2C)$, because each central angle is $2A$ or $2\pi - 2A$ etc.

  Now $\cos2A + \cos2B = 2\cos(A + B)\cos(A - B) = -2\cos C\cos(A - B)$ and $\cos 2C = 2\cos^2C - 1 =
  -2\cos C\cos(A + B) - 1$.

  $\Rightarrow \cos2A + \cos2B + \cos2C = -1 - 2\cos C[\cos(A - B) + \cos(A + B)] = -1 - 4\cos A\cos B\cos C$

  $\therefore OH^2 = 3R^2 - 2R^2 - 8R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C = R^2(1 - 8\cos A\cos B\cos C)$.

  Also $\sin^2A + \sin^2B + \sin^2C = \frac{3 - (\cos2A + \cos2B + \cos2C)}{2} = 2 + 2\cos A\cos B\cos C$

  $\Rightarrow a^2 + b^2 + c^2 = 4R^2(2 + 2\cos A\cos B\cos C) = 8R^2 + 8R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C$

  $\Rightarrow 9R^2 - (a^2 + b^2 + c^2) = R^2 - 8R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C = OH^2$.

  Since $a^2 + b^2 + c^2 > 0, OH^2 < 9R^2$, i.e.\ $OH < 3R$.

  Also $a^2 + b^2 + c^2 - 8R^2 = 8R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C$, which is positive, zero or negative according as
  all angles are acute, one is right or one is obtuse.
  %406
\item Let $p = \sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}, q = \cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}, S = \sin\frac{A}{2}$. Then
  $q - p = \cos\frac{B + C}{2} = S, q + p = \cos\frac{B - C}{2} = 2p + S$ and $\cos A = 1 - 2S^2$.

  As in the proof of Euler's theorem, $AI = 4Rp$ and $r = 4RpS$.

  If $BE$ is the altitude from $B$, then $AE = c\cos A$ and in $\triangle AHE, \angle HAE = 90^\circ - C$, so
  $\angle AHE = C$ and $AH = \frac{c\cos A}{\sin C} = 2R\cos A$.

  $\angle BAH = 90^\circ - B$ and $\angle BAI = \frac{A}{2}$, so $\angle IAH = \left|\frac{A}{2} - 90^\circ +
  B\right| = \frac{|B - C|}{2}$.

  Cosine rule in $\triangle AIH$:

  $IH^2 = 16R^2p^2 + 4R^2\cos^2A - 16R^2p\cos A(2p + S) = 4R^2\left[4p^2 + \cos^2A - 8p^2\cos A - 4pS\cos
  A\right]$

  On the other hand, $\cos B\cos C = \frac{1}{2}[\cos(B - C) + \cos(B + C)] = \frac{1}{2}\left[2\cos^2\frac{B
  - C}{2} - 1 - \cos A\right] = (2p + S)^2 - (1 - S^2)$

  $= 4p^2 + 4pS + 2S^2 - 1 = 4p^2 + 4pS - \cos A$

  $\Rightarrow 2r^2 - 4R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C = 4R^2\left[8p^2S^2 - 4p^2\cos A - 4pS\cos A + \cos^2A\right]$

  Subtracting, $IH^2 - (2r^2 - 4R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C) = 4R^2\left[4p^2 - 4p^2\cos A - 8p^2S^2\right] =
  16R^2p^2(1 - \cos A - 2S^2) = 0$.

  $\therefore IH^2 = 2r^2 - 4R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C$.
  %407
\item $IN$ is a median of $\triangle OIH$, so by Apollonius' theorem

  $IN^2 = \frac{2IO^2 + 2IH^2 - OH^2}{4}$

  $= \frac{2R^2 - 4Rr + 4r^2 - 8R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C - R^2 + 8R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C}{4}$

  $= \frac{R^2 - 4Rr + 4r^2}{4} = \left(\frac{R}{2} - r\right)^2$

  Since $R\geq 2r$, $IN = \frac{R}{2} - r$.

  The distance between the centers of the nine-point circle (radius $\frac{R}{2}$) and the incircle (radius
  $r$) equals the difference of their radii, so the two circles touch internally.
  %408
\item Let $x = s - a, y = s - b, z = s - c$, so that $x + y + z = s$ and $a = y + z, b = z + x, c = x + y$.

  By Hero's formula $\Delta^2 = sxyz$ and $\Delta = rs$, so $xyz = r^2s$.

  It is known (see the proof of problem 404) that $r_1 + r_2 + r_3 = 4R + r$. Since $r_1 = \frac{\Delta}{x}$
  etc.,

  $4R + r = \Delta\frac{xy + yz + zx}{xyz} = \frac{rs(xy + yz + zx)}{r^2s} = \frac{xy + yz + zx}{r}$

  $\Rightarrow xy + yz + zx = r^2 + 4Rr$

  Now $abc = 4R\Delta = 4Rrs$.

  $(y + z)(z + x) = z^2 + (xy + yz + zx)$, so summing cyclically

  $ab + bc + ca = x^2 + y^2 + z^2 + 3(xy + yz + zx) = (x + y + z)^2 + (xy + yz + zx) = s^2 + r^2 + 4Rr$

  $a^2 + b^2 + c^2 = (a + b + c)^2 - 2(ab + bc + ca) = 4s^2 - 2s^2 - 2r^2 - 8Rr = 2(s^2 - r^2 - 4Rr)$

  Since $a + b + c = 2s$, by Vieta's relations $a, b, c$ are the roots of $t^3 - 2st^2 + (s^2 + r^2 + 4Rr)t
  - 4Rrs = 0$.
  %409
\item Take $G$ as origin, so that $\vec{GA} + \vec{GB} + \vec{GC} = \vec{0}$. For any point $P$,

  $PA^2 + PB^2 + PC^2 = \sum|\vec{GA} - \vec{GP}|^2 = 3GP^2 - 2\vec{GP}\cdot\sum\vec{GA} + \sum GA^2 = 3PG^2 +
  GA^2 + GB^2 + GC^2$

  $GA = \frac{2}{3}m_a$ and $m_a^2 + m_b^2 + m_c^2 = \frac{3}{4}(a^2 + b^2 + c^2)$, so $GA^2 + GB^2 + GC^2 =
  \frac{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}{3}$.

  Putting $P = O$: $3R^2 = 3OG^2 + \frac{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}{3}\Rightarrow OG^2 = R^2 - \frac{a^2 + b^2 +
  c^2}{9}$.

  Putting $P = I$: the tangent from $A$ to the incircle has length $s - a$, so $IA^2 = r^2 + (s - a)^2$.

  With $x = s - a$ etc., $x^2 + y^2 + z^2 = s^2 - 2(xy + yz + zx) = s^2 - 2r^2 - 8Rr$ (problem 408).

  $\Rightarrow IA^2 + IB^2 + IC^2 = 3r^2 + s^2 - 2r^2 - 8Rr = s^2 + r^2 - 8Rr$

  Using $a^2 + b^2 + c^2 = 2(s^2 - r^2 - 4Rr)$,

  $3IG^2 = s^2 + r^2 - 8Rr - \frac{2(s^2 - r^2 - 4Rr)}{3} = \frac{s^2 + 5r^2 - 16Rr}{3}$

  $\therefore IG^2 = \frac{1}{9}\left(s^2 + 5r^2 - 16Rr\right)$.

  Since $IG^2\geq 0$, $s^2\geq 16Rr - 5r^2$, with equality only when $I = G$, i.e.\ for an equilateral
  triangle.
  %410
\item Let $A = 3\alpha, B = 3\beta, C = 3\gamma$, so that $\alpha + \beta + \gamma = 60^\circ$.

  First, $4\sin\alpha\sin(60^\circ - \alpha)\sin(60^\circ + \alpha) = 4\sin\alpha(\sin^260^\circ -
  \sin^2\alpha) = 3\sin\alpha - 4\sin^3\alpha = \sin3\alpha$.

  $\Rightarrow a = 2R\sin3\alpha = 8R\sin\alpha\sin(60^\circ - \alpha)\sin(60^\circ + \alpha)$, and similarly
  for $b, c$.

  In $\triangle BPC, \angle PBC = \beta, \angle PCB = \gamma$, so $\angle BPC = 180^\circ - (\beta + \gamma) =
  120^\circ + \alpha$.

  $\Rightarrow BP = \frac{a\sin\gamma}{\sin(120^\circ + \alpha)} = \frac{a\sin\gamma}{\sin(60^\circ -
  \alpha)} = 8R\sin\alpha\sin\gamma\sin(60^\circ + \alpha)$

  In $\triangle AR'B, \angle R'AB = \alpha, \angle R'BA = \beta$, so $\angle AR'B = 120^\circ + \gamma$.

  $\Rightarrow BR' = \frac{c\sin\alpha}{\sin(60^\circ - \gamma)} = 8R\sin\alpha\sin\gamma\sin(60^\circ +
  \gamma)$

  The middle trisector of $B$ gives $\angle PBR' = \beta$. Cosine rule in $\triangle PBR'$:

  $PR'^2 = 64R^2\sin^2\alpha\sin^2\gamma\left[\sin^2(60^\circ + \alpha) + \sin^2(60^\circ + \gamma) -
  2\sin(60^\circ + \alpha)\sin(60^\circ + \gamma)\cos\beta\right]$

  The angles $X = 60^\circ + \alpha, Y = 60^\circ + \gamma, Z = \beta$ are positive and $X + Y + Z =
  120^\circ + \alpha + \beta + \gamma = 180^\circ$. In a triangle with these angles and circumradius
  $\frac{1}{2}$ the sides are $\sin X, \sin Y, \sin Z$, so the cosine rule gives $\sin^2X + \sin^2Y - 2\sin
  X\sin Y\cos Z = \sin^2Z$.

  $\therefore PR'^2 = 64R^2\sin^2\alpha\sin^2\beta\sin^2\gamma$, i.e.\ $PR' =
  8R\sin\alpha\sin\beta\sin\gamma$.

  The expression is symmetric in $\alpha, \beta, \gamma$, so in the same way $PQ = QR' =
  8R\sin\alpha\sin\beta\sin\gamma$. Hence $\triangle PQR'$ is equilateral with side $8R\sin\frac{A}{3}\sin
  \frac{B}{3}\sin\frac{C}{3}$.
  %411
\item Since the triangle is acute and $\theta < 90^\circ$, $\angle ABA' = B + \theta < 180^\circ$ and $\angle
  ACA' = C + \theta < 180^\circ$, so $ABA'C$ is a convex quadrilateral and its diagonals $AA'$ and $BC$ meet
  at a point $X$ between $B$ and $C$.

  The triangles $ABA'$ and $ACA'$ have the common base $AA'$, so

  $\frac{BX}{XC} = \frac{[ABA']}{[ACA']} = \frac{\frac{1}{2}c\cdot BA'\sin(B + \theta)}{\frac{1}{2}b\cdot
  CA'\sin(C + \theta)} = \frac{c\sin(B + \theta)}{b\sin(C + \theta)}$, as $BA' = CA'$.

  Similarly, if $BB'$ meets $CA$ at $Y$ and $CC'$ meets $AB$ at $Z$,

  $\frac{CY}{YA} = \frac{a\sin(C + \theta)}{c\sin(A + \theta)}, \frac{AZ}{ZB} = \frac{b\sin(A + \theta)}{a\sin(B
  + \theta)}$

  $\Rightarrow \frac{BX}{XC}\cdot\frac{CY}{YA}\cdot\frac{AZ}{ZB} = \frac{c}{b}\cdot\frac{a}{c}\cdot\frac{b}{a} =
  1$

  By the converse of Ceva's theorem $AA', BB', CC'$ are concurrent.

  Taking $\theta = 60^\circ$ the erected triangles are equilateral, so the lines joining the vertices to the
  outer vertices of the equilateral triangles are concurrent.
  %412
\item Let $X$ be the center of the equilateral triangle on $BC$ and $Y$ that on $CA$. Then $CX =
  \frac{a}{\sqrt{3}}, CY = \frac{b}{\sqrt{3}}$ and $\angle XCB = \angle YCA = 30^\circ$, so $\angle XCY = C +
  60^\circ$ (or $360^\circ$ minus this, which has the same cosine).

  $XY^2 = \frac{a^2 + b^2}{3} - \frac{2ab}{3}\cos(C + 60^\circ) = \frac{a^2 + b^2}{3} - \frac{ab\cos C}{3} +
  \frac{ab\sin C}{\sqrt{3}}$

  Using $2ab\cos C = a^2 + b^2 - c^2$ and $ab\sin C = 2\Delta$,

  $XY^2 = \frac{a^2 + b^2}{3} - \frac{a^2 + b^2 - c^2}{6} + \frac{2\Delta}{\sqrt{3}} = \frac{a^2 + b^2 +
  c^2}{6} + \frac{2\Delta}{\sqrt{3}}$

  This is symmetric in $a, b, c$, so $YZ = ZX = XY$ and $\triangle XYZ$ is equilateral.

  For the inner triangles, $X'$ and $Y'$ lie on the same sides as the triangle, $CX' = \frac{a}{\sqrt{3}},
  CY' = \frac{b}{\sqrt{3}}$ and the rays $CX', CY'$ are obtained by turning $CB, CA$ through $30^\circ$
  towards each other, so $\angle X'CY' = |C - 60^\circ|$.

  $X'Y'^2 = \frac{a^2 + b^2}{3} - \frac{2ab}{3}\cos(C - 60^\circ) = \frac{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}{6} -
  \frac{2\Delta}{\sqrt{3}}$

  Again this is symmetric, so $\triangle X'Y'Z'$ is equilateral.

  Difference of areas $= \frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\left(XY^2 - X'Y'^2\right) =
  \frac{\sqrt{3}}{4}\cdot\frac{4\Delta}{\sqrt{3}} = \Delta$.
  %413
\item Let $A'$ be the vertex of the equilateral triangle erected outwards on $BC$.

  $\angle BFC + \angle BA'C = 120^\circ + 60^\circ = 180^\circ$ and $F, A'$ lie on opposite sides of $BC$, so
  $B, F, C, A'$ lie on a circle; let its radius be $\rho$.

  $\angle BFA' = \angle BCA' = 60^\circ$ (angles on the same arc $BA'$), so $\angle AFB + \angle BFA' =
  180^\circ$ and $A, F, A'$ are collinear with $F$ between $A$ and $A'$.

  Let $\angle FBC = u$; then $\angle FCB = 60^\circ - u$ and $\angle FBA' = u + 60^\circ$. By the extended
  sine rule in the circle,

  $FC = 2\rho\sin u, FB = 2\rho\sin(60^\circ - u), FA' = 2\rho\sin(u + 60^\circ)$

  $FB + FC = 2\rho\cdot2\sin30^\circ\cos(u - 30^\circ) = 2\rho\cos(u - 30^\circ) = 2\rho\sin(u + 60^\circ) =
  FA'$

  $\Rightarrow FA + FB + FC = AF + FA' = AA'$

  In $\triangle ABA', BA' = a$ and $\angle ABA' = B + 60^\circ$.

  $AA'^2 = c^2 + a^2 - 2ac\cos(B + 60^\circ) = c^2 + a^2 - ac\cos B + \sqrt{3}ac\sin B$

  $= c^2 + a^2 - \frac{c^2 + a^2 - b^2}{2} + 2\sqrt{3}\Delta = \frac{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}{2} + 2\sqrt{3}\Delta$

  $\therefore (FA + FB + FC)^2 = \frac{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}{2} + 2\sqrt{3}\Delta$.
  %414
\item Let $E, F$ be the feet of the perpendiculars from $P$ to $CA, AB$, so $PE = y, PF = z$.

  Since $\angle AEP = \angle AFP = 90^\circ$, the points $A, E, P, F$ lie on the circle with diameter $AP$.
  The chord $EF$ subtends $\angle A$ at $A$, so $EF = AP\sin A$.

  In $\triangle PEF, \angle EPF = 180^\circ - A$, so $EF^2 = y^2 + z^2 + 2yz\cos A$.

  As $\cos A = -\cos(B + C) = \sin B\sin C - \cos B\cos C$,

  $EF^2 = (y\sin C + z\sin B)^2 + (y\cos C - z\cos B)^2 \geq (y\sin C + z\sin B)^2$

  $\Rightarrow PA\geq y\frac{\sin C}{\sin A} + z\frac{\sin B}{\sin A}$

  Similarly $PB\geq z\frac{\sin A}{\sin B} + x\frac{\sin C}{\sin B}, PC\geq x\frac{\sin B}{\sin C} +
  y\frac{\sin A}{\sin C}$.

  Adding, $PA + PB + PC\geq x\left(\frac{\sin B}{\sin C} + \frac{\sin C}{\sin B}\right) + y\left(\frac{\sin
  C}{\sin A} + \frac{\sin A}{\sin C}\right) + z\left(\frac{\sin A}{\sin B} + \frac{\sin B}{\sin A}\right)$

  Since $t + \frac{1}{t}\geq 2$ for $t > 0$, $PA + PB + PC\geq 2(x + y + z)$.

  Equality requires $A = B = C$ and $y\cos C = z\cos B$ etc., i.e.\ an equilateral triangle with $P$ at its
  center.
  %415
\item $a^2 - (b - c)^2 = 2bc(1 - \cos A)$ and $2bc = \frac{4\Delta}{\sin A}$.

  $\Rightarrow a^2 - (b - c)^2 = 4\Delta\frac{1 - \cos A}{\sin A} = 4\Delta\tan\frac{A}{2}$

  Adding the three such relations,

  $a^2 + b^2 + c^2 - (a - b)^2 - (b - c)^2 - (c - a)^2 = 4\Delta\left(\tan\frac{A}{2} + \tan\frac{B}{2} +
  \tan\frac{C}{2}\right)$

  Let $x = \tan\frac{A}{2}, y = \tan\frac{B}{2}, z = \tan\frac{C}{2}$, all positive. Since $\frac{C}{2} =
  90^\circ - \frac{A + B}{2}$, $z = \cot\frac{A + B}{2} = \frac{1 - xy}{x + y}$, i.e.\ $xy + yz + zx = 1$.

  $(x + y + z)^2 - 3(xy + yz + zx) = \frac{1}{2}\left[(x - y)^2 + (y - z)^2 + (z - x)^2\right]\geq 0$

  $\Rightarrow x + y + z\geq \sqrt{3}$

  $\therefore a^2 + b^2 + c^2\geq 4\sqrt{3}\Delta + (a - b)^2 + (b - c)^2 + (c - a)^2$, and dropping the
  non-negative squares gives $a^2 + b^2 + c^2\geq 4\sqrt{3}\Delta$. Equality holds only for an equilateral
  triangle.
  %416
\item Let $D, E, F$ be the feet of the altitudes from $A, B, C$.

  (a) From the right triangles $ABE$ and $ACF$, $AE = c\cos A, AF = b\cos A$. Thus $\frac{AE}{AB} =
  \frac{AF}{AC} = \cos A$ and $\triangle AEF\sim\triangle ABC$ with ratio $\cos A$, so $EF = a\cos A$.
  Similarly $FD = b\cos B, DE = c\cos C$.

  (b) Perimeter $= a\cos A + b\cos B + c\cos C = R(\sin2A + \sin2B + \sin2C)$.

  $\sin2A + \sin2B = 2\sin(A + B)\cos(A - B) = 2\sin C\cos(A - B)$ and $\sin2C = -2\sin C\cos(A + B)$

  $\Rightarrow \sin2A + \sin2B + \sin2C = 2\sin C[\cos(A - B) - \cos(A + B)] = 4\sin A\sin B\sin C$

  So the perimeter is $4R\sin A\sin B\sin C$. Since $\Delta = \frac{1}{2}ab\sin C = 2R^2\sin A\sin B\sin C$,
  this equals $\frac{2\Delta}{R}$.

  (c) By the similarity in (a), $\angle AFE = C$ and similarly $\angle BFD = C$, so $\angle DFE = 180^\circ -
  2C$; in the same way $\angle EDF = 180^\circ - 2A$. The circumradius of $\triangle DEF$ is

  $\frac{EF}{2\sin(180^\circ - 2A)} = \frac{2R\sin A\cos A}{2\sin2A} = \frac{R}{2}$

  (d) Area of $\triangle DEF = \frac{EF\cdot FD\cdot DE}{4\cdot\frac{R}{2}} = \frac{abc\cos A\cos B\cos
  C}{2R} = 4R^2\sin A\sin B\sin C\cos A\cos B\cos C$

  Inradius $= \frac{\rm area}{\rm semi-perimeter} = \frac{4R^2\sin A\sin B\sin C\cos A\cos B\cos C}{2R\sin
  A\sin B\sin C} = 2R\cos A\cos B\cos C$.
  %417
\item Let $\delta$ denote the area of the pedal triangle.

  $P = O$: the feet are the midpoints of the sides, the pedal triangle is the medial triangle and $\delta =
  \frac{\Delta}{4}$. Since $OP = 0$, $\frac{R^2 - 0}{4R^2}\Delta = \frac{\Delta}{4}$.

  $P = I$: the feet $D, E, F$ are the points of contact and $ID = IE = IF = r$. In quadrilateral $AFIE$ the
  angles at $E, F$ are right, so $\angle EIF = 180^\circ - A$; similarly for the others. The angles of
  $\triangle DEF$ are $90^\circ - \frac{A}{2}$ etc., all acute, so its circumcenter $I$ is inside it.

  $\delta = \frac{1}{2}r^2[\sin(180^\circ - A) + \sin(180^\circ - B) + \sin(180^\circ - C)] =
  \frac{r^2}{2}\cdot\frac{a + b + c}{2R} = \frac{r^2s}{2R} = \frac{r\Delta}{2R}$

  By Euler's theorem $R^2 - OI^2 = 2Rr$, so $\frac{R^2 - OI^2}{4R^2}\Delta = \frac{r\Delta}{2R} = \delta$.

  $P = H$: the pedal triangle is the orthic triangle $DEF$. As in problem 416, $[AEF] = \Delta\cos^2A$ etc.

  $\Rightarrow \delta = \Delta(1 - \cos^2A - \cos^2B - \cos^2C)$

  Now $\cos^2A + \cos^2B = 1 + \frac{\cos2A + \cos2B}{2} = 1 + \cos(A + B)\cos(A - B) = 1 - \cos C\cos(A -
  B)$ and $\cos^2C = -\cos C\cos(A + B)$.

  $\Rightarrow \cos^2A + \cos^2B + \cos^2C = 1 - 2\cos A\cos B\cos C$

  $\Rightarrow \delta = 2\Delta\cos A\cos B\cos C$

  By problem 405, $R^2 - OH^2 = 8R^2\cos A\cos B\cos C$, so $\frac{R^2 - OH^2}{4R^2}\Delta = 2\Delta\cos A\cos
  B\cos C = \delta$.
  %418
\item In $\triangle AB\Omega, \angle\Omega AB = \omega, \angle\Omega BA = B - \omega$, so $\angle A\Omega B =
  180^\circ - B$.

  $\Rightarrow B\Omega = \frac{c\sin\omega}{\sin B}$

  In $\triangle B\Omega C, \angle\Omega BC = \omega, \angle\Omega CB = C - \omega$, so $\angle B\Omega C =
  180^\circ - C$.

  $\Rightarrow B\Omega = \frac{a\sin(C - \omega)}{\sin C}$

  Equating and using $a = 2R\sin A, c = 2R\sin C$,

  $\sin^2C\sin\omega = \sin A\sin B(\sin C\cos\omega - \cos C\sin\omega)$

  Dividing by $\sin A\sin B\sin C\sin\omega$:

  $\frac{\sin C}{\sin A\sin B} = \cot\omega - \cot C$

  But $\frac{\sin C}{\sin A\sin B} = \frac{\sin(A + B)}{\sin A\sin B} = \cot A + \cot B$.

  $\therefore \cot\omega = \cot A + \cot B + \cot C$.

  Since $\cos A = \frac{b^2 + c^2 - a^2}{2bc}$ and $\sin A = \frac{2\Delta}{bc}$, $\cot A = \frac{b^2 + c^2 -
  a^2}{4\Delta}$. Adding, $\cot\omega = \frac{a^2 + b^2 + c^2}{4\Delta}$.

  From $\cot(A + B) = -\cot C$ we get $\cot A\cot B + \cot B\cot C + \cot C\cot A = 1$. Hence

  $\cot^2\omega = (\cot A + \cot B + \cot C)^2\geq 3(\cot A\cot B + \cot B\cot C + \cot C\cot A) = 3$

  As $\cot\omega > 0$, $\cot\omega\geq\sqrt{3}$, i.e.\ $\omega\leq 30^\circ$, with equality only for an
  equilateral triangle.
  %419
\item By the previous problem and $\cot A\cot B + \cot B\cot C + \cot C\cot A = 1$,

  ${\rm cosec}^2\omega = 1 + (\cot A + \cot B + \cot C)^2 = 1 + \cot^2A + \cot^2B + \cot^2C + 2$

  $= (1 + \cot^2A) + (1 + \cot^2B) + (1 + \cot^2C) = {\rm cosec}^2A + {\rm cosec}^2B + {\rm cosec}^2C$

  For the second part, from the previous problem $B\Omega = \frac{c\sin\omega}{\sin B} = \frac{2R\sin
  C\sin\omega}{\sin B}$.

  Let $B\Omega$ produced meet the circumcircle again at $B_1$; $B_1$ lies on the arc $CA$ not containing $B$,
  hence on the same side of $BC$ as $A$.

  $\angle CBB_1 = \omega\Rightarrow CB_1 = 2R\sin\omega$, and $\angle BB_1C = \angle BAC = A$.

  $\angle C\Omega B_1 = 180^\circ - \angle B\Omega C = C$, so in $\triangle\Omega CB_1, \angle\Omega CB_1 =
  180^\circ - A - C = B$.

  $\Rightarrow \Omega B_1 = \frac{CB_1\sin B}{\sin C} = \frac{2R\sin\omega\sin B}{\sin C}$

  By the power of the point $\Omega$ with respect to the circumcircle,

  $R^2 - O\Omega^2 = B\Omega\cdot\Omega B_1 = 4R^2\sin^2\omega$

  $\therefore O\Omega = R\sqrt{1 - 4\sin^2\omega}$.
  %420
\item Let $A = \angle DAB, C = \angle BCD$. The cosine rule for $BD$ in $\triangle ABD$ and $\triangle CBD$
  gives

  $a^2 + d^2 - 2ad\cos A = b^2 + c^2 - 2bc\cos C\Rightarrow a^2 + d^2 - b^2 - c^2 = 2ad\cos A - 2bc\cos C$

  Also $K = \frac{1}{2}ad\sin A + \frac{1}{2}bc\sin C\Rightarrow 4K = 2ad\sin A + 2bc\sin C$.

  Squaring and adding,

  $16K^2 + (a^2 + d^2 - b^2 - c^2)^2 = 4a^2d^2 + 4b^2c^2 - 8abcd\cos(A + C)$

  Writing $\cos(A + C) = 2\cos^2\frac{A + C}{2} - 1$, the right side is $4(ad + bc)^2 - 16abcd\cos^2\frac{A
  + C}{2}$.

  $(2ad + 2bc)^2 - (a^2 + d^2 - b^2 - c^2)^2 = [(b + c)^2 - (a - d)^2][(a + d)^2 - (b - c)^2]$

  $= (b + c + d - a)(a + b + c - d)(a + c + d - b)(a + b + d - c) = 16(s - a)(s - b)(s - c)(s - d)$

  $\therefore K^2 = (s - a)(s - b)(s - c)(s - d) - abcd\cos^2\frac{A + C}{2}$.

  For a cyclic quadrilateral $A + C = 180^\circ$, so $K = \sqrt{(s - a)(s - b)(s - c)(s - d)}$
  (Brahmagupta).

  For fixed sides $K^2$ is greatest when $\cos\frac{A + C}{2} = 0$, i.e.\ $A + C = 180^\circ$, i.e.\ when the
  quadrilateral is cyclic.

  For sides $1, 2, 3, 4$: $s = 5$ and $K_{\max} = \sqrt{4\cdot3\cdot2\cdot1} = 2\sqrt{6}$.
  %421
\item As $ABCD$ is cyclic, $C = 180^\circ - A$. The cosine rule for $BD$ in $\triangle ABD$ and $\triangle
  CBD$:

  $BD^2 = a^2 + d^2 - 2ad\cos A = b^2 + c^2 + 2bc\cos A$

  $\Rightarrow \cos A = \frac{a^2 + d^2 - b^2 - c^2}{2(ad + bc)}$

  $\Rightarrow BD^2 = a^2 + d^2 - \frac{ad(a^2 + d^2 - b^2 - c^2)}{ad + bc} = \frac{bc(a^2 + d^2) + ad(b^2 +
  c^2)}{ad + bc}$

  Since $(ab + cd)(ac + bd) = a^2bc + ab^2d + ac^2d + bcd^2 = bc(a^2 + d^2) + ad(b^2 + c^2)$,

  $BD^2 = \frac{(ab + cd)(ac + bd)}{ad + bc}$

  By the same argument with the diagonal $AC$ (interchanging the roles of $b$ and $d$),

  $AC^2 = \frac{(ac + bd)(ad + bc)}{ab + cd}$

  Multiplying, $AC^2\cdot BD^2 = (ac + bd)^2$, so $AC\cdot BD = ac + bd$ (Ptolemy).

  $K = \frac{1}{2}ad\sin A + \frac{1}{2}bc\sin C = \frac{1}{2}(ad + bc)\sin A\Rightarrow \sin A =
  \frac{2K}{ad + bc}$

  The circumcircle of $ABCD$ is that of $\triangle ABD$, so

  $R = \frac{BD}{2\sin A} = \frac{BD(ad + bc)}{4K} = \frac{\sqrt{(ab + cd)(ac + bd)(ad + bc)}}{4K}$.
  %422
\item Let each side of length $3$ subtend $2x$ and each side of length $5$ subtend $2y$ at the center. Then
  $6x + 6y = 360^\circ$, i.e.\ $x + y = 60^\circ$, and $3 = 2R\sin x, 5 = 2R\sin y$.

  $\Rightarrow 5\sin x = 3\sin(60^\circ - x) = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}\cos x - \frac{3}{2}\sin x$

  $\Rightarrow \tan x = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{13}\Rightarrow \sin x = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{\sqrt{27 + 169}} =
  \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{14}, \cos x = \frac{13}{14}$

  $R = \frac{3}{2\sin x} = \frac{3\cdot14}{6\sqrt{3}} = \frac{7}{\sqrt{3}}$

  The required chord joins the ends of the three sides of length $3$, so it subtends $6x$ at the center and its
  length is $2R\sin3x$.

  $\sin3x = \sin x(3 - 4\sin^2x) = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{14}\left(3 - \frac{108}{196}\right) =
  \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{14}\cdot\frac{120}{49}$

  $\Rightarrow$ chord $= \frac{14}{\sqrt{3}}\cdot\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{14}\cdot\frac{120}{49} = \frac{360}{49}$

  (As a check, $2R\sin3y = 2R\sin(180^\circ - 3x)$ gives the same chord from the other side.)
  %423
\item Let $x = \frac{\pi}{n}$, so $0 < x\leq\frac{\pi}{4}$. The chord $A_1A_{k + 1} = 2R\sin kx$, hence

  $\frac{1}{\sin x} - \frac{1}{\sin3x} = \frac{1}{\sin2x}\Rightarrow \frac{\sin3x - \sin x}{\sin x\sin3x} =
  \frac{1}{\sin2x}$

  $\Rightarrow 2\cos2x\sin x\sin2x = \sin x\sin3x\Rightarrow 2\sin2x\cos2x = \sin3x\Rightarrow \sin4x =
  \sin3x$

  $\Rightarrow 2\cos\frac{7x}{2}\sin\frac{x}{2} = 0$. As $\sin\frac{x}{2}\neq 0$, $\cos\frac{7x}{2} = 0$.

  Since $0 < \frac{7x}{2}\leq\frac{7\pi}{8} < \frac{3\pi}{2}$, $\frac{7x}{2} = \frac{\pi}{2}$, i.e.\ $x =
  \frac{\pi}{7}$ and $n = 7$.

  For $n = 7$: $A_1A_2^2 + A_1A_3^2 + A_1A_4^2 = 4R^2\left(\sin^2\frac{\pi}{7} + \sin^2\frac{2\pi}{7} +
  \sin^2\frac{3\pi}{7}\right) = 2R^2\left[3 - \left(\cos\frac{2\pi}{7} + \cos\frac{4\pi}{7} +
  \cos\frac{6\pi}{7}\right)\right]$

  Multiplying $S = \cos\frac{2\pi}{7} + \cos\frac{4\pi}{7} + \cos\frac{6\pi}{7}$ by $2\sin\frac{\pi}{7}$
  telescopes: $2S\sin\frac{\pi}{7} = \sin\pi - \sin\frac{\pi}{7} = -\sin\frac{\pi}{7}$, so $S =
  -\frac{1}{2}$.

  $\therefore A_1A_2^2 + A_1A_3^2 + A_1A_4^2 = 2R^2\cdot\frac{7}{2} = 7R^2$.
  %424
\item (a) $b^2 - a^2 = 4R^2(\sin^2B - \sin^2A) = 4R^2\sin(B + A)\sin(B - A) =
  4R^2\sin\frac{3\pi}{7}\sin\frac{\pi}{7}$.

  Since $\sin\frac{3\pi}{7} = \sin\frac{4\pi}{7} = \sin C$, $b^2 - a^2 = (2R\sin C)(2R\sin A) = ca$.

  $c^2 - b^2 = 4R^2\sin\frac{6\pi}{7}\sin\frac{2\pi}{7} = 4R^2\sin\frac{\pi}{7}\sin\frac{2\pi}{7} = ab$.

  $c^2 - a^2 = 4R^2\sin\frac{5\pi}{7}\sin\frac{3\pi}{7} = 4R^2\sin\frac{2\pi}{7}\sin\frac{4\pi}{7} = bc$.

  (b) $(a^2 - b^2)(b^2 - c^2)(c^2 - a^2) = (-ca)(-ab)(bc) = a^2b^2c^2$.

  (c) $8\sin\frac{\pi}{7}\cos\frac{\pi}{7}\cos\frac{2\pi}{7}\cos\frac{4\pi}{7} = \sin\frac{8\pi}{7} =
  -\sin\frac{\pi}{7}$, so $\cos A\cos B\cos C = -\frac{1}{8}$.

  By the identity $\cos^2A + \cos^2B + \cos^2C = 1 - 2\cos A\cos B\cos C$ (problem 417), $\cos^2A + \cos^2B +
  \cos^2C = 1 + \frac{1}{4} = \frac{5}{4}$.

  (d) By problem 405, $OH^2 = R^2(1 - 8\cos A\cos B\cos C) = R^2(1 + 1) = 2R^2$, so $OH = \sqrt{2}R$.
  %425
\item $a^2 - b^2 = 4R^2(\sin^2A - \sin^2B) = 4R^2\sin(A + B)\sin(A - B) = 4R^2\sin C\sin(A - B)$.

  If $A = 2B$, then $a^2 - b^2 = 4R^2\sin C\sin B = bc$, i.e.\ $a^2 = b(b + c)$.

  Conversely, if $a^2 - b^2 = bc$ then $4R^2\sin C\sin(A - B) = 4R^2\sin C\sin B\Rightarrow \sin(A - B) = \sin
  B$.

  $\Rightarrow A - B = B$ or $A - B = 180^\circ - B$; the latter gives $A = 180^\circ$, impossible. Hence $A =
  2B$.

  Now let the sides be integers with $A = 2B$ and least perimeter. The triangle must be primitive (else
  dividing by the common factor reduces the perimeter), so $\gcd(a, b, c) = 1$.

  If a prime $p$ divides both $b$ and $b + c$, then $p\mid c$ and $p\mid a^2 = b(b + c)$, so $p\mid a$,
  contradiction. So $b$ and $b + c$ are coprime and their product is a square; hence $b = m^2, b + c = n^2, a
  = mn$ with $\gcd(m, n) = 1$.

  $c > 0\Rightarrow n > m$. The triangle inequality $a + b > c$ gives $mn + m^2 > n^2 - m^2$, i.e.\ $(2m -
  n)(m + n) > 0$, i.e.\ $n < 2m$. (The other two inequalities follow from $n > m$.)

  Perimeter $= mn + n^2 = n(m + n)$ with $m < n < 2m$. $m = 1$ is impossible; $m = 2$ forces $n = 3$, giving
  perimeter $15$; $m\geq 3$ gives $n\geq 4$ and perimeter $\geq 28$.

  So the required triangle has $a = 6, b = 4, c = 5$. Check: $\cos B = \frac{36 + 25 - 16}{60} = \frac{3}{4},
  \cos A = \frac{16 + 25 - 36}{40} = \frac{1}{8} = 2\cdot\frac{9}{16} - 1 = \cos2B$.
  %426
\item $C = 180^\circ - 4B > 0\Rightarrow B < 45^\circ$, and $\sin C = \sin4B$.

  $\frac{a}{b} = \frac{\sin3B}{\sin B} = 3 - 4\sin^2B = 1 + 2\cos2B$. As $0 < 2B < 90^\circ, \cos2B > 0$, so $a
  > b$, and $\cos2B = \frac{a - b}{2b}$.

  $\frac{c}{b} = \frac{\sin4B}{\sin B} = \frac{4\sin B\cos B\cos2B}{\sin B} = 4\cos B\cos2B$

  $\Rightarrow \frac{c^2}{b^2} = 16\cos^2B\cos^22B = 8(1 + \cos2B)\cos^22B = 8\cdot\frac{a + b}{2b}\cdot\frac{(a
  - b)^2}{4b^2} = \frac{(a + b)(a - b)^2}{b^3}$

  $\therefore (a - b)^2(a + b) = bc^2$.

  For $a = 10, b = 8, c = 3$ (a valid triangle since $3 + 8 > 10$): $(a - b)^2(a + b) = 4\cdot18 = 72 = 8\cdot9 =
  bc^2$.

  Directly, $\cos B = \frac{100 + 9 - 64}{60} = \frac{3}{4}$, so $\cos3B = 4\cdot\frac{27}{64} - 3\cdot\frac{3}{4}
  = -\frac{9}{16}$, and $\cos A = \frac{64 + 9 - 100}{48} = -\frac{9}{16}$. Since $A$ and $3B$ both lie in
  $(0^\circ, 180^\circ)$, $A = 3B$.
  %427
\item Take $BC = 1$.

  In $\triangle BCE, \angle EBC = 80^\circ, \angle BCE = 50^\circ$, so $\angle BEC = 50^\circ$ and $BE = BC =
  1$.

  In $\triangle BCD, \angle CBD = 60^\circ, \angle BCD = 80^\circ$, so $\angle BDC = 40^\circ$ and $BD =
  \frac{\sin80^\circ}{\sin40^\circ} = 2\cos40^\circ$.

  In $\triangle BDE, \angle DBE = 80^\circ - 60^\circ = 20^\circ$. Let $\angle BDE = x$; then $\angle BED =
  160^\circ - x$ and by the sine rule

  $\frac{\sin(160^\circ - x)}{\sin x} = \frac{BD}{BE} = 2\cos40^\circ$

  $\frac{\sin(160^\circ - x)}{\sin x} = \sin160^\circ\cot x - \cos160^\circ = \sin20^\circ\cot x +
  \cos20^\circ$

  $\Rightarrow \cot x = \frac{2\cos40^\circ - \cos20^\circ}{\sin20^\circ}$

  $2\cos40^\circ - \cos20^\circ = \cos40^\circ - 2\sin30^\circ\sin10^\circ = \sin50^\circ - \sin10^\circ =
  2\cos30^\circ\sin20^\circ$

  $\Rightarrow \cot x = 2\cos30^\circ = \sqrt{3}$, and as $0 < x < 160^\circ$, $x = 30^\circ$.

  $\therefore \angle BDE = 30^\circ$.
  %428
\item Let $AB = AC = 1$. Then $BC = 2\sin10^\circ = AD$, and $AD < AC$, so $D$ lies on the segment $AC$.

  Let $\angle ABD = x$. In $\triangle ABD, \angle BAD = 20^\circ$ and $\angle ADB = 160^\circ - x$.

  $\frac{AB}{\sin(160^\circ - x)} = \frac{AD}{\sin x}\Rightarrow \frac{\sin(160^\circ - x)}{\sin x} =
  \frac{1}{2\sin10^\circ}$

  As before, $\frac{\sin(160^\circ - x)}{\sin x} = \sin20^\circ\cot x + \cos20^\circ$, which is strictly
  decreasing for $0 < x < 160^\circ$, so the equation has at most one root.

  For $x = 10^\circ$: $\frac{\sin150^\circ}{\sin10^\circ} = \frac{1}{2\sin10^\circ}$. So $x = 10^\circ$.

  $\therefore \angle ABD = 10^\circ$ (and $\angle DBC = 70^\circ$).
  %429
\item $\angle B = \angle C = 50^\circ$, so $\angle PBA = 40^\circ$ and $\angle PCA = 20^\circ$. Let $\angle PAB =
  x$; then $\angle PAC = 80^\circ - x$.

  By the trigonometric form of Ceva's theorem for the concurrent cevians $AP, BP, CP$,

  $\frac{\sin\angle PAB}{\sin\angle PAC}\cdot\frac{\sin\angle PBC}{\sin\angle PBA}\cdot\frac{\sin\angle
  PCA}{\sin\angle PCB} = 1\Rightarrow \frac{\sin x}{\sin(80^\circ - x)}\cdot\frac{\sin10^\circ}{\sin40^\circ}
  \cdot\frac{\sin20^\circ}{\sin30^\circ} = 1$

  $\Rightarrow \frac{\sin x}{\sin(80^\circ - x)} = \frac{\sin30^\circ\sin40^\circ}{\sin10^\circ\sin20^\circ} =
  \frac{2\sin20^\circ\cos20^\circ}{2\sin10^\circ\sin20^\circ} = \frac{\cos20^\circ}{\sin10^\circ} =
  \frac{\sin70^\circ}{\sin10^\circ}$

  For $0 < x < 80^\circ$, $\sin x$ increases and $\sin(80^\circ - x)$ decreases, so the left side is strictly
  increasing and the equation has exactly one root, namely $x = 70^\circ$.

  $\therefore \angle PAB = 70^\circ$ (and $\angle PAC = 10^\circ$).
  %430
\item (a) By the trigonometric form of Ceva's theorem, cevians through the vertices (inside the angles) are
  concurrent if and only if

  $\frac{\sin\angle BAP}{\sin\angle PAC}\cdot\frac{\sin\angle CBP}{\sin\angle PBA}\cdot\frac{\sin\angle
  ACP}{\sin\angle PCB} = 1$

  Reflecting $AP$ in the bisector of $A$ interchanges the two angles into which it divides $\angle A$, and
  similarly at $B$ and $C$. So for the reflected cevians each of the three ratios is replaced by its
  reciprocal, and the product is again $1$. Hence the reflected cevians are concurrent.

  (b) Let $AM$ be the median, $\angle BAM = x, \angle MAC = y$. Since $\triangle ABM$ and $\triangle AMC$ have
  equal bases and the same height, $1 = \frac{[ABM]}{[AMC]} = \frac{\frac{1}{2}c\cdot AM\sin
  x}{\frac{1}{2}b\cdot AM\sin y}$, so $\frac{\sin x}{\sin y} = \frac{b}{c}$.

  For the symmedian $AK$, $\angle BAK = y, \angle KAC = x$, so

  $\frac{BK}{KC} = \frac{[ABK]}{[AKC]} = \frac{c\sin y}{b\sin x} = \frac{c}{b}\cdot\frac{c}{b} =
  \frac{c^2}{b^2}$

  (c) The medians are concurrent, so by (a) the symmedians are concurrent at a point $L$. As $L$ lies on $AK$,

  $\frac{{\rm dist}(L, AB)}{{\rm dist}(L, AC)} = \frac{AL\sin y}{AL\sin x} = \frac{c}{b}$

  Similarly $\frac{{\rm dist}(L, BC)}{{\rm dist}(L, BA)} = \frac{a}{c}$. Hence the distances of $L$ from $BC,
  CA, AB$ are in the ratio $a:b:c$.
  %431
\item Let $x = \frac{B}{2}, y = \frac{C}{2}$ and let the bisectors be $BE$ and $CF$.

  In $\triangle BEC, \angle EBC = x, \angle BCE = 2y$, so $\angle BEC = 180^\circ - (x + 2y)$ and $BE =
  \frac{a\sin2y}{\sin(x + 2y)}$. Similarly $CF = \frac{a\sin2x}{\sin(2x + y)}$.

  $BE = CF\Rightarrow \sin2y\sin(2x + y) - \sin2x\sin(x + 2y) = 0$

  Converting products into sums,

  $\sin2y\sin(2x + y) - \sin2x\sin(x + 2y) = \frac{1}{2}[\cos(2x - y) - \cos(x - 2y)] + \frac{1}{2}[\cos(3x +
  2y) - \cos(2x + 3y)]$

  Put $u = \frac{x - y}{2}, t = \frac{x + y}{2}$. Using $\cos P - \cos Q = -2\sin\frac{P + Q}{2}\sin\frac{P -
  Q}{2}$,

  $= -\sin3u\sin t - \sin5t\sin u = -\sin u\left[(3 - 4\sin^2u)\sin t + \sin5t\right]$

  Now $t = \frac{B + C}{4} < 45^\circ$ and $|u| < t$, so $\sin^2u\leq\sin^2t$ and

  $(3 - 4\sin^2u)\sin t + \sin5t\geq(3 - 4\sin^2t)\sin t + \sin5t = \sin3t + \sin5t = 2\sin4t\cos t > 0$,

  because $0 < 4t < 180^\circ$.

  Hence $\sin u = 0$, and since $|u| < 45^\circ$, $u = 0$, i.e.\ $B = C$ and $b = c$.
  %432
\item We use $r = 4R\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}$ and $AI = \frac{r}{\sin\frac{A}{2}}$.

  (a) $AI\perp OI\iff OA^2 = AI^2 + OI^2\iff R^2 = \frac{r^2}{\sin^2\frac{A}{2}} + R^2 - 2Rr$ (Euler)

  $\iff r = 2R\sin^2\frac{A}{2}\iff 2\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2} = \sin\frac{A}{2} = \cos\frac{B + C}{2}$

  $\iff \cos\frac{B - C}{2} - \cos\frac{B + C}{2} = \cos\frac{B + C}{2}\iff \cos\frac{B - C}{2} =
  2\cos\frac{B + C}{2}$

  Multiplying by $2\sin\frac{B + C}{2}\ (> 0)$: $\iff \sin B + \sin C = 2\sin(B + C) = 2\sin A\iff b + c = 2a$.

  (b) $\triangle OBC$ is isosceles with $\angle BOC = 2A$ (or $360^\circ - 2A$), so the signed distance of $O$
  from $BC$, taken positive on the side of $A$, is $R\cos A$. The distance of $I$ from $BC$ is $r$, and $I$
  is always on the side of $A$.

  So $OI\parallel BC\iff R\cos A = r$.

  Now $\cos A + \cos B = 2\sin\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{A - B}{2}$ and $\cos C = 1 - 2\sin\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{A
  + B}{2}$, so

  $\cos A + \cos B + \cos C = 1 + 2\sin\frac{C}{2}\left[\cos\frac{A - B}{2} - \cos\frac{A + B}{2}\right] = 1 +
  4\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2} = 1 + \frac{r}{R}$

  $\therefore R\cos A = r\iff \cos A = \cos A + \cos B + \cos C - 1\iff \cos B + \cos C = 1$.
  %433
\item (a) $I_2$ and $I_3$ lie on the external bisector of $A$, on opposite sides of $A$. The distance of $I_3$
  from $AB$ is $r_3$ and the external bisector makes an angle $90^\circ - \frac{A}{2}$ with $AB$, so

  $AI_3 = \frac{r_3}{\cos\frac{A}{2}} = \frac{4R\cos\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}}{\cos\frac{A}{2}}
  = 4R\cos\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}$

  Similarly $AI_2 = 4R\sin\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow I_2I_3 = 4R\sin\frac{B + C}{2} = 4R\cos\frac{A}{2}$

  (b) $B$ lies on $I_1I_3$ and $C$ on $I_1I_2$ (external bisectors). In $\triangle BI_1C, \angle I_1BC =
  90^\circ - \frac{B}{2}, \angle I_1CB = 90^\circ - \frac{C}{2}$, so $\angle I_2I_1I_3 = \angle BI_1C =
  90^\circ - \frac{A}{2}$.

  Circumradius of $\triangle I_1I_2I_3 = \frac{I_2I_3}{2\sin\left(90^\circ - \frac{A}{2}\right)} =
  \frac{4R\cos\frac{A}{2}}{2\cos\frac{A}{2}} = 2R$

  Since $\sin A + \sin B + \sin C = 4\cos\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}$, we have $s = R(\sin A +
  \sin B + \sin C) = 4R\cos\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}$.

  Area $= \frac{I_2I_3\cdot I_3I_1\cdot I_1I_2}{4\cdot2R} = \frac{64R^3\cos\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}
  \cos\frac{C}{2}}{8R} = 2R\cdot4R\cos\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2} = 2Rs$

  (c) $AI\cdot BI\cdot CI = \frac{r^3}{\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}} =
  \frac{r^3\cdot4R}{r} = 4Rr^2$.

  From problem 404, $AI = 4R\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}$ and $AI_1 = 4R\cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}$, and
  $I$ lies between $A$ and $I_1$.

  $\Rightarrow II_1 = 4R\cos\frac{B + C}{2} = 4R\sin\frac{A}{2}$

  $\Rightarrow II_1\cdot II_2\cdot II_3 = 64R^3\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2} = 16R^2r$

  (d) $M$ lies on the bisector $AI$ and $MB = MC = 2R\sin\frac{A}{2}$ (chords subtending $\frac{A}{2}$ at $A$).

  $\angle MBI = \angle MBC + \angle CBI = \angle MAC + \frac{B}{2} = \frac{A + B}{2}$ and $\angle MIB$, an
  exterior angle of $\triangle ABI$, is $\frac{A}{2} + \frac{B}{2}$. Hence $MI = MB = 2R\sin\frac{A}{2}$.

  Since $MI = 2R\sin\frac{A}{2} < II_1$, $M$ lies between $I$ and $I_1$ and $MI_1 = 4R\sin\frac{A}{2} -
  2R\sin\frac{A}{2} = 2R\sin\frac{A}{2}$.

  $\therefore MB = MC = MI = MI_1$.
  %434
\item (a) Proved in problem 404: $r_1 + r_2 + r_3 - r = 4R$.

  (b) $r_1r_2 + r_2r_3 + r_3r_1 = \Delta^2\frac{(s - c) + (s - a) + (s - b)}{(s - a)(s - b)(s - c)} =
  \frac{\Delta^2 s}{\Delta^2/s} = s^2$

  (c) $r_1 - r = \frac{\Delta a}{s(s - a)}$ etc., so

  $(r_1 - r)(r_2 - r)(r_3 - r) = \frac{\Delta^3abc}{s^3(s - a)(s - b)(s - c)} = \frac{\Delta^3\cdot4R\Delta}{s^2
  \Delta^2} = \frac{4R\Delta^2}{s^2} = 4Rr^2$

  (d) $rr_1 = \frac{\Delta^2}{s(s - a)} = (s - b)(s - c)$, so with $x = s - a$ etc., $r(r_1 + r_2 + r_3) = xy +
  yz + zx = r^2 + 4Rr$ (problem 408).

  Squaring (a): $16R^2 = r_1^2 + r_2^2 + r_3^2 + r^2 + 2(r_1r_2 + r_2r_3 + r_3r_1) - 2r(r_1 + r_2 + r_3)$

  $\Rightarrow r^2 + r_1^2 + r_2^2 + r_3^2 = 16R^2 - 2s^2 + 2r^2 + 8Rr = 16R^2 - 2(s^2 - r^2 - 4Rr) = 16R^2 -
  (a^2 + b^2 + c^2)$, by problem 408.
  %435
\item Let the medians $BE, CF$ meet at the centroid $G$. Then $BG = \frac{2}{3}m_b, CG = \frac{2}{3}m_c$
  and $\angle BGC = 90^\circ$.

  $\Rightarrow \frac{4}{9}(m_b^2 + m_c^2) = a^2$, and $m_b^2 + m_c^2 = \frac{2a^2 + 2c^2 - b^2}{4} + \frac{2a^2
  + 2b^2 - c^2}{4} = \frac{4a^2 + b^2 + c^2}{4}$

  $\Rightarrow 4a^2 + b^2 + c^2 = 9a^2\Rightarrow b^2 + c^2 = 5a^2$

  Using $\cot A = \frac{b^2 + c^2 - a^2}{4\Delta}$ etc.,

  $\cot A = \frac{4a^2}{4\Delta} = \frac{a^2}{\Delta}, \cot B + \cot C = \frac{(c^2 + a^2 - b^2) + (a^2 + b^2 -
  c^2)}{4\Delta} = \frac{a^2}{2\Delta}$

  $\therefore \cot A = 2(\cot B + \cot C)$.

  Also $\cos A = \frac{b^2 + c^2 - a^2}{2bc} = \frac{2a^2}{bc}\geq\frac{2a^2}{(b^2 + c^2)/2} =
  \frac{4}{5}$.

  So $A$ is acute with $\cos A\geq\frac{4}{5}$, hence $\sin A\leq\frac{3}{5}$ and $\cot A\geq\frac{4/5}{3/5}
  = \frac{4}{3}$.
  %436
\item (a) Place $B$ at the origin and $C$ at $(a, 0)$ with $A$ above $BC$. If $AL = h$ is the altitude, the
  abscissa of $L$ is $h\cot B$ and $a = h\cot B + h\cot C$ (signed lengths, valid also for obtuse $B$ or $C$).

  $D = \left(\frac{h(\cot B + \cot C)}{2}, 0\right), A = (h\cot B, h)$

  $\overrightarrow{DA} = \left(\frac{h(\cot B - \cot C)}{2}, h\right)$ and $\theta$ is the angle between
  $\overrightarrow{DA}$ and $\overrightarrow{DC}$, the positive $x$-direction.

  $\Rightarrow \cot\theta = \frac{\cot B - \cot C}{2}$, i.e.\ $2\cot\theta = \cot B - \cot C$.

  (b) If $\angle BAD = 90^\circ$, then in right $\triangle ABD, AB = BD\cos B$, i.e.\ $c = \frac{a}{2}\cos
  B$.

  $\Rightarrow 2\sin C = \sin A\cos B\Rightarrow 2\sin A\cos B + 2\cos A\sin B = \sin A\cos B$

  $\Rightarrow \sin A\cos B + 2\cos A\sin B = 0\Rightarrow \tan A + 2\tan B = 0$

  ($\cos A\cos B\neq 0$ here: $B$ is acute in the right triangle $ABD$, and $\cos A = 0$ would force
  $\sin A\cos B = 0$, which is impossible.)

  (c) If $B = C$ the three lines coincide, so let $B > C$. The bisector always lies between the altitude and
  the median, so going from $AB$ to $AC$ we meet the altitude $AL$, the bisector $AE$ and the median $AD$. Let
  each of the four parts be $\varphi$.

  From right $\triangle ABL, B = 90^\circ - \varphi$, and $C = 180^\circ - 4\varphi - B = 90^\circ -
  3\varphi$.

  Sine rule in $\triangle ABD$ and $\triangle ADC$: $BD = \frac{AD\sin3\varphi}{\sin B}, DC =
  \frac{AD\sin\varphi}{\sin C}$.

  $BD = DC\Rightarrow \sin3\varphi\cos3\varphi = \sin\varphi\cos\varphi\Rightarrow \sin6\varphi =
  \sin2\varphi$

  As $0 < 4\varphi < 180^\circ$, $6\varphi\neq 2\varphi$ forces $6\varphi = 180^\circ - 2\varphi$, i.e.\
  $\varphi = 22\frac{1}{2}^\circ$.

  $\therefore A = 90^\circ, B = 67\frac{1}{2}^\circ, C = 22\frac{1}{2}^\circ$.
  %437
\item By the bisector theorem $AE = \frac{bc}{a + c}$ ($E$ on $CA$) and $AF = \frac{bc}{a + b}$ ($F$ on
  $AB$).

  $[AEF] = \frac{1}{2}AE\cdot AF\sin A = \Delta\cdot\frac{AE}{b}\cdot\frac{AF}{c} = \frac{\Delta bc}{(a + b)(a +
  c)}$

  Similarly $[BFD] = \frac{\Delta ca}{(b + c)(b + a)}, [CDE] = \frac{\Delta ab}{(c + a)(c + b)}$.

  $[DEF] = \Delta\left[1 - \frac{bc(b + c) + ca(c + a) + ab(a + b)}{(a + b)(b + c)(c + a)}\right]$

  Since $(a + b)(b + c)(c + a) = bc(b + c) + ca(c + a) + ab(a + b) + 2abc$,

  $[DEF] = \frac{2abc\Delta}{(a + b)(b + c)(c + a)}$

  By the A.M.--G.M.\ inequality $(a + b)(b + c)(c + a)\geq 2\sqrt{ab}\cdot2\sqrt{bc}\cdot2\sqrt{ca} = 8abc$, so
  $[DEF]\leq\frac{\Delta}{4}$, with equality only for an equilateral triangle.
  %438
\item The perpendicular from $O$ bisects $BC$ and $\angle BOC = 2A$, so the distance of $O$ from $BC$ is
  $R\cos A$ when $A$ is acute; similarly for the other sides.
