  For an acute triangle the sum is $R(\cos A + \cos B + \cos C) = R\left(1 + \frac{r}{R}\right) = R + r$,
  using $\cos A + \cos B + \cos C = 1 + \frac{r}{R}$ (problem 432).

  As $R\geq 2r$, $R + r\leq R + \frac{R}{2} = \frac{3R}{2}$.

  If $A$ is obtuse, $O$ lies on the other side of $BC$ and its distance from $BC$ is $-R\cos A$, while the
  distances from $CA, AB$ are still $R\cos B, R\cos C$. Thus the identity becomes

  ${\rm dist}(O, CA) + {\rm dist}(O, AB) - {\rm dist}(O, BC) = R + r$.
  %439
\item Let $N = 2n + 1$ and let $P$ make the central angle $2\varphi$ with $A_1$, where $0 < 2\varphi <
  \frac{2\pi}{N}$. Measuring from $P$ through $A_1, A_2, \ldots$, the arc $PA_k$ subtends $2\varphi + (k -
  1)\frac{2\pi}{N} < 2\pi$ at the center, so

  $PA_k = 2R\sin\left(\varphi + \frac{(k - 1)\pi}{N}\right)$

  We must prove $S = \displaystyle\sum_{j = 0}^{N - 1}(-1)^j\sin(\varphi + 2jh) = 0$, where $h =
  \frac{\pi}{2N}$.

  Let $T_m = \sin(\varphi + mh)$. Since $2\cos h\sin(\varphi + 2jh) = T_{2j + 1} + T_{2j - 1}$,

  $2S\cos h = \displaystyle\sum_{j = 0}^{N - 1}(-1)^j(T_{2j + 1} + T_{2j - 1})$

  The term $(-1)^jT_{2j + 1}$ cancels with the term $(-1)^{j + 1}T_{2j + 1}$ coming from $j + 1$, leaving only
  $T_{-1}$ (from $j = 0$) and $(-1)^{N - 1}T_{2N - 1} = T_{2N - 1}$ (as $N - 1$ is even).

  $\Rightarrow 2S\cos h = \sin(\varphi - h) + \sin(\varphi + \pi - h) = \sin(\varphi - h) - \sin(\varphi - h) =
  0$

  As $\cos h\neq 0, S = 0$, i.e.\ $PA_1 + PA_3 + \cdots + PA_{2n + 1} = PA_2 + PA_4 + \cdots + PA_{2n}$.
  %440
\item Since the triangle is acute, $O$ and $H$ lie inside it, on the same side of $BC$ as $A$, and so does
  $I$.

  $\angle BOC = 2A = 120^\circ, \angle BIC = 90^\circ + \frac{A}{2} = 120^\circ$ and $\angle BHC = 180^\circ - A
  = 120^\circ$.

  So $O, I, H$ all see $BC$ under $120^\circ$ from the same side; hence $B, C, O, I, H$ lie on one circle.

  In this circle the chords $OI$ and $IH$ subtend $\angle OBI$ and $\angle IBH$ at $B$.

  $\angle OBC = 90^\circ - A = 30^\circ$, $\angle IBC = \frac{B}{2}$ and $\angle HBC = 90^\circ - C = 90^\circ -
  (120^\circ - B) = B - 30^\circ$.

  $\Rightarrow \angle OBI = \left|\frac{B}{2} - 30^\circ\right|, \angle IBH = \left|B - 30^\circ -
  \frac{B}{2}\right| = \left|\frac{B}{2} - 30^\circ\right|$

  Equal angles at the circumference stand on equal chords, so $OI = IH$.
  %441
\item Take $B = (0, 0), C = (a, 0)$ and $A = (c\cos B, c\sin B)$ above $BC$.

  $O = \left(\frac{a}{2}, R\cos A\right)$ (signed distance of $O$ from $BC$, as in problem 438).

  $H$ lies on the altitude $x = c\cos B$ and on the altitude from $B$, which is perpendicular to $\overrightarrow{CA}
  = (-b\cos C, b\sin C)$, i.e.\ the line $y = x\cot C$. So $H = (c\cos B, c\cos B\cot C) = (c\cos B, 2R\cos B\cos
  C)$.

  $x_H - x_O = c\cos B - \frac{c\cos B + b\cos C}{2} = \frac{c\cos B - b\cos C}{2} = R(\sin C\cos B - \cos C\sin
  B) = R\sin(C - B)$

  $y_H - y_O = R(2\cos B\cos C + \cos(B + C)) = R(3\cos B\cos C - \sin B\sin C)$

  $\tan\varphi = \frac{3\cos B\cos C - \sin B\sin C}{\sin(C - B)}$, and dividing numerator and denominator by
  $\cos B\cos C\ (\neq 0)$,

  $\tan\varphi = \frac{3 - \tan B\tan C}{\tan C - \tan B}$

  ($\tan C\neq\tan B$ as the triangle is scalene.) The Euler line is parallel to $BC$ iff $\tan\varphi = 0$ iff
  $\tan B\tan C = 3$.
  %442
\item Let the bisector from $A$ meet $BC$ at $D$, the median from $B$ meet $CA$ at its midpoint $E$, and the
  altitude from $C$ meet $AB$ at $F$. Since $A$ and $B$ are acute, $F$ lies between $A$ and $B$, with $AF =
  b\cos A, FB = a\cos B$.

  $\frac{BD}{DC} = \frac{c}{b}, \frac{CE}{EA} = 1, \frac{AF}{FB} = \frac{b\cos A}{a\cos B}$

  By Ceva's theorem and its converse, the three cevians are concurrent iff

  $\frac{c}{b}\cdot1\cdot\frac{b\cos A}{a\cos B} = 1\iff c\cos A = a\cos B\iff \sin C\cos A = \sin A\cos B$

  Dividing by $\cos A\cos B > 0$: $\iff \tan A = \frac{\sin C}{\cos B}$.

  (For example, $A = 50^\circ$ gives $B\approx 33.5^\circ, C\approx 96.5^\circ$, and the three lines do meet.)
  %443
\item The chord joining two vertices $k$ steps apart is $2R\sin\frac{k\pi}{9}$, so $a = 2R\sin20^\circ, d_2 =
  2R\sin40^\circ, d_3 = 2R\sin60^\circ, d_4 = 2R\sin80^\circ$.

  $d_4 - d_2 = 2R(\sin80^\circ - \sin40^\circ) = 2R\cdot2\cos60^\circ\sin20^\circ = 2R\sin20^\circ = a$

  Using $\sin x\sin(60^\circ - x)\sin(60^\circ + x) = \frac{1}{4}\sin3x$ with $x = 20^\circ$,
  $\sin20^\circ\sin40^\circ\sin80^\circ = \frac{1}{4}\sin60^\circ$.

  $\Rightarrow ad_2d_4 = 8R^3\cdot\frac{1}{4}\sin60^\circ = R^2\cdot2R\sin60^\circ = R^2d_3$.
  %444
\item (a) $AB = 2R\sin\angle ACB, CD = 2R\sin\angle CBD$. In $\triangle PBC$ the angle at $P$ is $90^\circ$,
  so $\angle ACB + \angle DBC = \angle PCB + \angle PBC = 90^\circ$.

  $\Rightarrow AB^2 + CD^2 = 4R^2(\sin^2\angle ACB + \cos^2\angle ACB) = 4R^2$

  (b) $\angle AOB = 2\angle ACB$ with $\angle ACB$ acute, so the distance of $O$ from $AB$ is $R\cos\angle ACB
  = R\sin\angle CBD = \frac{CD}{2}$.

  (c) Let the line through $P$ perpendicular to $BC$ meet $BC$ at $L$ and $AD$ at $M$. Let $\angle PCB =
  \gamma$; then $\angle PBC = 90^\circ - \gamma$ and $\angle LPC = 90^\circ - \gamma$.

  $\angle MPA = \angle LPC = 90^\circ - \gamma$ (vertically opposite), and $\angle MAP = \angle DAC = \angle DBC
  = 90^\circ - \gamma$ (same arc $DC$). So $MA = MP$.

  $\angle MPD = 90^\circ - \angle MPA = \gamma$ and $\angle MDP = \angle ADB = \angle ACB = \gamma$ (same arc
  $AB$). So $MD = MP$.

  $\therefore MA = MD$, i.e.\ $M$ is the midpoint of $AD$.
  %445
\item Equal tangents from the vertices to the incircle give $a + c = b + d$, so $s = a + c = b + d$ and $s - a
  = c, s - b = d, s - c = a, s - d = b$.

  By Brahmagupta's formula (problem 420) the area is $K = \sqrt{(s - a)(s - b)(s - c)(s - d)} =
  \sqrt{abcd}$.

  For a polygon with an incircle, $K = rs$, so $r = \frac{\sqrt{abcd}}{s} = \frac{2\sqrt{abcd}}{a + b + c +
  d}$.

  From problem 421, $\cos A = \frac{a^2 + d^2 - b^2 - c^2}{2(ad + bc)}$.

  $\tan^2\frac{A}{2} = \frac{1 - \cos A}{1 + \cos A} = \frac{(b + c)^2 - (a - d)^2}{(a + d)^2 - (b - c)^2} =
  \frac{(2s - 2a)(2s - 2d)}{(2s - 2c)(2s - 2b)} = \frac{(s - a)(s - d)}{(s - b)(s - c)}$

  $= \frac{cb}{da} = \frac{bc}{ad}$.
  %446
\item Let $OT = h$ be the tower. In $\triangle OPT$, $\angle TOP = 90^\circ - \theta$ (between the vertical
  and the slope), $\angle OPT = \alpha$, so $\angle OTP = 90^\circ + \theta - \alpha$.

  $\frac{h}{\sin\alpha} = \frac{a}{\sin(90^\circ + \theta - \alpha)} = \frac{a}{\cos(\alpha - \theta)}\Rightarrow
  h(\cos\alpha\cos\theta + \sin\alpha\sin\theta) = a\sin\alpha$

  $\Rightarrow h\cos\theta(\cot\alpha + \tan\theta) = a$

  Similarly from $Q$: $h\cos\theta(\cot\beta + \tan\theta) = b$.

  Subtracting: $h\cos\theta(\cot\alpha - \cot\beta) = a - b\Rightarrow h = \frac{(a - b)\sec\theta}{\cot\alpha -
  \cot\beta}$.

  Dividing: $b(\cot\alpha + \tan\theta) = a(\cot\beta + \tan\theta)\Rightarrow \tan\theta = \frac{b\cot\alpha -
  a\cot\beta}{a - b}$.
  %447
\item Let $T$ be the top of the hill and $P$ the point reached. $AT$ makes $\alpha$ and $AP$ makes $\gamma$ with
  the horizontal, so $\angle TAP = \alpha - \gamma$.

  At $P$ the direction $\overrightarrow{PA}$ points downward at $\gamma$ below the horizontal (backwards) and
  $\overrightarrow{PT}$ points upward at $\beta$ (forwards), so $\angle APT = 180^\circ - (\beta - \gamma)$.

  $\Rightarrow \angle ATP = 180^\circ - (\alpha - \gamma) - 180^\circ + (\beta - \gamma) = \beta - \alpha$

  By the sine rule $AT = \frac{d\sin(\beta - \gamma)}{\sin(\beta - \alpha)}$.

  $\therefore$ height $= AT\sin\alpha = \frac{d\sin\alpha\sin(\beta - \gamma)}{\sin(\beta - \alpha)}$.
  %448
\item Let $k = H(H + h)$. At distance $x$ the top and bottom of the statue have elevations $\theta_2,
  \theta_1$ with $\tan\theta_2 = \frac{H + h}{x}, \tan\theta_1 = \frac{H}{x}$.

  $\tan(\theta_2 - \theta_1) = \frac{\frac{h}{x}}{1 + \frac{H(H + h)}{x^2}} = \frac{hx}{x^2 + k}$

  Equal angles at $x_1\neq x_2$ (all angles here are acute):

  $\frac{x_1}{x_1^2 + k} = \frac{x_2}{x_2^2 + k}\Rightarrow x_1x_2^2 + kx_1 = x_1^2x_2 + kx_2\Rightarrow (x_1 -
  x_2)(k - x_1x_2) = 0$

  $\therefore x_1x_2 = k = H(H + h)$.

  Also $\frac{hx}{x^2 + k} = \frac{h}{x + \frac{k}{x}}\leq\frac{h}{2\sqrt{k}}$, with equality at $x = \sqrt{k}$.
  So the greatest angle satisfies $\tan\theta = \frac{h}{2\sqrt{H(H + h)}}$.

  $\Rightarrow \sin\theta = \frac{h}{\sqrt{h^2 + 4H(H + h)}} = \frac{h}{\sqrt{(2H + h)^2}} = \frac{h}{2H + h}$.
  %449
\item Let the cloud be at height $x$ above the lake and at horizontal distance $l$ from the observer. Its
  reflection is at depth $x$ below the surface.

  $\tan\alpha = \frac{x - h}{l}, \tan\beta = \frac{x + h}{l}$

  $\Rightarrow \frac{x + h}{x - h} = \frac{\tan\beta}{\tan\alpha}\Rightarrow x = h\frac{\tan\beta +
  \tan\alpha}{\tan\beta - \tan\alpha} = \frac{h\sin(\alpha + \beta)}{\sin(\beta - \alpha)}$

  Also $l(\tan\beta - \tan\alpha) = 2h\Rightarrow l = \frac{2h\cos\alpha\cos\beta}{\sin(\beta - \alpha)}$.

  Distance of the cloud from the observer $= l\sec\alpha = \frac{2h\cos\beta}{\sin(\beta - \alpha)}$.
  %450
\item Let $G$ be the center of the triangle, $\rho = GA = \frac{a}{\sqrt{3}}$, let the foot $Q$ of the pole be
  at distance $\delta$ from $G$ and let $\angle QGA = \psi$, so that $GQ$ makes angles $\psi, \psi\mp120^\circ$
  with $GA, GB, GC$.

  By the cosine rule, with $u = \delta^2 + \rho^2, v = 2\delta\rho$,

  $QA^2 = u - v\cos\psi, QB^2 = u - v\cos(\psi - 120^\circ), QC^2 = u - v\cos(\psi + 120^\circ)$

  $\cos(\psi - 120^\circ) + \cos(\psi + 120^\circ) = 2\cos\psi\cos120^\circ = -\cos\psi$, so $\sum\cos = 0$; and
  $\sum\cos^2 = \frac{3}{2} + \frac{1}{2}\sum\cos(2\psi + 240^\circ k) = \frac{3}{2}$ by the same fact.

  $\Rightarrow QA^2 + QB^2 + QC^2 = 3u, QA^4 + QB^4 + QC^4 = 3u^2 + \frac{3}{2}v^2$

  $3\left(\sum QA^4 + a^4\right) - \left(\sum QA^2 + a^2\right)^2 = 9u^2 + \frac{9}{2}v^2 + 3a^4 - 9u^2 - 6ua^2 -
  a^4 = \frac{9}{2}v^2 + 2a^4 - 6ua^2$

  With $\rho^2 = \frac{a^2}{3}$: $\frac{9}{2}v^2 = 18\delta^2\rho^2 = 6\delta^2a^2$ and $6ua^2 = 6\delta^2a^2 +
  2a^4$. So the expression is $0$.

  Since $QA = h\cot\alpha, QB = h\cot\beta, QC = h\cot\gamma$,

  $3\left[h^4\left(\cot^4\alpha + \cot^4\beta + \cot^4\gamma\right) + a^4\right] = \left[h^2\left(\cot^2\alpha +
  \cot^2\beta + \cot^2\gamma\right) + a^2\right]^2$.
  %451
\item After $t$ minutes the balloon is at height $vt$ and at horizontal distance $d + ut$ from the observer, so
  if $\theta_t$ is its elevation,

  $\cot\theta_t = \frac{d + ut}{vt}\Rightarrow t\cot\theta_t = \frac{d}{v} + \frac{u}{v}t$

  Thus $\cot\alpha, 2\cot\beta, 3\cot\gamma$ are the values at $t = 1, 2, 3$ of a linear function of $t$,
  hence in A.P.\ with common difference $\frac{u}{v}$.

  $\Rightarrow \frac{u}{v} = 2\cot\beta - \cot\alpha$

  Also $\cot\theta_t = \frac{u}{v} + \frac{d}{vt}$ decreases as $t$ increases and is always greater than
  $\frac{u}{v}$. So $\theta_t$ increases but always stays below $\cot^{-1}\frac{u}{v} = \cot^{-1}(2\cot\beta -
  \cot\alpha)$.
  %452
\item Let $h$ be the height, $O$ the foot and $P$ the second position. Then $AO = h\cot30^\circ = \sqrt{3}h,
  PO = h\cot45^\circ = h$ and $\angle OAP = 30^\circ$.

  Cosine rule in $\triangle AOP$:

  $h^2 = 3h^2 + 100 - 2\cdot10\cdot\sqrt{3}h\cos30^\circ = 3h^2 + 100 - 30h$

  $\Rightarrow h^2 - 15h + 50 = 0\Rightarrow h = 5$ or $h = 10$

  Both are admissible: for $h = 10, AO = 10\sqrt{3}, PO = 10$; for $h = 5, AO = 5\sqrt{3}, PO = 5$, and in each
  case the cosine rule is satisfied. Hence the height is $5$ m or $10$ m.
  %453
\item Let the tangents at $B, C$ meet at $T_A$, those at $C, A$ at $T_B$ and those at $A, B$ at $T_C$. As
  the triangle is acute, $\angle BOC = 2A < 180^\circ$ and $OT_A$ bisects it, so $T_AB = T_AC = R\tan A$;
  similarly $T_BC = T_BA = R\tan B, T_CA = T_CB = R\tan C$.

  $A$ lies between $T_B$ and $T_C$, so $T_BT_C = R(\tan B + \tan C)$, and similarly for the other sides.

  The circumcircle touches all three sides and lies inside the tangential triangle, so it is its incircle.
  Semi-perimeter $= R(\tan A + \tan B + \tan C)$, hence

  Area $= R\cdot R(\tan A + \tan B + \tan C) = R^2\tan A\tan B\tan C$,

  since $\tan A + \tan B + \tan C = \tan A\tan B\tan C$ when $A + B + C = \pi$.

  The angle at $T_A$ is $180^\circ - \angle BOC = 180^\circ - 2A$. Its circumradius is

  $\frac{T_BT_C}{2\sin2A} = \frac{R\sin A}{\cos B\cos C}\cdot\frac{1}{4\sin A\cos A} = \frac{R}{4\cos A\cos
  B\cos C}$, using $\tan B + \tan C = \frac{\sin(B + C)}{\cos B\cos C} = \frac{\sin A}{\cos B\cos C}$.

  Finally let $P = \tan A\tan B\tan C = \tan A + \tan B + \tan C$, all terms positive. By A.M.--G.M.,
  $P\geq 3P^{1/3}\Rightarrow P^{2/3}\geq 3\Rightarrow P\geq 3\sqrt{3}$. Hence area $\geq 3\sqrt{3}R^2$, with
  equality for an equilateral triangle.
  %454
\item Since $A + B + C = \pi$, we have $\cos A = -\cos(B + C) = \sin B\sin C - \cos B\cos C$.

  Consider $E = (x - z\cos B - y\cos C)^2 + (z\sin B - y\sin C)^2\geq 0$.

  Expanding, $E = x^2 + z^2\cos^2B + y^2\cos^2C - 2zx\cos B - 2xy\cos C + 2yz\cos B\cos C + z^2\sin^2B +
  y^2\sin^2C - 2yz\sin B\sin C$

  $= x^2 + y^2 + z^2 - 2zx\cos B - 2xy\cos C - 2yz(\sin B\sin C - \cos B\cos C)$

  $= x^2 + y^2 + z^2 - 2yz\cos A - 2zx\cos B - 2xy\cos C$.

  $\therefore x^2 + y^2 + z^2 - 2yz\cos A - 2zx\cos B - 2xy\cos C = E\geq 0$, which is the required
  inequality.

  Equality holds iff $z\sin B = y\sin C$ and $x = z\cos B + y\cos C$. The first condition means $y = k\sin
  B, z = k\sin C$ for some real $k$, and then the second gives $x = k(\sin C\cos B + \cos C\sin B) = k\sin(B
  + C) = k\sin A$. Conversely $x:y:z = \sin A:\sin B:\sin C$ makes both brackets vanish. Thus equality holds
  iff $x:y:z = \sin A:\sin B:\sin C$.

  Putting $x = y = z = 1$ gives $3\geq 2(\cos A + \cos B + \cos C)\Rightarrow\cos A + \cos B + \cos
  C\leq\frac{3}{2}$, with equality iff $\sin A = \sin B = \sin C$, i.e.\ for the equilateral triangle.

  For positive $p, q, r$ choose $x = \sqrt{\frac{qr}{p}}, y = \sqrt{\frac{rp}{q}}, z = \sqrt{\frac{pq}{r}}$,
  so that $yz = p, zx = q, xy = r$ and $x^2 = \frac{qr}{p}, y^2 = \frac{rp}{q}, z^2 = \frac{pq}{r}$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{qr}{p} + \frac{rp}{q} + \frac{pq}{r}\geq 2p\cos A + 2q\cos B + 2r\cos C$, which is the
  last result.
  %455
\item $\cos A + \cos B = 2\cos\frac{A + B}{2}\cos\frac{A - B}{2} = 2\sin\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{A - B}{2}$.

  Since $0 < \frac{C}{2} < \frac{\pi}{2}$, $\sin\frac{C}{2} > 0$, and $\cos\frac{A - B}{2}\leq 1$
  $\Rightarrow\cos A + \cos B\leq 2\sin\frac{C}{2}$.

  Similarly $\cos B + \cos C\leq 2\sin\frac{A}{2}$ and $\cos C + \cos A\leq 2\sin\frac{B}{2}$.

  Adding, $2(\cos A + \cos B + \cos C)\leq 2\left(\sin\frac{A}{2} + \sin\frac{B}{2}
  + \sin\frac{C}{2}\right)$, which is the first inequality. Equality needs $\cos\frac{A - B}{2} = \cos\frac{B
    - C}{2} = \cos\frac{C - A}{2} = 1$, i.e.\ $A = B = C$.

  For the second inequality put $A' = \frac{\pi - A}{2}, B' = \frac{\pi - B}{2}, C' = \frac{\pi -
    C}{2}$. Each lies in $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$ and $A' + B' + C' = \frac{3\pi - \pi}{2} = \pi$, so
  $A', B', C'$ are the angles of a triangle.

  Also $\sin\frac{A}{2} = \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - A'\right) = \cos A'$, etc.

  $\therefore\sin\frac{A}{2} + \sin\frac{B}{2} + \sin\frac{C}{2} = \cos A' + \cos B' + \cos C'\leq\frac{3}{2}$
  by the previous problem, with equality iff $A' = B' = C'$, i.e.\ iff $A = B = C$.
  %456
\item Let $u = \sin\frac{A}{2}, v = \sin\frac{B}{2}, w = \sin\frac{C}{2}$. These are positive and by the
  previous problem $u + v + w\leq\frac{3}{2}$.

  By the A.M.--G.M. inequality, $(u + v + w)\left(\frac{1}{u} + \frac{1}{v} + \frac{1}{w}\right)\geq
  3(uvw)^{1/3}\cdot\frac{3}{(uvw)^{1/3}} = 9$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{1}{u} + \frac{1}{v} + \frac{1}{w}\geq\frac{9}{u + v + w}\geq\frac{9}{3/2} = 6$, i.e.\
  ${\rm cosec}\frac{A}{2} + {\rm cosec}\frac{B}{2} + {\rm cosec}\frac{C}{2}\geq 6$.

  Again by A.M.--G.M., $uvw\leq\left(\frac{u + v + w}{3}\right)^3\leq\left(\frac{1}{2}\right)^3
  = \frac{1}{8}\Rightarrow{\rm cosec}\frac{A}{2}{\rm cosec}\frac{B}{2}{\rm cosec}\frac{C}{2}
  = \frac{1}{uvw}\geq 8$.

  Finally, for a positive integer $m$, A.M.--G.M. applied to the three numbers $u^{-m}, v^{-m}, w^{-m}$
  gives $u^{-m} + v^{-m} + w^{-m}\geq 3\left(\frac{1}{uvw}\right)^{m/3}\geq 3\cdot 8^{m/3} = 3\cdot 2^m$.

  In all three inequalities equality holds only for $u = v = w = \frac{1}{2}$, i.e.\ for the equilateral
  triangle.
  %457
\item Since $1 - \cos A = 2\sin^2\frac{A}{2}$ etc., the two forms of the inequality are the same.

  Case 1: the triangle is not acute. At most one angle is $\geq\frac{\pi}{2}$, so exactly one cosine is
  $\leq 0$ and the other two are positive. Hence $\cos A\cos B\cos C\leq 0 < (1 - \cos A)(1 - \cos B)(1 - \cos
  C)$, and the inequality holds strictly.

  Case 2: the triangle is acute. By the cosine rule, $\cos A = \frac{b^2 + c^2 - a^2}{2bc}$ and $1 - \cos A
  = \frac{a^2 - (b - c)^2}{2bc} = \frac{(a - b + c)(a + b - c)}{2bc}$, and similarly for $B$ and $C$.

  Put $\alpha = b^2 + c^2 - a^2, \beta = c^2 + a^2 - b^2, \gamma = a^2 + b^2 - c^2$ (all positive as the
  triangle is acute) and $p = b + c - a, q = c + a - b, r = a + b - c$ (all positive by the triangle
  inequality).

  Then $\cos A\cos B\cos C = \frac{\alpha\beta\gamma}{8a^2b^2c^2}$, and multiplying the three expressions
  for $1 - \cos A, 1 - \cos B, 1 - \cos C$, each of $p, q, r$ occurs twice, so $(1 - \cos A)(1 - \cos B)(1 -
  \cos C) = \frac{p^2q^2r^2}{8a^2b^2c^2}$.

  So we have to prove $\alpha\beta\gamma\leq(pqr)^2$.

  Now $\alpha\beta = \left(c^2 - (a^2 - b^2)\right)\left(c^2 + (a^2 - b^2)\right) = c^4 - (a^2 - b^2)^2$ and
  $pq = (c - a + b)(c + a - b) = c^2 - (a - b)^2$.

  $\Rightarrow p^2q^2 - \alpha\beta = c^4 - 2c^2(a - b)^2 + (a - b)^4 - c^4 + (a - b)^2(a + b)^2$

  $= (a - b)^2\left[(a - b)^2 + (a + b)^2 - 2c^2\right] = 2(a - b)^2(a^2 + b^2 - c^2) = 2(a -
  b)^2\gamma\geq 0$.

  $\therefore\alpha\beta\leq p^2q^2$, and in the same way $\beta\gamma\leq q^2r^2$ and $\gamma\alpha\leq
  r^2p^2$.

  All quantities being positive, multiplying gives $(\alpha\beta\gamma)^2\leq(pqr)^4\Rightarrow
  \alpha\beta\gamma\leq(pqr)^2$, as required.

  Equality forces $(a - b)^2\gamma = (b - c)^2\alpha = (c - a)^2\beta = 0$, i.e.\ $a = b = c$. So equality
  holds only for the equilateral triangle.
  %458
\item $\cos^2A + \cos^2B = 1 + \frac{1}{2}(\cos2A + \cos2B) = 1 + \cos(A + B)\cos(A - B) = 1 - \cos
  C\cos(A - B)$.

  Also $\cos^2C = -\cos C\cos(A + B)$ because $\cos C = -\cos(A + B)$.

  $\Rightarrow\cos^2A + \cos^2B + \cos^2C = 1 - \cos C\left[\cos(A - B) + \cos(A + B)\right] = 1 - 2\cos A\cos
  B\cos C$.

  $\therefore\sin^2A + \sin^2B + \sin^2C = 3 - (\cos^2A + \cos^2B + \cos^2C) = 2 + 2\cos A\cos B\cos C$.

  At most one angle of a triangle is $\geq\frac{\pi}{2}$. If all are acute the product of cosines is
  positive; if one is right it is $0$; if one is obtuse it is negative. Hence $\sin^2A + \sin^2B
  + \sin^2C >, =, < 2$ according as the triangle is acute, right-angled, obtuse.

  Upper bound: if the triangle is not acute, the sum is $\leq 2$. If it is acute, $\cos A\cos B
  = \frac{1}{2}[\cos(A - B) + \cos(A + B)]\leq\frac{1}{2}(1 - \cos C)$, so $\cos A\cos B\cos C\leq\frac{1}{2}\cos
  C(1 - \cos C)\leq\frac{1}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{4} = \frac{1}{8}$, since $t(1 - t)\leq\frac{1}{4}$.

  $\therefore\sin^2A + \sin^2B + \sin^2C\leq 2 + \frac{1}{4} = \frac{9}{4}$, with equality iff $A = B$ and
  $\cos C = \frac{1}{2}$, i.e.\ for the equilateral triangle. Clearly the sum is always $> 0$.

  To see which values occur, take $B = C = t, A = \pi - 2t$ with $0 < t < \frac{\pi}{2}$; then $f(t)
  = \sin^22t + 2\sin^2t$ is continuous, $f\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \frac{3}{4} + \frac{3}{2}
  = \frac{9}{4}$ and $f(t)\to 0$ as $t\to 0^+$. By the intermediate value property every value in $\left(0,
    \frac{9}{4}\right]$ is attained.

  (i) Over all triangles the set of values is $\left(0, \frac{9}{4}\right]$.

  (ii) For acute triangles the sum is $> 2$ and $\leq\frac{9}{4}$. For $\frac{\pi}{4} < t < \frac{\pi}{2}$ the
  triangle above is acute, $f(t)\to\sin^2\frac{\pi}{2} + 2\cdot\frac{1}{2} = 2$ as
  $t\to\frac{\pi}{4}^+$, and $f\left(\frac{\pi}{3}\right) = \frac{9}{4}$, so every value in $\left(2,
    \frac{9}{4}\right]$ is attained. The set is $\left(2, \frac{9}{4}\right]$.
  %459
\item Let $s = \sin\frac{A}{2}$, so $0 < s < 1$ and $\cos A = 1 - 2s^2$.

  $\cos B + \cos C = 2\cos\frac{B + C}{2}\cos\frac{B - C}{2} = 2s\cos\frac{B - C}{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow x(\cos B + \cos C)\leq|x|\cdot 2s\left|\cos\frac{B - C}{2}\right|\leq 2|x|s$.

  $\therefore\cos A + x(\cos B + \cos C)\leq 1 - 2s^2 + 2|x|s = 1 + \frac{x^2}{2} - 2\left(s
  - \frac{|x|}{2}\right)^2\leq 1 + \frac{x^2}{2}$.

  For equality we need $s = \frac{|x|}{2}$ (so $x\neq 0$) and $x\cdot 2s\cos\frac{B - C}{2} = 2|x|s$, which
  requires $x > 0$ and $B = C$.

  Hence equality holds iff $0 < x < 2$, $B = C$ and $\sin\frac{A}{2} = \frac{x}{2}$. For $x\leq 0$ or $x\geq
  2$ the inequality is strict.
  %460
\item First, $\sin2A + \sin2B = 2\sin(A + B)\cos(A - B) = 2\sin C\cos(A - B)$ and $\sin2C = 2\sin C\cos C
  = -2\sin C\cos(A + B)$.

  $\Rightarrow\sin2A + \sin2B + \sin2C = 2\sin C[\cos(A - B) - \cos(A + B)] = 4\sin A\sin B\sin C$.

  Next, $\sin A + \sin B = 2\sin\frac{A + B}{2}\cos\frac{A - B}{2} = 2\cos\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{A - B}{2}$ and
  $\sin C = 2\sin\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2} = 2\cos\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{A + B}{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow\sin A + \sin B + \sin C = 2\cos\frac{C}{2}\left[\cos\frac{A - B}{2} + \cos\frac{A +
      B}{2}\right] = 4\cos\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}$.

  Since $\sin A = 2\sin\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{A}{2}$ etc.,

  $\frac{\sin2A + \sin2B + \sin2C}{\sin A + \sin B + \sin C} = \frac{\sin A\sin B\sin
    C}{\cos\frac{A}{2}\cos\frac{B}{2}\cos\frac{C}{2}} = 8\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}$.

  By problem 456, $\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2}\leq\frac{1}{8}$, so the ratio is $\leq 1$.

  As $\sin A + \sin B + \sin C > 0$, we get $\sin2A + \sin2B + \sin2C\leq\sin A + \sin B + \sin C$, with
  equality iff $\sin\frac{A}{2}\sin\frac{B}{2}\sin\frac{C}{2} = \frac{1}{8}$, i.e.\ for the equilateral
  triangle.
  %461
\item $\sin A + \sin B = 2\sin\frac{A + B}{2}\cos\frac{A - B}{2} = 2\cos\frac{C}{2}\cos\frac{A -
    B}{2}\leq 2\cos\frac{C}{2}$, since $\cos\frac{C}{2} > 0$.

  Similarly $\sin B + \sin C\leq 2\cos\frac{A}{2}$ and $\sin C + \sin A\leq 2\cos\frac{B}{2}$.

  Adding and dividing by $2$, $\sin A + \sin B + \sin C\leq\cos\frac{A}{2} + \cos\frac{B}{2}
  + \cos\frac{C}{2}$.

  For the second inequality, by the Cauchy--Schwarz inequality $(u + v + w)^2\leq 3(u^2 + v^2 + w^2)$,

  $\left(\cos\frac{A}{2} + \cos\frac{B}{2} + \cos\frac{C}{2}\right)^2\leq 3\left(\cos^2\frac{A}{2}
  + \cos^2\frac{B}{2} + \cos^2\frac{C}{2}\right) = 3\cdot\frac{3 + \cos A + \cos B + \cos C}{2}$.

  Using $\cos A + \cos B + \cos C\leq\frac{3}{2}$ (problem 454), the right side is
  $\leq\frac{3}{2}\left(3 + \frac{3}{2}\right) = \frac{27}{4}$.

  $\therefore\cos\frac{A}{2} + \cos\frac{B}{2} + \cos\frac{C}{2}\leq\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}$.

  Equality holds throughout only for the equilateral triangle.
  %462
\item Jordan's inequality. For $x > 0$, $\frac{d}{dx}(x - \sin x) = 1 - \cos x\geq 0$, vanishing only at
  isolated points, so $x - \sin x$ is increasing and $\sin x\leq x$ on $\left[0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$.

  Next, $\frac{d}{dx}(\tan x - x) = \sec^2x - 1 = \tan^2x > 0$ on $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$, so $\tan
  x > x$ there.

  Let $\phi(x) = \frac{\sin x}{x}$ on $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right]$. Then $\phi'(x) = \frac{x\cos x - \sin
    x}{x^2} = \frac{\cos x(x - \tan x)}{x^2} < 0$ for $0 < x < \frac{\pi}{2}$.

  So $\phi$ is strictly decreasing and $\phi(x)\geq\phi\left(\frac{\pi}{2}\right) = \frac{2}{\pi}$, i.e.\
  $\sin x\geq\frac{2x}{\pi}$, with equality only at $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$ (and trivially at $x = 0$).

  Triangle. The upper bounds $\frac{3}{2}$ and $\frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}$ were proved in problems 455 and 461.

  Since $0 < \frac{A}{2} < \frac{\pi}{2}$, Jordan's inequality gives $\sin\frac{A}{2} > \frac{2}{\pi}\cdot\frac{A}{2}
  = \frac{A}{\pi}$ (strict, because $\frac{A}{2}\neq 0, \frac{\pi}{2}$).

  $\Rightarrow\sin\frac{A}{2} + \sin\frac{B}{2} + \sin\frac{C}{2} > \frac{A + B + C}{\pi} = 1$.

  Also $\cos\frac{A}{2} = \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - \frac{A}{2}\right) > \frac{2}{\pi}\left(\frac{\pi}{2}
  - \frac{A}{2}\right) = 1 - \frac{A}{\pi}$, so $\cos\frac{A}{2} + \cos\frac{B}{2} + \cos\frac{C}{2} > 3 -
  \frac{A + B + C}{\pi} = 2$.

  To see that $1$ and $2$ are best possible, take $A = \pi - 2\epsilon, B = C = \epsilon$. Then
  $\sin\frac{A}{2} + \sin\frac{B}{2} + \sin\frac{C}{2} = \cos\epsilon + 2\sin\frac{\epsilon}{2}\to 1$ and
  $\cos\frac{A}{2} + \cos\frac{B}{2} + \cos\frac{C}{2} = \sin\epsilon + 2\cos\frac{\epsilon}{2}\to 2$ as
  $\epsilon\to 0^+$.

  Hence no larger constants can be used.
  %463
\item Each angle lies in $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$, so by Jordan's inequality (problem 462) $\sin A
  > \frac{2A}{\pi}$, the inequality being strict since $A\neq 0, \frac{\pi}{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow\sin A + \sin B + \sin C > \frac{2(A + B + C)}{\pi} = 2$.

  To see that $2$ is best possible take $A = B = \frac{\pi}{2} - \epsilon, C = 2\epsilon$ with small
  $\epsilon > 0$ (an acute triangle). Then $\sin A + \sin B + \sin C = 2\cos\epsilon + \sin2\epsilon\to 2$.

  For the second inequality: since $C < \frac{\pi}{2}$, $A + B = \pi - C > \frac{\pi}{2}\Rightarrow A
  > \frac{\pi}{2} - B$.

  Both $A$ and $\frac{\pi}{2} - B$ lie in $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$, where sine is strictly
  increasing, so $\sin A > \sin\left(\frac{\pi}{2} - B\right) = \cos B$.

  Similarly $\sin B > \cos C$ and $\sin C > \cos A$. Adding, $\sin A + \sin B + \sin C > \cos A + \cos B
  + \cos C$.
  %464
\item Step 1 ($n = 2$). For $x, y\in[0, \pi]$, $\sin x + \sin y = 2\sin\frac{x + y}{2}\cos\frac{x -
    y}{2}$. Here $\frac{x + y}{2}\in[0, \pi]$, so $\sin\frac{x + y}{2}\geq 0$, and $\cos\frac{x - y}{2}\leq
  1$.

  $\Rightarrow\sin x + \sin y\leq 2\sin\frac{x + y}{2}$.

  Step 2 ($n = 2^m$). Suppose the result holds for $2^m$ numbers. Given $2^{m + 1}$ numbers in $[0, \pi]$, let
  $\mu_1$ be the mean of the first $2^m$ and $\mu_2$ the mean of the last $2^m$; both lie in $[0, \pi]$ and
  the mean of all is $\mu = \frac{\mu_1 + \mu_2}{2}$.

  By the hypothesis and Step 1, $\displaystyle\sum_{i = 1}^{2^{m + 1}}\sin x_i\leq 2^m\sin\mu_1 + 2^m\sin\mu_2\leq
  2^m\cdot 2\sin\frac{\mu_1 + \mu_2}{2} = 2^{m + 1}\sin\mu$. By induction the result holds for all powers of
  $2$.

  Step 3 (any $n$). Let $\mu = \frac{x_1 + \cdots + x_n}{n}\in[0, \pi]$ and choose $m$ with $2^m\geq n$. Apply
  Step 2 to the $2^m$ numbers $x_1, \ldots, x_n$ together with $2^m - n$ copies of $\mu$. Their mean is
  $\frac{n\mu + (2^m - n)\mu}{2^m} = \mu$.

  $\Rightarrow\sin x_1 + \cdots + \sin x_n + (2^m - n)\sin\mu\leq 2^m\sin\mu\Rightarrow\sin x_1 + \cdots + \sin
  x_n\leq n\sin\mu$.

  For a triangle, $A, B, C\in(0, \pi)$ with mean $\frac{\pi}{3}$, so $\sin A + \sin B + \sin C\leq
  3\sin\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2}$.

  By A.M.--G.M., $\sin A\sin B\sin C\leq\left(\frac{\sin A + \sin B + \sin
    C}{3}\right)^3\leq\left(\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2}\right)^3 = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{8}$.
  %465
\item We use repeatedly: if $g(0) = 0$ and $g'(x) > 0$ for $x > 0$, then $g(x) > 0$ for $x > 0$.

  (a) $u(x) = x - \sin x$: $u'(x) = 1 - \cos x\geq 0$ with equality only at isolated points, so $u$ is
  strictly increasing and $\sin x < x$ for $x > 0$.

  (b) $v(x) = \cos x - 1 + \frac{x^2}{2}$: $v'(x) = x - \sin x > 0$ by (a), so $\cos x > 1 -
  \frac{x^2}{2}$.

  (c) $w(x) = \sin x - x + \frac{x^3}{6}$: $w'(x) = \cos x - 1 + \frac{x^2}{2} = v(x) > 0$ by (b), so $\sin
  x > x - \frac{x^3}{6}$.

  (d) $h(x) = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^4}{24} - \cos x$: $h'(x) = -x + \frac{x^3}{6} + \sin x = w(x) > 0$,
  so $\cos x < 1 - \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^4}{24}$.

  (e) $k(x) = x - \frac{x^3}{6} + \frac{x^5}{120} - \sin x$: $k'(x) = 1 - \frac{x^2}{2} + \frac{x^4}{24} -
  \cos x = h(x) > 0$, so $\sin x < x - \frac{x^3}{6} + \frac{x^5}{120}$.

  All five functions vanish at $0$, so all four required inequalities hold for $x > 0$.

  From (c) and (e), for $x > 0$: $\frac{1}{6} - \frac{x^2}{120} < \frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} < \frac{1}{6}$. The
  middle expression is an even function of $x$, so the same bounds hold for $x < 0$. By the sandwich
  theorem $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{x - \sin x}{x^3} = \frac{1}{6}$.

  From (b) and (d), $\frac{1}{2} - \frac{x^2}{24} < \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2} < \frac{1}{2}$ for $x\neq 0$, so
  $\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 0}\frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2} = \frac{1}{2}$.
  %466
\item Right half (Cusa). Let $h(x) = x(2 + \cos x) - 3\sin x$, so $h(0) = 0$.

  $h'(x) = 2 + \cos x - x\sin x - 3\cos x = 2(1 - \cos x) - x\sin x = 4\sin^2\frac{x}{2} -
  2x\sin\frac{x}{2}\cos\frac{x}{2}$

  $= 2\sin\frac{x}{2}\cos\frac{x}{2}\left(2\tan\frac{x}{2} - x\right)$.

  For $0 < x < \frac{\pi}{2}$, $\sin\frac{x}{2}, \cos\frac{x}{2} > 0$ and $\tan\frac{x}{2} > \frac{x}{2}$, so
  $h'(x) > 0$.

  $\Rightarrow h(x) > 0\Rightarrow\frac{\sin x}{x} < \frac{2 + \cos x}{3}$.

  Left half. Let $f(x) = \sin x(\cos x)^{-1/3} - x$ on $\left[0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$, so $f(0) = 0$.

  $f'(x) = (\cos x)^{2/3} + \frac{1}{3}\sin^2x(\cos x)^{-4/3} - 1$.

  Put $u = (\cos x)^{2/3}\in(0, 1)$. Then $(\cos x)^{-4/3} = u^{-2}$ and $\sin^2x = 1 - \cos^2x = 1 - u^3$.

  $\Rightarrow f'(x) = u + \frac{1 - u^3}{3u^2} - 1 = \frac{2u^3 - 3u^2 + 1}{3u^2} = \frac{(u - 1)^2(2u +
    1)}{3u^2} > 0$ for $0 < x < \frac{\pi}{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow f(x) > 0\Rightarrow\sin x > x(\cos x)^{1/3}\Rightarrow\frac{\sin x}{x} > (\cos x)^{1/3}$.
  %467
\item By problem 466, $\left(\frac{\sin x}{x}\right)^3 > \cos x$ for $0 < x < \frac{\pi}{2}$.

  By A.M.--G.M., $\left(\frac{\sin x}{x}\right)^2 + \frac{\tan x}{x}\geq 2\sqrt{\frac{\sin^2x}{x^2}\cdot\frac{\sin
      x}{x\cos x}} = 2\sqrt{\frac{1}{\cos x}\left(\frac{\sin x}{x}\right)^3}$.

  Since $\left(\frac{\sin x}{x}\right)^3 > \cos x > 0$, the quantity under the root exceeds $1$.

  $\therefore\left(\frac{\sin x}{x}\right)^2 + \frac{\tan x}{x} > 2$.

  For the second inequality, squaring $\frac{\sin x}{x} > (\cos x)^{1/3}$ gives $\frac{\sin^2x}{x^2} > (\cos
  x)^{2/3}$.

  As $0 < \cos x < 1$ and $\frac{2}{3} < 1$, $(\cos x)^{2/3} > \cos x$. Hence $\sin^2x > x^2\cos x$, i.e.\
  $\sin x\tan x > x^2$.
  %468
\item $\sin(a - b) = \sin a\cos b - \cos a\sin b$

  $\Rightarrow|\sin(a - b)|\leq|\sin a||\cos b| + |\cos a||\sin b|\leq|\sin a| + |\sin b|$.

  Equality in the last step would require ($\sin a = 0$ or $|\cos b| = 1$) and ($\sin b = 0$ or $|\cos a| =
  1$).

  Take $a = k + 1, b = k$ with $k$ a positive integer. Since $\pi$ is irrational, $\sin k\neq 0$ and $\sin(k +
  1)\neq 0$, so $|\cos k|\neq 1$ and $|\cos(k + 1)|\neq 1$; hence equality cannot occur and $|\sin k| +
  |\sin(k + 1)| > \sin1$.

  Grouping the $100$ terms into the $50$ pairs $(1, 2), (3, 4), \ldots, (99, 100)$,

  $|\sin1| + |\sin2| + \cdots + |\sin100| > 50\sin1$.

  By problem 465, $\sin1 > 1 - \frac{1}{6} = \frac{5}{6}$.

  $\therefore|\sin1| + |\sin2| + \cdots + |\sin100| > \frac{250}{6} = 41\frac{2}{3} > 41$.
  %469
\item Since $|\cos k|\leq 1$, $|\cos k|\geq\cos^2k = \frac{1 + \cos2k}{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n|\cos k|\geq\frac{n}{2} + \frac{1}{2}\sum_{k = 1}^n\cos2k$.

  Now $2\sin1\cos2k = \sin(2k + 1) - \sin(2k - 1)$, so summing, $2\sin1\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\cos2k
  = \sin(2n + 1) - \sin1$.

  $\Rightarrow\left|\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\cos2k\right|\leq\frac{2}{2\sin1} = \frac{1}{\sin1}$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n|\cos k|\geq\frac{n}{2} - \frac{1}{2\sin1}$.

  By problem 465, $\sin1 > \frac{5}{6}$, so $\frac{1}{2\sin1} < \frac{3}{5}$ and the sum is $>\frac{n}{2}
  - \frac{3}{5}$.

  For $n\geq 3$, $\frac{n}{2} - \frac{3}{5} > \frac{n}{4}$ because $\frac{n}{4}\geq\frac{3}{4} >
  \frac{3}{5}$.

  For $n = 1, 2$: $|\cos1| = \cos1 > 1 - \frac{1}{2} = \frac{1}{2}$ (problem 465), so the sum is $>\frac{1}{2}\geq
  \frac{n}{4}$.

  Hence $|\cos1| + |\cos2| + \cdots + |\cos n| > \frac{n}{4}$ for every $n\geq 1$.
  %470
\item Let $S_n(x) = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\frac{\sin kx}{k}$. It is odd and $2\pi$-periodic and
  vanishes at $x = 0, \pi$, so it suffices to take $0 < x < \pi$.

  Partial sums. Let $T_j = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^j\sin kx$ ($T_0 = 0$). From $2\sin\frac{x}{2}\sin kx
  = \cos\left(k - \frac{1}{2}\right)x - \cos\left(k + \frac{1}{2}\right)x$ we get $2\sin\frac{x}{2}T_j
  = \cos\frac{x}{2} - \cos\left(j + \frac{1}{2}\right)x$, so $|T_j|\leq\frac{1}{\sin\frac{x}{2}}$.

  By Jordan's inequality (problem 462), $\sin\frac{x}{2}\geq\frac{2}{\pi}\cdot\frac{x}{2} = \frac{x}{\pi}$, so
  $|T_j|\leq M = \frac{\pi}{x}$ for all $j$.

  Let $c = \sqrt{2\pi}$ and $m = \left\lfloor\frac{c}{x}\right\rfloor\geq 0$, so $mx\leq c < (m + 1)x$.

  Head: for $k\leq m$, $|\sin kx|\leq kx$, so $\left|\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{\min(m, n)}\frac{\sin
      kx}{k}\right|\leq mx\leq c$.

  Tail: if $n > m$, by Abel's summation, $\displaystyle\sum_{k = m + 1}^n\frac{T_k - T_{k - 1}}{k}
  = \frac{T_n}{n} - \frac{T_m}{m + 1} + \sum_{k = m + 1}^{n - 1}T_k\left(\frac{1}{k} - \frac{1}{k +
      1}\right)$.

  $\Rightarrow\left|\displaystyle\sum_{k = m + 1}^n\frac{\sin kx}{k}\right|\leq M\left[\frac{1}{n}
  + \frac{1}{m + 1} + \frac{1}{m + 1} - \frac{1}{n}\right] = \frac{2M}{m + 1} = \frac{2\pi}{(m + 1)x} <
  \frac{2\pi}{c} = c$.

  $\therefore|S_n(x)|\leq c + c = 2\sqrt{2\pi}$ for all $x$ and $n$.
  %471
\item Let $S_n(x) = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\frac{\sin kx}{k}$. We use induction on $n$. For $n = 1$,
  $S_1(x) = \sin x > 0$ on $(0, \pi)$.

  Let $n\geq 2$ and assume $S_{n - 1} > 0$ on $(0, \pi)$. Suppose, for contradiction, that $S_n(x_1)\leq 0$
  for some $x_1\in(0, \pi)$.

  $S_n$ is continuous on $[0, \pi]$ with $S_n(0) = S_n(\pi) = 0$. Its minimum on $[0, \pi]$ is $\leq S_n(x_1)\leq
  0$; if the minimum is negative it is attained inside, and if it is $0$ it is attained at $x_1$. So there is
  $x_0\in(0, \pi)$ where $S_n$ has a minimum, with $S_n(x_0)\leq 0$ and $S_n'(x_0) = 0$.

  $S_n'(x) = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\cos kx$. Using $2\sin\frac{x}{2}\cos kx = \sin\left(k +
  \frac{1}{2}\right)x - \sin\left(k - \frac{1}{2}\right)x$, $S_n'(x) = \frac{\sin\left(n +
      \frac{1}{2}\right)x - \sin\frac{x}{2}}{2\sin\frac{x}{2}}$.

  $\Rightarrow\sin\left(n + \frac{1}{2}\right)x_0 = \sin\frac{x_0}{2}$. Squaring, $\cos^2\left(n +
  \frac{1}{2}\right)x_0 = \cos^2\frac{x_0}{2}$, and as $\cos\frac{x_0}{2} > 0$, $\cos\left(n +
  \frac{1}{2}\right)x_0\leq\cos\frac{x_0}{2}$.

  Now $\sin nx_0 = \sin\left[\left(n + \frac{1}{2}\right)x_0 - \frac{x_0}{2}\right] = \sin\left(n +
  \frac{1}{2}\right)x_0\cos\frac{x_0}{2} - \cos\left(n + \frac{1}{2}\right)x_0\sin\frac{x_0}{2}$

  $= \sin\frac{x_0}{2}\left[\cos\frac{x_0}{2} - \cos\left(n + \frac{1}{2}\right)x_0\right]\geq 0$.

  $\therefore S_n(x_0) = S_{n - 1}(x_0) + \frac{\sin nx_0}{n}\geq S_{n - 1}(x_0) > 0$, a contradiction.

  Hence $S_n > 0$ on $(0, \pi)$ for every $n$.
  %472
\item Let $C = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\cos x_k$ and $S = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\sin x_k$.

  $C^2 + S^2 = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n(\cos^2x_k + \sin^2x_k) + 2\sum_{i < j}(\cos x_i\cos x_j + \sin
  x_i\sin x_j) = n + 2\sum_{i < j}\cos(x_i - x_j)$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{i < j}\cos(x_i - x_j) = \frac{C^2 + S^2 - n}{2}\geq -\frac{n}{2}$.

  Equality holds iff $C = S = 0$, e.g.\ for $x_k = \frac{2k\pi}{n}$, since for $n\geq 2$ the sums of
  $\cos\frac{2k\pi}{n}$ and of $\sin\frac{2k\pi}{n}$ over $k = 1, \ldots, n$ are $0$. So the minimum value is
  $-\frac{n}{2}$.

  Next, $\sin^2\frac{x_i - x_j}{2} = \frac{1 - \cos(x_i - x_j)}{2}$, and there are $\frac{n(n - 1)}{2}$
  pairs.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{i < j}\sin^2\frac{x_i - x_j}{2} = \frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{n(n - 1)}{2} -
  \sum_{i < j}\cos(x_i - x_j)\right]\leq\frac{1}{2}\left[\frac{n(n - 1)}{2} + \frac{n}{2}\right]
  = \frac{n^2}{4}$.
  %473
\item Since $\cos(x + \pi) = -\cos x, \sin(x + \pi) = -\sin x$ and $\cos(2x + 2\pi) = \cos2x$,

  $f(x) + f(x + \pi) = 2 + 2(A\cos2x + B\sin2x)\geq 0$ for all $x$.

  Let $R = \sqrt{A^2 + B^2}$. If $R > 0$, write $A\cos2x + B\sin2x = R\cos(2x - \alpha)$ and choose $2x =
  \alpha + \pi$; then $2 - 2R\geq 0$, i.e.\ $A^2 + B^2\leq 1$.

  Next, $\cos\left(x + \frac{\pi}{2}\right) = -\sin x, \sin\left(x + \frac{\pi}{2}\right) = \cos x$ and
  $\cos(2x + \pi) = -\cos2x, \sin(2x + \pi) = -\sin2x$, so the second harmonic cancels:

  $f(x) + f\left(x + \frac{\pi}{2}\right) = 2 + a(\cos x - \sin x) + b(\sin x + \cos x) = 2 + (a + b)\cos x +
  (b - a)\sin x\geq 0$.

  The amplitude of $(a + b)\cos x + (b - a)\sin x$ is $\sqrt{(a + b)^2 + (b - a)^2} = \sqrt{2(a^2 +
    b^2)}$. Choosing $x$ where it equals minus its amplitude, $2 - \sqrt{2(a^2 + b^2)}\geq 0$, i.e.\ $a^2 +
  b^2\leq 2$.

  Sharpness: $1 + \sqrt{2}\cos x + \frac{1}{2}\cos2x = \frac{1}{2} + \sqrt{2}\cos x + \cos^2x = \left(\cos x +
  \frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\right)^2\geq 0$ has $a^2 + b^2 = 2$; and $1 + \cos2x = 2\cos^2x\geq 0$ has $A^2 + B^2 =
  1$.
  %474
\item For $p, q\geq 0$, $\tan^{-1}p - \tan^{-1}q\in\left(-\frac{\pi}{2}, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$, so
  $\tan^{-1}p - \tan^{-1}q = \tan^{-1}\frac{p - q}{1 + pq}$.

  First sum. $\frac{(n + 1) - (n - 1)}{1 + (n + 1)(n - 1)} = \frac{2}{n^2}\Rightarrow\tan^{-1}\frac{2}{n^2}
  = \tan^{-1}(n + 1) - \tan^{-1}(n - 1)$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{n = 1}^N\tan^{-1}\frac{2}{n^2} = \tan^{-1}(N + 1) + \tan^{-1}N - \tan^{-1}1 -
  \tan^{-1}0$.

  As $N\to\infty$ this tends to $\frac{\pi}{2} + \frac{\pi}{2} - \frac{\pi}{4} = \frac{3\pi}{4}$.

  Second sum. Note $n^4 - 2n^2 + 5 = (n^2 - 1)^2 + 4$. Let $u_0 = \frac{\pi}{2}$ and $u_k =
  \tan^{-1}\frac{2}{k^2}$ for $k\geq 1$.

  For $n\geq 2$, $\frac{\frac{2}{(n - 1)^2} - \frac{2}{(n + 1)^2}}{1 + \frac{4}{(n - 1)^2(n + 1)^2}}
  = \frac{2[(n + 1)^2 - (n - 1)^2]}{(n^2 - 1)^2 + 4} = \frac{8n}{n^4 - 2n^2 + 5}$, so the $n$th term is $u_{n
    - 1} - u_{n + 1}$.

  For $n = 1$ the term is $\tan^{-1}\frac{8}{4} = \tan^{-1}2 = \frac{\pi}{2} - \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{2} = u_0 -
  u_2$, so the same formula holds.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{n = 1}^N(u_{n - 1} - u_{n + 1}) = u_0 + u_1 - u_N - u_{N + 1}$.

  Since $u_N\to 0$, the sum is $u_0 + u_1 = \frac{\pi}{2} + \tan^{-1}2$.
  %475
\item $\sin3\theta = 3\sin\theta - 4\sin^3\theta = \sin\theta(3 - 4\sin^2\theta) = \sin\theta(1 +
  2\cos2\theta)$, for every $\theta$.

  Let $P_n = \displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^n\frac{1 + 2\cos\frac{2x}{3^k}}{3}$. Applying the identity with $\theta
  = \frac{x}{3^n}$, then $\frac{x}{3^{n - 1}}$, and so on,

  $3^nP_n\sin\frac{x}{3^n} = \left(1 + 2\cos\frac{2x}{3}\right)\cdots\left(1 + 2\cos\frac{2x}{3^n}\right)\sin\frac{x}{3^n}
  = \sin x$.

  For $x\neq 0$ and $n$ large, $0 < \frac{|x|}{3^n} < \pi$, so $\sin\frac{x}{3^n}\neq 0$ and

  $P_n = \frac{\sin x}{3^n\sin\frac{x}{3^n}} = \frac{\sin x}{x}\cdot\frac{x/3^n}{\sin(x/3^n)}\to\frac{\sin
    x}{x}$.

  Since $1 + 2\cos2\theta = 1 + 2(1 - 2\sin^2\theta) = 3 - 4\sin^2\theta$, the factors of the second product
  are the same as those of the first, so it has the same value $\frac{\sin x}{x}$.
  %476
\item $(2\cos\theta - 1)(2\cos\theta + 1) = 4\cos^2\theta - 1 = 2(1 + \cos2\theta) - 1 = 2\cos2\theta + 1$.

  Applying this with $\theta, 2\theta, \ldots, 2^{n - 1}\theta$ in turn,

  $(2\cos\theta + 1)(2\cos\theta - 1)(2\cos2\theta - 1)\cdots(2\cos2^{n - 1}\theta - 1) = (2\cos2\theta +
  1)(2\cos2\theta - 1)\cdots = \cdots = 2\cos2^n\theta + 1$.

  Dividing by $2\cos\theta + 1\neq 0$ gives the identity.

  Infinite product. Put $\theta = \frac{x}{2^n}$; then $2^k\theta = \frac{x}{2^{n - k}}$ and the identity
  reads $\displaystyle\prod_{j = 1}^n\left(2\cos\frac{x}{2^j} - 1\right) = \frac{2\cos x + 1}{2\cos\frac{x}{2^n}
  + 1}$, valid once $\frac{|x|}{2^n} < \frac{2\pi}{3}$ (then the denominator is positive).

  As $n\to\infty$, $2\cos\frac{x}{2^n} + 1\to 3$, so the product tends to $\frac{1 + 2\cos x}{3}$.

  Finite product. Let $\theta = \frac{2\pi}{2^n + 1}$ with $n\geq 2$. Then $\theta\leq\frac{2\pi}{5} <
  \frac{2\pi}{3}$, so $2\cos\theta + 1 > 0$.

  $2^n\theta = \frac{2^n\cdot 2\pi}{2^n + 1} = 2\pi - \theta\Rightarrow\cos2^n\theta = \cos\theta$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\prod_{k = 0}^{n - 1}(2\cos2^k\theta - 1) = \frac{2\cos\theta + 1}{2\cos\theta + 1} =
  1$, and $2^k\theta = \frac{2^{k + 1}\pi}{2^n + 1}$, which is the required result.
  %477
\item Let $\theta_k = \frac{k\pi}{n}, k = 1, 2, \ldots, n - 1$, and $t_k = \cot\theta_k$.

  $\cot\theta_k + i = \frac{\cos\theta_k + i\sin\theta_k}{\sin\theta_k}\Rightarrow(t_k + i)^n
  = \frac{\cos k\pi + i\sin k\pi}{\sin^n\theta_k} = \frac{(-1)^k}{\sin^n\theta_k}$, which is real.

  By the binomial theorem the imaginary part of $(t + i)^n$ is $P(t) = {}^nC_1t^{n - 1} - {}^nC_3t^{n - 3} +
  {}^nC_5t^{n - 5} - \cdots$, a polynomial of degree $n - 1$ with leading coefficient $n$.

  $\Rightarrow P(t_k) = 0$ for $k = 1, \ldots, n - 1$. These $t_k$ are distinct because $\cot$ is strictly
  decreasing on $(0, \pi)$, so they are all the roots of $P$.

  By Vieta's relations, $\displaystyle\sum t_k = 0$ (there is no $t^{n - 2}$ term) and
  $\displaystyle\sum_{i < j}t_it_j = -\frac{{}^nC_3}{n} = -\frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)}{6}$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}t_k^2 = \left(\sum t_k\right)^2 - 2\sum_{i < j}t_it_j
  = \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)}{3}$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}\cot^2\frac{k\pi}{n} = \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)}{3}$.

  Since ${\rm cosec}^2\theta = 1 + \cot^2\theta$, $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}{\rm
    cosec}^2\frac{k\pi}{n} = (n - 1) + \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)}{3} = \frac{(n - 1)(n + 1)}{3} = \frac{n^2 -
    1}{3}$.
  %478
\item Let $m = \left\lfloor\frac{n - 1}{2}\right\rfloor$ and $u_k = \cot^2\frac{k\pi}{n}$ for $1\leq k\leq
  m$.

  The polynomial $P(t)$ of problem 477 contains only powers $t^{n - 1 - 2j}$. If $n$ is odd, $P(t) = Q(t^2)$;
  if $n$ is even, $P(t) = tQ(t^2)$, where $Q(u) = nu^m - {}^nC_3u^{m - 1} + {}^nC_5u^{m - 2} - \cdots$ has
  degree $m$.

  Since $P\left(\cot\frac{k\pi}{n}\right) = 0$ and $\cot\frac{k\pi}{n}\neq 0$ for $k\leq m$, $Q(u_k) = 0$. The
  $u_k$ are distinct ($\cot^2$ is strictly decreasing on $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$), so they are all
  the roots of $Q$.

  Vieta: $E_1 = \displaystyle\sum u_k = \frac{{}^nC_3}{n} = \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)}{6}$ and $E_2
  = \displaystyle\sum_{i < j}u_iu_j = \frac{{}^nC_5}{n} = \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)(n - 3)(n - 4)}{120}$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^mu_k^2 = E_1^2 - 2E_2 = \frac{(n - 1)^2(n - 2)^2}{36} - \frac{(n -
    1)(n - 2)(n - 3)(n - 4)}{60}$

  $= \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)[5(n - 1)(n - 2) - 3(n - 3)(n - 4)]}{180} = \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)(n^2 + 3n -
    13)}{90}$.

  Because $\cot(\pi - \theta) = -\cot\theta$, the terms $k$ and $n - k$ of $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n -
    1}\cot^4\frac{k\pi}{n}$ are equal, and for even $n$ the term $k = \frac{n}{2}$ is $0$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}\cot^4\frac{k\pi}{n} = 2\sum_{k = 1}^mu_k^2 = \frac{(n - 1)(n -
    2)(n^2 + 3n - 13)}{45}$.

  Now ${\rm cosec}^4\theta = (1 + \cot^2\theta)^2 = 1 + 2\cot^2\theta + \cot^4\theta$. Using problem 477,

  $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}{\rm cosec}^4\frac{k\pi}{n} = (n - 1) + \frac{2(n - 1)(n - 2)}{3}
  + \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)(n^2 + 3n - 13)}{45}$

  $= \frac{n - 1}{45}\left[45 + 30(n - 2) + (n^3 + n^2 - 19n + 26)\right] = \frac{(n - 1)(n^3 + n^2 + 11n +
    11)}{45}$

  $= \frac{(n - 1)(n + 1)(n^2 + 11)}{45} = \frac{(n^2 - 1)(n^2 + 11)}{45}$.
  %479
\item The given sum follows from problem 477 with $n = 2m + 1$: there $\displaystyle\sum_{k =
    1}^{2m}\cot^2\frac{k\pi}{2m + 1} = \frac{2m(2m - 1)}{3}$ and the terms $k$ and $2m + 1 - k$ are equal.

  For $0 < \theta < \frac{\pi}{2}$, $0 < \sin\theta < \theta < \tan\theta\Rightarrow\cot^2\theta <
  \frac{1}{\theta^2} < {\rm cosec}^2\theta = 1 + \cot^2\theta$.

  Put $\theta = \frac{k\pi}{2m + 1}$, $k = 1, \ldots, m$ (all in $\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$), and add:

  $\frac{m(2m - 1)}{3} < \frac{(2m + 1)^2}{\pi^2}\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^m\frac{1}{k^2} < m + \frac{m(2m -
    1)}{3} = \frac{2m(m + 1)}{3}$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{\pi^2}{3}\cdot\frac{m(2m - 1)}{(2m + 1)^2} < \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^m\frac{1}{k^2}
  < \frac{2\pi^2}{3}\cdot\frac{m(m + 1)}{(2m + 1)^2}$.

  As $m\to\infty$ both bounds tend to $\frac{\pi^2}{3}\cdot\frac{2}{4} = \frac{\pi^2}{6}$, so
  $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^\infty\frac{1}{k^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{6}$.

  The series has positive terms, so we may separate even and odd terms: $\displaystyle\sum_{k =
    1}^\infty\frac{1}{(2k)^2} = \frac{1}{4}\cdot\frac{\pi^2}{6} = \frac{\pi^2}{24}$.

  $\Rightarrow 1 + \frac{1}{3^2} + \frac{1}{5^2} + \cdots = \frac{\pi^2}{6} - \frac{\pi^2}{24}

  For the alternating series, its partial sum up to $2N$ terms is $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{2N}\frac{1}{k^2}
  - 2\sum_{k = 1}^N\frac{1}{(2k)^2}\to\frac{\pi^2}{6} - \frac{\pi^2}{12} = \frac{\pi^2}{12}$, and the terms

  %480
\item Let $u_k = \cot^2\frac{k\pi}{2m + 1}, k = 1, \ldots, m$. As in problem 478 (with $n = 2m + 1$), they
  are the roots of $Q(u) = (2m + 1)u^m - {}^{2m + 1}C_3u^{m - 1} + {}^{2m + 1}C_5u^{m - 2} - \cdots$.

  $E_1 = \displaystyle\sum u_k = \frac{{}^{2m + 1}C_3}{2m + 1} = \frac{m(2m - 1)}{3}$ and $E_2
  = \displaystyle\sum_{i < j}u_iu_j = \frac{{}^{2m + 1}C_5}{2m + 1} = \frac{m(m - 1)(2m - 1)(2m - 3)}{30}$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^mu_k^2 = E_1^2 - 2E_2 = \frac{m^2(2m - 1)^2}{9} - \frac{m(m - 1)(2m -
    1)(2m - 3)}{15}$

  $= \frac{m(2m - 1)[5m(2m - 1) - 3(m - 1)(2m - 3)]}{45} = \frac{m(2m - 1)(4m^2 + 10m - 9)}{45}$.

  For $0 < \theta < \frac{\pi}{2}$, $\cot^2\theta < \frac{1}{\theta^2} < 1 + \cot^2\theta$, so
  $\cot^4\theta < \frac{1}{\theta^4} < 1 + 2\cot^2\theta + \cot^4\theta$.

  With $\theta = \frac{k\pi}{2m + 1}$ and summing over $k = 1, \ldots, m$ (write $\Sigma_4$ for the sum of
  $u_k^2$),

  $\Sigma_4 < \frac{(2m + 1)^4}{\pi^4}\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^m\frac{1}{k^4} < m + \frac{2m(2m - 1)}{3}
  + \Sigma_4$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{\pi^4\Sigma_4}{(2m + 1)^4} < \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^m\frac{1}{k^4}
  < \frac{\pi^4\Sigma_4}{(2m + 1)^4} + \frac{\pi^4\left(m + \frac{2m(2m - 1)}{3}\right)}{(2m + 1)^4}$.

  As $m\to\infty$, $\frac{\Sigma_4}{(2m + 1)^4} = \frac{m(2m - 1)(4m^2 + 10m - 9)}{45(2m + 1)^4}\to\frac{8}{45\cdot
    16} = \frac{1}{90}$, while the last fraction tends to $0$ (numerator of degree $2$, denominator of degree
  $4$).

  $\therefore\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^\infty\frac{1}{k^4} = \frac{\pi^4}{90}$.
  %481
\item Since $\sin x = 2\sin\frac{x}{2}\cos\frac{x}{2}$, ${\rm cosec}^2x = \frac{\sin^2\frac{x}{2}
  + \cos^2\frac{x}{2}}{4\sin^2\frac{x}{2}\cos^2\frac{x}{2}} = \frac{1}{4}\left[{\rm cosec}^2\frac{x}{2} +
  \sec^2\frac{x}{2}\right] = \frac{1}{4}\left[{\rm cosec}^2\frac{x}{2} + {\rm cosec}^2\frac{x +
    \pi}{2}\right]$.

  This is the case $n = 1$. If the formula holds for $n$, apply this identity to each term ${\rm
    cosec}^2\frac{x + k\pi}{2^n}$:

  ${\rm cosec}^2\frac{x + k\pi}{2^n} = \frac{1}{4}\left[{\rm cosec}^2\frac{x + k\pi}{2^{n + 1}} + {\rm
      cosec}^2\frac{x + (k + 2^n)\pi}{2^{n + 1}}\right]$.

  As $k$ runs over $0, \ldots, 2^n - 1$, the numbers $k$ and $k + 2^n$ run over $0, \ldots, 2^{n + 1} - 1$,
  which gives the formula for $n + 1$. By induction it holds for all $n$.

  Since ${\rm cosec}^2$ has period $\pi$, the terms with $2^{n - 1}\leq k\leq 2^n - 1$ may be replaced by
  those with $k - 2^n$, so ${\rm cosec}^2x = \frac{1}{4^n}\displaystyle\sum_{k = -2^{n - 1}}^{2^{n - 1} - 1}{\rm
    cosec}^2\frac{x + k\pi}{2^n}$.

  Both ${\rm cosec}^2x$ and $\displaystyle\sum_k\frac{1}{(x + k\pi)^2}$ have period $\pi$ (the latter by
  shifting $k$), so let $0 < x < \pi$. For the $k$ above, $-2^{n - 1}\pi < x + k\pi < 2^{n - 1}\pi$, so $t_k
  = \frac{x + k\pi}{2^n}$ satisfies $0 < |t_k| < \frac{\pi}{2}$.

  For $0 < |t| < \frac{\pi}{2}$, $|\sin t| < |t| < |\tan t|$, so $\frac{1}{t^2} < {\rm cosec}^2t = 1 +
  \cot^2t < 1 + \frac{1}{t^2}$.

  Multiplying by $\frac{1}{4^n}$ and adding over the $2^n$ values of $k$, and noting $\frac{1}{4^nt_k^2}
  = \frac{1}{(x + k\pi)^2}$,

  $S_n < {\rm cosec}^2x < S_n + \frac{2^n}{4^n}$, where $S_n = \displaystyle\sum_{k = -2^{n - 1}}^{2^{n - 1}
    - 1}\frac{1}{(x + k\pi)^2}$.

  The series $\displaystyle\sum_{k = -\infty}^\infty\frac{1}{(x + k\pi)^2}$ has positive terms of order
  $\frac{1}{k^2}$, so it converges, and $S_n$ is a sequence of its partial sums. Letting $n\to\infty$,
  $\displaystyle\sum_{k = -\infty}^{\infty}\frac{1}{(x + k\pi)^2} = {\rm cosec}^2x$.

  Put $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$: $\displaystyle\sum_k\frac{4}{\pi^2(2k + 1)^2} = 1$. The map $k\mapsto -1 - k$
  sends the negative $k$ to the non-negative ones with the same $|2k + 1|$, so $2\displaystyle\sum_{k =
    0}^\infty\frac{1}{(2k + 1)^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{4}$, i.e.\ $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^\infty\frac{1}{(2k +
    1)^2} = \frac{\pi^2}{8}$.

  Put $x = \frac{\pi}{3}$: $\frac{9}{\pi^2}\displaystyle\sum_k\frac{1}{(3k + 1)^2} = {\rm
    cosec}^2\frac{\pi}{3} = \frac{4}{3}$. As $k$ runs over all integers, $|3k + 1|$ runs exactly once over the
  positive integers $\equiv 1$ (for $k\geq 0$) and $\equiv 2$ (for $k < 0$) mod $3$, i.e.\ over all positive
  integers not divisible by $3$. Hence that sum equals $\frac{4\pi^2}{27}$.
  %482
\item Let $x$ not be a multiple of $\pi$. Then neither $\frac{x}{2}$ nor $\frac{x + \pi}{2}$ is a multiple
  of $\pi$. The series converges absolutely, so we may split it into even and odd $k$.

  Even $k = 2j$: $\displaystyle\sum_j\frac{1}{(x + 2j\pi)^2} = \frac{1}{4}\sum_j\frac{1}{\left(\frac{x}{2} +
    j\pi\right)^2} = \frac{1}{4}{\rm cosec}^2\frac{x}{2}$, by problem 481.

  Odd $k = 2j + 1$: $\displaystyle\sum_j\frac{1}{(x + (2j + 1)\pi)^2} = \frac{1}{4}\sum_j\frac{1}{\left(\frac{x
      + \pi}{2} + j\pi\right)^2} = \frac{1}{4}{\rm cosec}^2\frac{x + \pi}{2} = \frac{1}{4}\sec^2\frac{x}{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_k\frac{(-1)^k}{(x + k\pi)^2} = \frac{1}{4}\left[{\rm cosec}^2\frac{x}{2} -
  \sec^2\frac{x}{2}\right] = \frac{\cos^2\frac{x}{2} - \sin^2\frac{x}{2}}{4\sin^2\frac{x}{2}\cos^2\frac{x}{2}}
  = \frac{\cos x}{\sin^2x} = {\rm cosec}\,x\cot x$.

  Put $x = \frac{\pi}{4}$: $x + k\pi = \frac{(4k + 1)\pi}{4}$ and ${\rm cosec}\frac{\pi}{4}\cot\frac{\pi}{4} =
  \sqrt{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{16}{\pi^2}\displaystyle\sum_k\frac{(-1)^k}{(4k + 1)^2} = \sqrt{2}\Rightarrow\sum_k\frac{(-1)^k}{(4k
    + 1)^2} = \frac{\sqrt{2}\pi^2}{16} = \frac{\pi^2}{8\sqrt{2}}$.

  As $k$ runs over $0, -1, 1, -2, 2, -3, 3, -4, \ldots$, $|4k + 1|$ runs over $1, 3, 5, 7, 9, 11, 13, 15,
  \ldots$, each odd number once, with signs $(-1)^k$: $+, -, -, +, +, -, -, +, \ldots$.

  $\therefore 1 - \frac{1}{3^2} - \frac{1}{5^2} + \frac{1}{7^2} + \frac{1}{9^2} - \frac{1}{11^2} -
  \frac{1}{13^2} + \cdots = \frac{\pi^2}{8\sqrt{2}}$ (the series converges absolutely, so the order does not
  matter).
  %483
\item Finite identity. By De Moivre's theorem and the binomial theorem, $\sin N\theta = {\rm
    Im}(\cos\theta + i\sin\theta)^N = \displaystyle\sum_{j = 0}^m(-1)^j\,{}^NC_{2j + 1}\cos^{N - 2j -
    1}\theta\sin^{2j + 1}\theta$.

  Since $N - 2j - 1 = 2(m - j)$ is even, $\cos^{N - 2j - 1}\theta = (1 - \sin^2\theta)^{m - j}$. Hence
  $\sin N\theta = \sin\theta\,Q(\sin^2\theta)$, where $Q(s) = \displaystyle\sum_{j = 0}^m(-1)^j\,{}^NC_{2j +
    1}(1 - s)^{m - j}s^j$ is a polynomial of degree at most $m$ with $Q(0) = N$.

  For $k = 1, \ldots, m$, $\theta_k = \frac{k\pi}{N}\in\left(0, \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$, $\sin N\theta_k = 0$ and
  $\sin\theta_k\neq 0$, so $Q(s_k) = 0$ where $s_k = \sin^2\frac{k\pi}{N}$. These $m$ numbers are distinct, and
  $Q\not\equiv 0$, so $Q$ has degree exactly $m$ and $Q(s) = c\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^m(s - s_k)$.

  Using $Q(0) = N$, $Q(s) = N\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^m\left(1 - \frac{s}{s_k}\right)$, which gives the
  identity.

  Euler's product. Fix $x\neq 0$ (for $x = 0$ there is nothing to prove) and an integer $K\geq|x|$. Put $\theta
  = \frac{x}{N}$ with $N = 2m + 1$, $m > K$:

  $\sin x = N\sin\frac{x}{N}\cdot F_N\cdot G_N$, where $F_N = \displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^K(1 - a_k)$, $G_N
  = \displaystyle\prod_{k = K + 1}^m(1 - a_k)$ and $a_k = \frac{\sin^2(x/N)}{\sin^2(k\pi/N)}$.

  Since $\frac{k\pi}{N} < \frac{\pi}{2}$, Jordan's inequality gives $\sin\frac{k\pi}{N}\geq\frac{2k}{N}$, and
  $\left|\sin\frac{x}{N}\right|\leq\frac{|x|}{N}$. So $0\leq a_k\leq\frac{x^2}{4k^2}$, which is $\leq\frac{1}{4}$
  for $k > K$.

  If $0\leq a_k\leq 1$, then $\prod(1 - a_k)\geq 1 - \sum a_k$ (induction: $(1 - \sigma)(1 - a)\geq 1 -
  \sigma - a$ when $1 - a\geq 0, \sigma a\geq 0$). Also $\displaystyle\sum_{k > K}\frac{1}{k^2} < \sum_{k >
    K}\frac{1}{k(k - 1)} = \frac{1}{K}$.

  $\Rightarrow 1\geq G_N\geq 1 - \frac{x^2}{4}\displaystyle\sum_{k > K}\frac{1}{k^2}\geq 1 - \delta_K$, where
  $\delta_K = \frac{x^2}{4K}$, for every admissible $N$.

  Hence $\left|\sin x - N\sin\frac{x}{N}F_N\right| = \left|N\sin\frac{x}{N}F_N\right|(1 - G_N)\leq\left|N\sin\frac{x}{N}F_N\right|\delta_K$.

  Let $N\to\infty$ with $K$ fixed. $N\sin\frac{x}{N}\to x$ and $N\sin\frac{k\pi}{N}\to k\pi$, so $a_k\to\frac{x^2}{k^2\pi^2}$
  and $F_N\to\Phi_K = \displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^K\left(1 - \frac{x^2}{k^2\pi^2}\right)$.

  $\Rightarrow|\sin x - x\Phi_K|\leq|x\Phi_K|\delta_K$.

  Using $1 + y\leq e^y$, $|\Phi_K|\leq\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^K\left(1 + \frac{x^2}{k^2\pi^2}\right)\leq\exp\left(\frac{x^2}{\pi^2}\sum_{k
    = 1}^\infty\frac{1}{k^2}\right)\leq e^{2x^2/\pi^2}$, a bound independent of $K$.

  $\therefore|\sin x - x\Phi_K|\leq\frac{|x|^3e^{2x^2/\pi^2}}{4K}\to 0$ as $K\to\infty$, i.e.\ $\sin x =
  x\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^\infty\left(1 - \frac{x^2}{k^2\pi^2}\right)$.
  %484
\item $x = \frac{\pi}{2}$: $1 = \frac{\pi}{2}\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^\infty\left(1 -
  \frac{1}{4k^2}\right)\Rightarrow\prod_{k = 1}^K\frac{(2k - 1)(2k + 1)}{(2k)^2}\to\frac{2}{\pi}$.

  Taking reciprocals (the limit is non-zero), $\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^K\frac{2k\cdot 2k}{(2k - 1)(2k +
    1)}\to\frac{\pi}{2}$, i.e.\ $\frac{\pi}{2} = \frac{2\cdot 2}{1\cdot 3}\cdot\frac{4\cdot 4}{3\cdot
    5}\cdot\frac{6\cdot 6}{5\cdot 7}\cdots$, which is Wallis' product.

  $x = \frac{\pi}{3}$: $\frac{\sqrt{3}}{2} = \frac{\pi}{3}\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^\infty\left(1 -
  \frac{1}{9k^2}\right)\Rightarrow\prod_{k = 1}^\infty\left(1 - \frac{1}{9k^2}\right) = \frac{3\sqrt{3}}{2\pi}$.

  $x = \frac{\pi}{4}$: $\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}} = \frac{\pi}{4}\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^\infty\left(1 -
  \frac{1}{16k^2}\right)\Rightarrow\prod_{k = 1}^\infty\left(1 - \frac{1}{16k^2}\right) = \frac{2\sqrt{2}}{\pi}$.

  $x = \frac{\pi}{6}$: $\frac{1}{2} = \frac{\pi}{6}\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^\infty\left(1 -
  \frac{1}{36k^2}\right)\Rightarrow\prod_{k = 1}^\infty\left(1 - \frac{1}{36k^2}\right) = \frac{3}{\pi}$.
  %485
\item Let $\omega = \cos\frac{2\pi}{m} + i\sin\frac{2\pi}{m}$. For an integer $d$, $\frac{1}{m}\displaystyle\sum_{j
    = 0}^{m - 1}\omega^{jd}$ equals $1$ if $m\mid d$ and $0$ otherwise (geometric series with ratio
  $\omega^d\neq 1$ and $\omega^{dm} = 1$).

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k\equiv r}{}^nC_k = \sum_{k = 0}^n{}^nC_k\cdot\frac{1}{m}\sum_{j = 0}^{m -
    1}\omega^{j(k - r)} = \frac{1}{m}\sum_{j = 0}^{m - 1}\omega^{-jr}(1 + \omega^j)^n$.

  Now $1 + \omega^j = 1 + \cos\frac{2j\pi}{m} + i\sin\frac{2j\pi}{m} = 2\cos\frac{j\pi}{m}\left(\cos\frac{j\pi}{m}
  + i\sin\frac{j\pi}{m}\right)$.

  $\Rightarrow\omega^{-jr}(1 + \omega^j)^n = \left(2\cos\frac{j\pi}{m}\right)^n\left(\cos\frac{(n - 2r)j\pi}{m}
  + i\sin\frac{(n - 2r)j\pi}{m}\right)$.

  The left side is real, so taking real parts gives the stated formula.

  Dividing by $2^n$, $\frac{1}{2^n}\displaystyle\sum_{k\equiv r}{}^nC_k = \frac{1}{m}\left[1 + \sum_{j = 1}^{m
    - 1}\left(\cos\frac{j\pi}{m}\right)^n\cos\frac{(n - 2r)j\pi}{m}\right]$.

  For $1\leq j\leq m - 1$, $\left|\cos\frac{j\pi}{m}\right| < 1$, so each of these finitely many terms is at
  most $\left|\cos\frac{j\pi}{m}\right|^n\to 0$. Hence the limit is $\frac{1}{m}$.

  For $n = 100, m = 3, r = 0$: $3\displaystyle\sum_{3\mid k}{}^{100}C_k = 2^{100} + 1^{100}\cos\frac{100\pi}{3}
  + (-1)^{100}\cos\frac{200\pi}{3}$.

  $\cos\frac{100\pi}{3} = \cos\left(33\pi + \frac{\pi}{3}\right) = -\frac{1}{2}$ and $\cos\frac{200\pi}{3} =
  \cos\left(66\pi + \frac{2\pi}{3}\right) = -\frac{1}{2}$, so the result is $2^{100} - 1$.
  %486
\item Let $\zeta = \cos\frac{2\pi}{n} + i\sin\frac{2\pi}{n}$ and $W = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}(1 +
  \zeta^k)^n$.

  Expanding, $W = \displaystyle\sum_{j = 0}^n{}^nC_j\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}\zeta^{jk}$. The inner sum is $n$ if
  $n\mid j$ and $0$ otherwise.

  The only $j\in\{0, \ldots, n\}$ divisible by $n$ are $0$ and $n$, so $W = n({}^nC_0 + {}^nC_n) = 2n$.

  On the other hand $1 + \zeta^k = 2\cos\frac{k\pi}{n}\left(\cos\frac{k\pi}{n} + i\sin\frac{k\pi}{n}\right)$, so
  $(1 + \zeta^k)^n = 2^n\cos^n\frac{k\pi}{n}(\cos k\pi + i\sin k\pi) = (-1)^k2^n\cos^n\frac{k\pi}{n}$.

  $\Rightarrow 2^n\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}(-1)^k\cos^n\frac{k\pi}{n} = 2n\Rightarrow\sum_{k = 0}^{n -
    1}(-1)^k\cos^n\frac{k\pi}{n} = \frac{n}{2^{n - 1}}$.
  %487
\item Since $\zeta^n = 1$, $\zeta^{k^2}$ depends only on $k$ modulo $n$ (if $k'\equiv k$ then $k'^2\equiv
  k^2$ mod $n$). So in $G_n$, $k$ may run over any complete set of residues mod $n$.

  $|G_n|^2 = G_n\overline{G_n} = \displaystyle\sum_{a = 0}^{n - 1}\sum_{b = 0}^{n - 1}\zeta^{a^2 - b^2}$.

  For fixed $b$, put $a = b + h$; as $a$ runs over the residues so does $h$, and $a^2 - b^2 = h^2 + 2bh$.

  $\Rightarrow|G_n|^2 = \displaystyle\sum_{h = 0}^{n - 1}\zeta^{h^2}\sum_{b = 0}^{n - 1}\left(\zeta^{2h}\right)^b$.

  The inner geometric sum is $n$ if $\zeta^{2h} = 1$, i.e.\ $n\mid 2h$, and $0$ otherwise.

  $n$ odd: $n\mid 2h$ only for $h = 0$, so $|G_n|^2 = n$, $|G_n| = \sqrt{n}$.

  $n$ even: $h = 0$ and $h = \frac{n}{2}$ contribute, and $\zeta^{n^2/4} = \cos\frac{n\pi}{2} +
  i\sin\frac{n\pi}{2} = i^n$. So $|G_n|^2 = n(1 + i^n)$.

  If $4\mid n$, $i^n = 1$ and $|G_n| = \sqrt{2n}$; if $n\equiv 2$ (mod $4$), $i^n = -1$, so $|G_n| = 0$, i.e.\
  $G_n = 0$.

  Finally $G_n = \displaystyle\sum_k\cos\frac{2\pi k^2}{n} + i\sum_k\sin\frac{2\pi k^2}{n}$, so for odd $n$
  the sum of the squares of these two real sums is $|G_n|^2 = n$.
  %488
\item Let $x_k = \frac{k\pi}{n}, k = 0, 1, \ldots, 2n - 1$. Then $\cos nx_k = \cos k\pi = (-1)^k$.

  For an integer $j$ with $0\leq j < n$, $(-1)^k(\cos jx_k + i\sin jx_k) = \rho^k$ with $\rho = \cos\frac{(n
    + j)\pi}{n} + i\sin\frac{(n + j)\pi}{n}$.

  Since $1\leq\frac{n + j}{n} < 2$, $\rho\neq 1$, while $\rho^{2n} = 1$. So $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{2n -
    1}\rho^k = \frac{1 - \rho^{2n}}{1 - \rho} = 0$, i.e.\ $\displaystyle\sum_k(-1)^k\cos jx_k
  = \sum_k(-1)^k\sin jx_k = 0$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{2n - 1}(-1)^kT(x_k) = \sum_k(-1)^k\cos nx_k = \sum_k 1 = 2n$.

  $\Rightarrow 2n\leq\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{2n - 1}|T(x_k)|\leq 2n\max_x|T(x)|$, so $\displaystyle\max_x|T(x)|\geq 1$.

  For the polynomial, note $\cos^jx = \frac{1}{2^j}\left(e^{ix} + e^{-ix}\right)^j = \frac{1}{2^j}\displaystyle\sum_{r =
    0}^j{}^jC_r\cos(j - 2r)x$, which equals $\frac{1}{2^{j - 1}}\cos jx$ plus a combination of $\cos ix$,
  $i < j$ (the terms $r = 0$ and $r = j$ give $\frac{2}{2^j}\cos jx$).

  Hence $p(\cos x) = \cos^nx + c_{n - 1}\cos^{n - 1}x + \cdots + c_0 = \frac{1}{2^{n - 1}}\cos nx +
  \displaystyle\sum_{j < n}\alpha_j\cos jx$ for some reals $\alpha_j$.

  So $T(x) = 2^{n - 1}p(\cos x)$ has the form considered, and $\displaystyle\max_x|2^{n - 1}p(\cos x)|\geq
  1$.

  As $x$ varies, $\cos x$ covers $[-1, 1]$, so $\displaystyle\max_{-1\leq t\leq 1}|p(t)|\geq\frac{1}{2^{n -
      1}}$.
  %489
\item Let $D_m(x) = 1 + 2\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^m\cos kx$. From $2\sin\frac{x}{2}\cdot 2\cos kx = 2\left[\sin\left(k
    + \frac{1}{2}\right)x - \sin\left(k - \frac{1}{2}\right)x\right]$ we get $2\sin\frac{x}{2}D_m(x) =
  2\sin\left(m + \frac{1}{2}\right)x$.

  $\Rightarrow D_m(x) = \frac{\sin\left(m + \frac{1}{2}\right)x}{\sin\frac{x}{2}}$ for $x$ not a multiple of
  $2\pi$.

  Sum over $m = 0, 1, \ldots, n$. On the left, $\cos kx$ occurs for $m = k, k + 1, \ldots, n$, i.e.\ $n + 1 -
  k$ times:

  $\displaystyle\sum_{m = 0}^nD_m(x) = (n + 1) + 2\sum_{k = 1}^n(n + 1 - k)\cos kx$.

  On the right, $\sin\frac{x}{2}\sin\left(m + \frac{1}{2}\right)x = \frac{1}{2}[\cos mx - \cos(m + 1)x]$, which
  telescopes:

  $\displaystyle\sum_{m = 0}^n\sin\left(m + \frac{1}{2}\right)x\sin\frac{x}{2} = \frac{1 - \cos(n + 1)x}{2} =
  \sin^2\frac{(n + 1)x}{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow(n + 1) + 2\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n(n + 1 - k)\cos kx = \frac{\sin^2\frac{(n +
      1)x}{2}}{\sin^2\frac{x}{2}}$, and dividing by $2$ gives the identity.

  The right side is $\geq 0$, so $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n(n + 1 - k)\cos kx\geq -\frac{n + 1}{2}$, which
  is the stated inequality ($n\cos x + (n - 1)\cos2x + \cdots + \cos nx$). At multiples of $2\pi$ the sum is
  $\frac{n(n + 1)}{2} > -\frac{n + 1}{2}$.

  Equality holds iff $\sin\frac{(n + 1)x}{2} = 0$ with $x$ not a multiple of $2\pi$, i.e.\ $x = \frac{2j\pi}{n +
    1}$ where $j$ is an integer not divisible by $n + 1$.
  %490
\item Let $w = \cos\frac{2\pi}{n} + i\sin\frac{2\pi}{n}$; then $w\neq 1, w^n = 1$ and $\displaystyle\sum_{k =
    1}^{n - 1}w^k = -1$.

  Let $S = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}kw^k$. Then $S - wS = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}kw^k -
  \sum_{k = 2}^{n}(k - 1)w^k = \sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}w^k - (n - 1)w^n = -1 - (n - 1) = -n$.

  $\Rightarrow S = \frac{n}{w - 1}$.

  With $\alpha = \frac{2\pi}{n}$, $w - 1 = 2i\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(\cos\frac{\alpha}{2} +
  i\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)$, so $\frac{1}{w - 1} = \frac{\cos\frac{\alpha}{2} -
    i\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}}{2i\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}} = -\frac{1}{2} - \frac{i}{2}\cot\frac{\alpha}{2}$.

  $\Rightarrow S = -\frac{n}{2} - \frac{in}{2}\cot\frac{\pi}{n}$.

  Comparing real and imaginary parts, $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}k\cos\frac{2k\pi}{n} = -\frac{n}{2}$ and
  $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}k\sin\frac{2k\pi}{n} = -\frac{n}{2}\cot\frac{\pi}{n}$.
  %491
\item Let $\sin n\phi\neq 0$; then every factor $\sin\left(\phi + \frac{k\pi}{n}\right)$ is non-zero.

  Differentiating the given identity, $n\cos n\phi = 2^{n - 1}\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}\cos\left(\phi
  + \frac{k\pi}{n}\right)\prod_{j\neq k}\sin\left(\phi + \frac{j\pi}{n}\right)$.

  Dividing by $\sin n\phi = 2^{n - 1}\displaystyle\prod_j\sin\left(\phi + \frac{j\pi}{n}\right)$,
  $n\cot n\phi = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}\cot\left(\phi + \frac{k\pi}{n}\right)$.

  Differentiating again, $-n^2{\rm cosec}^2n\phi = -\displaystyle\sum_k{\rm cosec}^2\left(\phi + \frac{k\pi}{n}\right)$,
  which is the second identity.

  Replace $\phi$ by $\phi + \frac{\pi}{2}$ in the first identity and use $\cot\left(\theta + \frac{\pi}{2}\right)
  = -\tan\theta$:

  $-\displaystyle\sum_k\tan\left(\phi + \frac{k\pi}{n}\right) = n\cot\left(n\phi + \frac{n\pi}{2}\right)$.

  If $n$ is odd, $\frac{n\pi}{2} = \frac{\pi}{2} + \frac{n - 1}{2}\pi$, and since $\cot$ has period $\pi$,
  $\cot\left(n\phi + \frac{n\pi}{2}\right) = -\tan n\phi$; so $\displaystyle\sum_k\tan\left(\phi +
    \frac{k\pi}{n}\right) = n\tan n\phi$.

  If $n$ is even, $\frac{n\pi}{2}$ is a multiple of $\pi$, so $\cot\left(n\phi + \frac{n\pi}{2}\right) = \cot
  n\phi$ and the sum is $-n\cot n\phi$.

  With $n = 3, \phi = 20^\circ$: $\tan20^\circ + \tan80^\circ + \tan140^\circ = 3\tan60^\circ = 3\sqrt{3}$, and
  $\tan140^\circ = -\tan40^\circ$, so $\tan20^\circ - \tan40^\circ + \tan80^\circ = 3\sqrt{3}$.
  %492
\item For a complex $z$ with $|z| < 1$, $\frac{1 + z}{1 - z} = (1 + z)\displaystyle\sum_{m = 0}^\infty z^m =
  1 + 2\sum_{m = 1}^\infty z^m$.

  Also $\frac{1 + z}{1 - z} = \frac{(1 + z)(1 - \bar{z})}{|1 - z|^2} = \frac{1 - |z|^2 + (z - \bar{z})}{|1 -
    z|^2}$, whose real part is $\frac{1 - |z|^2}{|1 - z|^2}$.

  Put $z = a(\cos\phi + i\sin\phi)$; then $|1 - z|^2 = 1 - 2a\cos\phi + a^2$, and taking real parts,

  $\frac{1 - a^2}{1 - 2a\cos\phi + a^2} = 1 + 2\displaystyle\sum_{m = 1}^\infty a^m\cos m\phi$.

  Let $\phi_k = x + \frac{2k\pi}{n}$. Then $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}\cos m\phi_k$ is the real part of
  $(\cos mx + i\sin mx)\displaystyle\sum_k\left(\cos\frac{2m\pi}{n} + i\sin\frac{2m\pi}{n}\right)^k$, which is $n\cos
  mx$ if $n\mid m$ and $0$ otherwise.

  Adding the $n$ convergent series term by term, only $m = jn$ survive:

  $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}\frac{1 - a^2}{1 - 2a\cos\phi_k + a^2} = n\left[1 + 2\sum_{j = 1}^\infty(a^n)^j\cos
  j(nx)\right]$.

  By the same expansion with $a^n$ (also of modulus $< 1$) and $nx$ in place of $a$ and $\phi$, the bracket is
  $\frac{1 - a^{2n}}{1 - 2a^n\cos nx + a^{2n}}$, which proves the identity.

  With $a = \frac{1}{2}$: $\frac{1 - \frac{1}{4}}{\frac{5}{4} - \cos\phi} = \frac{3}{5 - 4\cos\phi}$, so

  $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}\frac{3}{5 - 4\cos\phi_k} = \frac{n(1 - 4^{-n})}{1 - 2^{1 - n}\cos nx +
    4^{-n}}\Rightarrow\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}\frac{1}{5 - 4\cos\left(x + \frac{2k\pi}{n}\right)} = \frac{n(4^n -
    1)}{3\left(4^n - 2^{n + 1}\cos nx + 1\right)}$.
  %493
\item Let $\phi_k = x + \frac{2k\pi}{n}$. Since $\cos\phi = \frac{e^{i\phi} + e^{-i\phi}}{2}$,

  $\cos^{2m}\phi = \frac{1}{4^m}\displaystyle\sum_{j = 0}^{2m}{}^{2m}C_j\,e^{i(2m - 2j)\phi}$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}\cos^{2m}\phi_k = \frac{1}{4^m}\sum_{j = 0}^{2m}{}^{2m}C_je^{i(2m -
    2j)x}\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}e^{2\pi i(2m - 2j)k/n}$.

  The inner sum is $n$ if $n\mid(2m - 2j)$ and $0$ otherwise. Since $|2m - 2j|\leq 2m < n$, this happens only
  for $2m - 2j = 0$, i.e.\ $j = m$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^{n - 1}\cos^{2m}\phi_k = \frac{n}{4^m}{}^{2m}C_m$, independent of $x$.

  The condition cannot be dropped: for $m = 1, n = 2$, $\cos^2x + \cos^2(x + \pi) = 2\cos^2x$, which is not
  constant (the formula would give $1$).

  For $n = 7, m = 3$ ($7 > 6$): $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^6\cos^6\left(x + \frac{2k\pi}{7}\right) =
  \frac{7}{64}\cdot{}^6C_3 = \frac{7\cdot 20}{64} = \frac{35}{16}$.
  %494
\item If $y = \frac{1}{1 - w}$, then $w = 1 - \frac{1}{y} = \frac{y - 1}{y}$. So $w^n = 1, w\neq 1$ iff
  $(y - 1)^n = y^n$.

  $R(y) = y^n - (y - 1)^n = ny^{n - 1} - {}^nC_2y^{n - 2} + {}^nC_3y^{n - 3} - \cdots$ has degree $n - 1$. If
  $R(y) = 0$ then $y\neq 0$ and $w = \frac{y - 1}{y}$ is an $n$th root of unity other than $1$, i.e.\ $w =
  \zeta_k$ for some $k$. Conversely each $y_k$ is a root. The $n - 1$ numbers $y_k$ are distinct, so they are
  exactly the roots of $R$.

  With $\alpha = \frac{2k\pi}{n}$, $1 - \zeta_k = -2i\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}\left(\cos\frac{\alpha}{2} +
  i\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)$, so $y_k = \frac{i\left(\cos\frac{\alpha}{2} -
    i\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}\right)}{2\sin\frac{\alpha}{2}} = \frac{1}{2} + \frac{i}{2}\cot\frac{k\pi}{n}$.

  Thus every $y_k$ lies on the line ${\rm Re}\,y = \frac{1}{2}$.

  Vieta for $R$: $\displaystyle\sum y_k = \frac{{}^nC_2}{n} = \frac{n - 1}{2}$ and $\displaystyle\sum_{i <
    j}y_iy_j = \frac{{}^nC_3}{n} = \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)}{6}$.

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}y_k^2 = \frac{(n - 1)^2}{4} - \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)}{3}
  = \frac{(n - 1)[3(n - 1) - 4(n - 2)]}{12} = -\frac{(n - 1)(n - 5)}{12}$.

  Second proof of the cotangent sum. $y_k^2 = \frac{1}{4} - \frac{1}{4}\cot^2\frac{k\pi}{n} +
  \frac{i}{2}\cot\frac{k\pi}{n}$. Taking real parts,

  $\frac{n - 1}{4} - \frac{1}{4}\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}\cot^2\frac{k\pi}{n} = -\frac{(n - 1)(n -
    5)}{12}$

  $\Rightarrow\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}\cot^2\frac{k\pi}{n} = (n - 1) + \frac{(n - 1)(n - 5)}{3}
  = \frac{(n - 1)(n - 2)}{3}$.
  %495
\item $e\cdot n! = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 0}^n\frac{n!}{k!} + \sum_{k = n + 1}^\infty\frac{n!}{k!} = N_n + r_n$,
  where $N_n$ is an integer since $k!\mid n!$ for $k\leq n$.

  $r_n = \frac{1}{n + 1} + \frac{1}{(n + 1)(n + 2)} + \frac{1}{(n + 1)(n + 2)(n + 3)} + \cdots$.

  Lower bound: $r_n > \frac{1}{n + 1}$.

  Upper bound: $r_n < \frac{1}{n + 1} + \frac{1}{(n + 1)^2} + \frac{1}{(n + 1)^3} + \cdots = \frac{1/(n +
    1)}{1 - 1/(n + 1)} = \frac{1}{n}$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{n}{n + 1} < nr_n < 1$, so $nr_n\to 1$ and $r_n\to 0$.

  $\sin(2\pi e\cdot n!) = \sin(2\pi N_n + 2\pi r_n) = \sin(2\pi r_n)$.

  $\Rightarrow n\sin(2\pi e\cdot n!) = 2\pi\cdot nr_n\cdot\frac{\sin(2\pi r_n)}{2\pi r_n}\to 2\pi\cdot 1\cdot 1 =
  2\pi$.
  %496
\item Let $d = \frac{\pi}{n}$. From $2\sin\frac{d}{2}\sin kd = \cos\left(k - \frac{1}{2}\right)d - \cos\left(k
  + \frac{1}{2}\right)d$, summing over $k = 1, \ldots, n$:

  $2\sin\frac{\pi}{2n}\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\sin\frac{k\pi}{n} = \cos\frac{\pi}{2n} - \cos\left(\pi +
  \frac{\pi}{2n}\right) = 2\cos\frac{\pi}{2n}$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\sin\frac{k\pi}{n} = \cot\frac{\pi}{2n}$.

  Put $t = \frac{\pi}{2n}$. Then $\frac{1}{n}\cot\frac{\pi}{2n} = \frac{2}{\pi}t\cot t = \frac{2}{\pi}\cos
  t\cdot\frac{t}{\sin t}\to\frac{2}{\pi}$ as $n\to\infty$.

  For the refinement, $n^2\left(\frac{2}{\pi} - \frac{2}{\pi}t\cot t\right) = \frac{2}{\pi}\cdot\frac{\pi^2}{4t^2}(1
  - t\cot t) = \frac{\pi}{2}\cdot\frac{1 - t\cot t}{t^2}$, so we need $\displaystyle\lim_{t\to 0^+}\frac{1 -
    t\cot t}{t^2}$. Take $0 < t < 1$ and use problem 465.

  Lower estimate: $(1 - \frac{t^2}{3})\sin t > \left(1 - \frac{t^2}{3}\right)\left(t - \frac{t^3}{6}\right) = t
  - \frac{t^3}{2} + \frac{t^5}{18} > t - \frac{t^3}{2} + \frac{t^5}{24} > t\cos t$.

  $\Rightarrow t\cot t < 1 - \frac{t^2}{3}\Rightarrow\frac{1 - t\cot t}{t^2} > \frac{1}{3}$.

  Upper estimate: $\cos t > 1 - \frac{t^2}{2} > 0$ and $0 < \sin t < tD$ with $D = 1 - \frac{t^2}{6} +
  \frac{t^4}{120} > 0$.

  $\Rightarrow t\cot t > \frac{1 - \frac{t^2}{2}}{D}\Rightarrow 1 - t\cot t < \frac{D - 1 +
    \frac{t^2}{2}}{D} = \frac{\frac{t^2}{3} + \frac{t^4}{120}}{D}$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{1}{3} < \frac{1 - t\cot t}{t^2} < \frac{\frac{1}{3} + \frac{t^2}{120}}{D}\to\frac{1}{3}$ as
  $t\to 0^+$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}n^2\left(\frac{2}{\pi} - \frac{1}{n}\sum_{k =
    1}^n\sin\frac{k\pi}{n}\right) = \frac{\pi}{2}\cdot\frac{1}{3} = \frac{\pi}{6}$.
  %497
\item Let $x_k = \frac{\sqrt{k}}{n}\in(0, 1]$ for $1\leq k\leq n$, and $L_n = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^n\ln\cos
  x_k$.

  Upper bound: $g(x) = \ln\cos x + \frac{x^2}{2}$ has $g(0) = 0$ and $g'(x) = x - \tan x < 0$ on $\left(0,
    \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$, so $\ln\cos x < -\frac{x^2}{2}$ there.

  Lower bound: for $0\leq u\leq\frac{1}{2}$, $\ell(u) = \ln(1 - u) + u + u^2$ has $\ell(0) = 0$ and
  $\ell'(u) = -\frac{1}{1 - u} + 1 + 2u = \frac{u(1 - 2u)}{1 - u}\geq 0$, so $\ln(1 - u)\geq -u - u^2$.

  For $0 < x\leq 1$, $\cos x > 1 - \frac{x^2}{2}$ (problem 465) with $u = \frac{x^2}{2}\leq\frac{1}{2}$, so
  $\ln\cos x > \ln\left(1 - \frac{x^2}{2}\right)\geq -\frac{x^2}{2} - \frac{x^4}{4}$.

  Now $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^nx_k^2 = \frac{1}{n^2}\sum_{k = 1}^nk = \frac{n + 1}{2n}$ and
  $\displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^nx_k^4 = \frac{1}{n^4}\sum_{k = 1}^nk^2 = \frac{(n + 1)(2n + 1)}{6n^3}$.

  $\Rightarrow -\frac{n + 1}{4n} - \frac{(n + 1)(2n + 1)}{24n^3} < L_n < -\frac{n + 1}{4n}$.

  Both bounds tend to $-\frac{1}{4}$, so $L_n\to -\frac{1}{4}$ and, $e^x$ being continuous,
  $\displaystyle\prod_{k = 1}^n\cos\frac{\sqrt{k}}{n} = e^{L_n}\to e^{-1/4}$.
  %498
\item (i) $\frac{1}{\sin^2x} - \frac{1}{x^2} = \frac{(x - \sin x)(x + \sin x)}{x^2\sin^2x} = \frac{x - \sin
    x}{x^3}\cdot\frac{x + \sin x}{x}\cdot\frac{x^2}{\sin^2x}$.

  By problem 465 the first factor $\to\frac{1}{6}$, the second $\to 1 + 1 = 2$ and the third $\to 1$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 0}\left(\frac{1}{\sin^2x} - \frac{1}{x^2}\right) = \frac{1}{6}\cdot 2 =
  \frac{1}{3}$.

  (ii) $\frac{1}{x}\left(\frac{1}{x} - \cot x\right) = \frac{\sin x - x\cos x}{x^2\sin x} = \frac{\sin x - x\cos
    x}{x^3}\cdot\frac{x}{\sin x}$.

  $\frac{\sin x - x\cos x}{x^3} = \frac{\sin x - x}{x^3} + \frac{1 - \cos x}{x^2}\to -\frac{1}{6} + \frac{1}{2}
  = \frac{1}{3}$ (problem 465), and $\frac{x}{\sin x}\to 1$. So the limit is $\frac{1}{3}$.

  (iii) The expression is even in $x$, so let $0 < x < 1$ and $u = 1 - \frac{\sin x}{x} = \frac{x - \sin
    x}{x}$. By problem 465, $\frac{x^2}{6} - \frac{x^4}{120} < u < \frac{x^2}{6} < \frac{1}{2}$.

  For $0\leq u\leq\frac{1}{2}$, $-u - u^2\leq\ln(1 - u)\leq -u$ (the left inequality was proved in problem 497;
  the right one because $\ln(1 - u) + u$ vanishes at $0$ and has derivative $-\frac{u}{1 - u}\leq 0$).

  $\Rightarrow -\frac{u}{x^2} - \frac{u^2}{x^2}\leq\frac{1}{x^2}\ln\frac{\sin x}{x}\leq -\frac{u}{x^2}$.

  Here $\frac{u}{x^2}\to\frac{1}{6}$ and $0 < \frac{u^2}{x^2} < \frac{x^2}{36}\to 0$, so
  $\frac{1}{x^2}\ln\frac{\sin x}{x}\to -\frac{1}{6}$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\lim_{x\to 0}\left(\frac{\sin x}{x}\right)^{1/x^2} = e^{-1/6}$.
  %499
\item Let $a_1 = \sqrt{2}$ and $a_{j + 1} = \sqrt{2 + a_j}$, so $a_j$ has $j$ square-root signs and $b_n =
  \sqrt{2 - a_{n - 1}}$.

  Claim: $a_j = 2\cos\frac{\pi}{2^{j + 1}}$. True for $j = 1$ since $2\cos\frac{\pi}{4} = \sqrt{2}$.

  If it holds for $j$, then $a_{j + 1} = \sqrt{2 + 2\cos\frac{\pi}{2^{j + 1}}} = \sqrt{4\cos^2\frac{\pi}{2^{j +
        2}}} = 2\cos\frac{\pi}{2^{j + 2}}$, the cosine being positive. This proves the claim.

  $\Rightarrow b_n = \sqrt{2 - 2\cos\frac{\pi}{2^n}} = \sqrt{4\sin^2\frac{\pi}{2^{n + 1}}} = 2\sin\frac{\pi}{2^{n
      + 1}}$.

  $\Rightarrow 2^nb_n = 2^{n + 1}\sin\frac{\pi}{2^{n + 1}} = \pi\cdot\frac{\sin t}{t}$ with $t =
  \frac{\pi}{2^{n + 1}}\to 0$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}2^nb_n = \pi$.
  %500
\item Let $t = \frac{\pi}{n}$, so $n = \frac{\pi}{t}$ and $t\to 0^+$ as $n\to\infty$.

  $n^2(2\pi - p_n) = 2n^2(\pi - n\sin t) = 2n^3(t - \sin t) = 2\pi^3\cdot\frac{t - \sin t}{t^3}$.

  By problem 465 this tends to $2\pi^3\cdot\frac{1}{6} = \frac{\pi^3}{3}$.

  $n^2(P_n - 2\pi) = 2n^3(\tan t - t) = 2\pi^3\cdot\frac{\tan t - t}{t^3}$.

  Now $\tan t - t = \frac{\sin t - t\cos t}{\cos t}$, so $\frac{\tan t - t}{t^3} = \frac{\sin t - t\cos
    t}{t^3}\cdot\frac{1}{\cos t}\to\frac{1}{3}\cdot 1$ by part (ii) of problem 498.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}n^2(P_n - 2\pi) = \frac{2\pi^3}{3}$.
  %501
\item $\pi\sqrt{n^2 + n} - n\pi = \frac{\pi n}{\sqrt{n^2 + n} + n} = \frac{\pi}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{1}{n}} +
    1}\to\frac{\pi}{2}$.

  Since $\sin^2$ has period $\pi$, $\sin^2\left(\pi\sqrt{n^2 + n}\right) = \sin^2\left(\pi\sqrt{n^2 + n} -
  n\pi\right)\to\sin^2\frac{\pi}{2} = 1$.

  Next, let $\epsilon_n = \pi\sqrt{n^2 + 1} - n\pi = \frac{\pi}{\sqrt{n^2 + 1} + n}\in\left(0,
    \frac{\pi}{2}\right)$.

  $\sin\left(\pi\sqrt{n^2 + 1}\right) = \sin(n\pi + \epsilon_n) = (-1)^n\sin\epsilon_n$.

  $\Rightarrow n\left|\sin\left(\pi\sqrt{n^2 + 1}\right)\right| = n\epsilon_n\cdot\frac{\sin\epsilon_n}{\epsilon_n}$,
  and $n\epsilon_n = \frac{\pi}{\sqrt{1 + \frac{1}{n^2}} + 1}\to\frac{\pi}{2}$, $\frac{\sin\epsilon_n}{\epsilon_n}\to
  1$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}n\left|\sin\left(\pi\sqrt{n^2 + 1}\right)\right| = \frac{\pi}{2}$.

  However $n\sin\left(\pi\sqrt{n^2 + 1}\right) = (-1)^nn\sin\epsilon_n$ is close to $\frac{\pi}{2}$ for large
  even $n$ and close to $-\frac{\pi}{2}$ for large odd $n$, so this limit does not exist.
  %502
\item Bounds for ${\rm cosec}$. For $0 < t\leq\frac{\pi}{2}$, $\sin t\leq t$ gives ${\rm cosec}\,t\geq\frac{1}{t}$;
  and using $t - \sin t < \frac{t^3}{6}$ (problem 465) and $\sin t\geq\frac{2t}{\pi}$ (Jordan),

  ${\rm cosec}\,t - \frac{1}{t} = \frac{t - \sin t}{t\sin t}\leq\frac{t^3/6}{t\cdot 2t/\pi} = \frac{\pi
    t}{12}\leq\frac{\pi^2}{24} < 1$.

  Harmonic numbers. Let $H_M = 1 + \frac{1}{2} + \cdots + \frac{1}{M}$. Since $\ln(1 + u) < u$ for $u > 0$,
  $\frac{1}{k} > \ln\frac{k + 1}{k}$; since $-\ln(1 - u) > u$ for $0 < u < 1$, $\frac{1}{k} < \ln\frac{k}{k -
    1}$ for $k\geq 2$. Summing, $\ln(M + 1) < H_M < 1 + \ln M$.

  Let $n\geq 3$ and $M = \left\lceil\frac{n}{2}\right\rceil - 1$, the number of $k$ with $1\leq k <
  \frac{n}{2}$. Since ${\rm cosec}\frac{(n - k)\pi}{n} = {\rm cosec}\frac{k\pi}{n}$,

  $\Sigma_n = \displaystyle\sum_{k = 1}^{n - 1}{\rm cosec}\frac{k\pi}{n} = 2\sum_{k = 1}^M{\rm
    cosec}\frac{k\pi}{n} + \epsilon$, where $\epsilon = 1$ if $n$ is even (the term $k = \frac{n}{2}$) and $0$
  otherwise.

  For $k\leq M$, $\frac{k\pi}{n} < \frac{\pi}{2}$, so $\frac{n}{k\pi}\leq{\rm cosec}\frac{k\pi}{n} < \frac{n}{k\pi} +
  1$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{2n}{\pi}H_M\leq\Sigma_n < \frac{2n}{\pi}H_M + 2M + 1\leq\frac{2n}{\pi}H_M + n + 1$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{2}{\pi}\cdot\frac{H_M}{\ln n}\leq\frac{\Sigma_n}{n\ln n} < \frac{2}{\pi}\cdot\frac{H_M}{\ln n} +
  \frac{n + 1}{n\ln n}$.

  Since $\frac{n}{2} - 1\leq M\leq\frac{n}{2}$, $\ln M = \ln n + \ln\frac{M}{n}$ with $\frac{M}{n}$ between
  $\frac{1}{6}$ and $\frac{1}{2}$ for $n\geq 3$; so $\frac{\ln M}{\ln n}\to 1$ and $\frac{\ln(M + 1)}{\ln
    n}\to 1$, whence $\frac{H_M}{\ln n}\to 1$.

  Also $\frac{n + 1}{n\ln n}\to 0$. $\therefore\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}\frac{1}{n\ln n}\sum_{k = 1}^{n -
    1}{\rm cosec}\frac{k\pi}{n} = \frac{2}{\pi}$.
  %503
\item From problem 474, $\tan^{-1}\frac{2}{k^2} = \tan^{-1}(k + 1) - \tan^{-1}(k - 1)$, so $S_n = \tan^{-1}(n
  + 1) + \tan^{-1}n - \frac{\pi}{4}$.

  $\Rightarrow\frac{3\pi}{4} - S_n = \left[\frac{\pi}{2} - \tan^{-1}(n + 1)\right] + \left[\frac{\pi}{2} -
    \tan^{-1}n\right] = \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{n + 1} + \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{n}$, using $\frac{\pi}{2} - \tan^{-1}y =
  \tan^{-1}\frac{1}{y}$ for $y > 0$.

  $\Rightarrow n\left(\frac{3\pi}{4} - S_n\right) = \frac{n}{n + 1}\cdot\frac{\tan^{-1}\frac{1}{n +
      1}}{\frac{1}{n + 1}} + \frac{\tan^{-1}\frac{1}{n}}{\frac{1}{n}}$.

  Since $\displaystyle\lim_{u\to 0}\frac{\tan^{-1}u}{u} = \lim_{\theta\to 0}\frac{\theta}{\tan\theta} = 1$, each
  term tends to $1$.

  $\therefore\displaystyle\lim_{n\to\infty}n\left(\frac{3\pi}{4} - S_n\right) = 1 + 1 = 2$.
\stopitemize
